L p L^{p} optimal prediction of the last zero of a spectrally negative Lévy process
Erik J. Baurdoux, José M. Pedraza

Abstract
Given a spectrally negative Lévy process X X drifting to infinity, (inspired on the early ideas of Shiryaev (2002)) we are interested in finding a stopping time that minimises the L p L^{p} distance ( p > 1 p>1 ) with g g , the last time X X is negative. The solution is substantially more difficult compared to the case p = 1 p=1 , for which it was shown by Baurdoux and Pedraza (2020) that it is optimal to stop as soon as X X exceeds a constant barrier. In the case of p > 1 p>1 treated here, we prove that solving this optimal prediction problem is equivalent to solving an optimal stopping problem in terms of a two-dimensional strong Markov process that incorporates the length of the current positive excursion away from 0 0 . We show that an optimal stopping time is now given by the first time that X X exceeds a non-increasing and non-negative curve depending on the length of the current positive excursion away from 0 0 . We further characterise the optimal boundary and the value function as the unique solution of a non-linear system of integral equations within a subclass of functions. As examples, the case of a Brownian motion with drift and a Brownian motion with drift perturbed by a Poisson process with exponential jumps are considered.

1
Introduction

In recent years, last passage times have received considerable attention in the literature. For instance, in risk theory, the capital of an insurance company over time is modelled by a stochastic process
X=\{X_{t},t\geq 0\}
. In the classical risk theory,
X
is modelled by the Cramér–Lundberg process, defined as a compound Poisson process with drift. This model assumes that a premium is collected continuously at rate
c>0
. In contrast, the claims arrive according to a Poisson process, with the claims sizes being independent and identically distributed. In more recent literature,
X
is considered to be a more general Lévy process (see e.g.
Huzak et al. 2004
or
Klüppelberg, Kyprianou and
Maller 2004
) with the motivation that the surplus of the company is the superposition of several independent small and large claims, and that some uncertainty should be added in the premium income. A critical quantity of interest is the moment of ruin, which is classically defined as the first passage time below zero.

Gerber 1990
and
Chiu and Yin 2005
propose the following extension by considering that
X
represents the capital of an individual company portfolio. After the moment of ruin, the company may have funds to endure a negative surplus of this portfolio for some time (possibly with the influx of capital from other portfolios) with the hope of having a positive surplus in the long term. Then, the last passage time below level zero is regarded as the last recovery time, so after that, there will no longer be ruin. Note that prior knowledge of this last passage time may have important implications in the risk management of the company (or even a start-up company). Indeed, when launching a new product or portfolio, the insurance company naturally expects losses in the first few months (or even years), aiming that the project is profitable in the long term. Hence, after the last moment of ruin has occurred, fewer funds are needed on their reserves, and they can be destined for other projects or portfolios within the company. In
Chiu and Yin 2005
, the Laplace transform of the last passage time is derived in this framework.

Secondly,
Paroissin and
Rabehasaina 2013
consider spectrally positive Lévy processes as a degradation model. In particular, the ageing of a device is modelled by a process
D=\{D_{t},t\geq 0\}
, given by a subordinator perturbed by a Brownian motion. In this framework, large values of
D
represent a significant deterioration of the device, so the effect of the subordinator means constant degradation, whilst the Brownian motion component represents minor repairs made to the device. In a traditional setting, the failure time of a device is the first time the model hits a specific critical level
b
. However, another approach has been used in the literature. For example,
Barker and Newby 2009
considered the failure time as the last time the process is below
b
. After the last passage time, the process can never go back to this level, meaning that the device is “beyond repair”.

Thirdly,
Egami and Kevkhishvili 2020
studied the last passage time of a general time-homogeneous transient diffusion with applications to credit risk management. They proposed the leverage process (the ratio of a company asset process over its debt) as a geometric Brownian motion over a process that grows at a risk-free rate. It is shown there that the last passage time of the leverage ratio is equivalent to a last passage time of a Brownian motion with drift. In this setting, the last passage represents the situation where the company cannot recover to normal business conditions after this time has occurred.

An important feature of last passage times is that they are random times that are not stopping times. In the recent literature, the problem of finding a stopping time that approximates last passage times has been solved. There are, for example, various papers in which the approximation is in
L_{1}
sense. To mention a few:
du Toit, Peskir and
Shiryaev 2008
predicted the last zero of a Brownian motion with drift in a finite horizon setting;
du Toit and Peskir 2008
predicted the time of the ultimate maximum at time
t=1
for a Brownian motion with drift is attained;
Shiryaev 2009
focused on the last time of the attainment of the ultimate maximum of a Brownian motion and proceeded to show that it is equivalent to predicting the last zero of the process in this setting;
Glover, Hulley and
Peskir 2013
predicted the time in which a transient diffusion attains its ultimate minimum;
Glover and Hulley 2014
predicted the last passage time of a level
z>0
for an arbitrary non-negative time-homogeneous transient diffusion;
Baurdoux and van
Schaik 2014
predicted the time at which a Lévy process attains its ultimate supremum and
Baurdoux, Kyprianou and
Ott 2016
predicted when a positive self-similar Markov process attain its path-wise global supremum or infimum before hitting zero for the first time and
Baurdoux and
Pedraza 2020
predicted the last zero of a spectrally negative Lévy process.

Note that in
Shiryaev 2002
, the author states some general optimal prediction problems that are natural for the “technical analysis” of financial data. In particular, among other problems, it is proposed to predict the time in which a process attains its maximum (over a finite interval) in an
L_{p}
sense. However, no solution to the problem is provided. Moreover, to the best of our knowledge, optimal prediction problems for last passage times have been only solved in an
L_{1}
sense. Hence, inspired by this, we consider the problem of predicting the last zero of a spectrally negative Lévy process (drifting to infinity) in an
L^{p}
sense, i.e. we are interested in solving

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}(|\tau-g|^{p}),

where
g=\sup\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq 0\}
is the last time a spectrally negative Lévy process drifting to infinity is below the level zero and
p>1
. The case when
p=1
was solved in
Baurdoux and
Pedraza 2020
for the spectrally negative case. An optimal stopping time, in this case, is the first time the process crosses above a fixed level
a^{*}\geq 0
, which is characterised in terms of the distribution function of the infimum of the process. The case
p>1
is substantially more complex, as an optimal stopping time now depends on the length of the current excursion above the level zero given by
U_{t}=t-\sup\{0\leq s\leq t:X_{s}\leq 0\}
. The process
(U,X)
is a Markov process taking values in
E=[(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)]\cup[\{0\}\times(-\infty,0)]
.

We show that an optimal stopping time (when
p>1
) is given by
\tau_{D}=\inf\{t>0:(U_{t},X_{t})\in D\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq b(U_{t})\}
, where
b
is a non-negative, non-increasing and continuous curve. That is, it is not optimal to stop when
(U,X)
is in the (continuation) set
C:=E\setminus D
whilst we should stop as soon as the process enters the (stopping) set
D
(see Figure
1
). In other words, given the strong dependence of
U
on
X
, the latter has the following interpretation in terms of the sample paths of
X
: It is optimal to stop when
X
is sufficiently large or has stayed for a sufficiently large amount of time above zero, whereas we will never stop when
X
is in the negative half-line (see Figure
2
).

C
D
U
X

Fig 1:
Stopping and continuation set in the
(U,X)
plane

In the figure below we include a plot of a sample path of
X_{t}
and
b(U_{t})
, where we calculated numerically the function
b
for the Brownian motion with drift case (see Section
6.1
and Figure
3
).

Fig 2:
Black line:
t\mapsto X_{t}
; Blue line:
t\mapsto b(U_{t})
.

The paper is organised as follows. Section
2
gives a short overview of the main results and notation on the fluctuation theory of spectrally negative Lévy processes. In Section
3
, we formulate the optimal prediction problem, and we show that it is equivalent to an optimal stopping problem whose solution is described in Theorem
3.3
. Since the proof of Theorem
3.3
is rather long, we dedicate Section
4
for that purpose. In particular, we show that an optimal stopping time is given by the first time
X
exceeds a boundary
b
, which depends on the length of the current excursion above zero. We derive various properties of
b
. For example, in Lemma
4.15
, we show that
b
is continuous and in Lemma
4.17
, we show that smooth fit holds at the boundary. The main result of this paper is Theorem
4.18
, which proof is devoted to Section
5
, providing a characterisation of
b
and the value function of the optimal stopping problem. In Section
6
, we provide two numerical examples: Firstly, when
X
is a Brownian motion with drift, and secondly, when
X
is a Brownian motion perturbed by a compound Poisson process with exponential jumps. Finally, some of the more technical proofs are deferred to the Appendices
A
and
B
.

2
Preliminaries

A Lévy process
X=\{X_{t},t\geq 0\}
is an almost surely càdlàg process that has independent and stationary increments such that
\mathbb{P}(X_{0}=0)=1
. We take it to be defined on a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
where
\mathbb{F}=\{\mathcal{F}_{t},t\geq 0\}
is the filtration generated by
X
which is naturally enlarged (see Definition 1.3.38 of
Bichteler 2002
). From the stationary and independent increments property, the law of
X
is characterised by the distribution of
X_{1}
. We hence define the characteristic exponent of
X
,
\Psi(\theta):=-\log(\mathbb{E}(e^{i\theta X_{1}}))
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. The Lévy–Khintchine formula guarantees the existence of constants,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma\geq 0
and a measure
\Pi
concentrated in
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
with the property that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1\wedge x^{2})\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
(called the Lévy measure) such that for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Psi(\theta)=i\mu\theta+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\theta^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}(e^{i\theta y}-1-i\theta y\mathbb{I}_{\{|y|<1\}})\Pi(\textup{d}y).

We now state some properties and facts about Lévy processes. The reader can refer, for example, to
Bertoin 1998
,
Sato 1999
and
Kyprianou 2014
for more details. Every Lévy process
X
is also a strong Markov
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process. For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, denote
\mathbb{P}_{x}
as the law of
X
when started at the point
x\in\mathbb{R}
, that is,
\mathbb{E}_{x}(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}(\cdot|X_{0}=x)
. Due to the spatial homogeneity of Lévy processes, the law of
X
under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
is the same as that of
X+x
under
\mathbb{P}
.

From the Lévy–Itô decomposition we can write for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle X_{t}=\sigma B_{t}-\mu t+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(-\infty,\infty)\setminus(-1,1)}xN(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(-1,1)}x(N(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}x)-\textup{d}s\Pi(\textup{d}x)),

where
B
is a standard Brownian motion and
N
is an independent Poisson random measure on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
with intensity
\textup{d}t\times\Pi(\textup{d}x)
. Note that the Poisson random measure
N
describes the jumps of the process
X
.

In the following sections, we often use the so-called compensation formula for Poisson random measures (see e.g. Theorem 4.4 in
Kyprianou 2014
). Let
\xi:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\times\Omega\mapsto[0,\infty)
a measurable function such that: for each
t\geq 0
, the random variable
\xi(t,x)
is
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable, where
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R})
is the Borel sigma algebra on
\mathbb{R}
; for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the stochastic process
\{\xi(t,x),t\geq 0\}
is almost surely left continuous. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(s,x)N(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}x)\right)=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(s,x)\Pi(\textup{d}x)\textup{d}s\right).

(1)

The process
X
is a spectrally negative Lévy process if it has no positive jumps (
\Pi(0,\infty)=0
) with no monotone paths. We now state some important properties and fluctuation identities of spectrally negative Lévy processes, which will be useful to us later in this paper. We refer to
Bertoin 1998
, Chapter VII or Chapter 8 in
Kyprianou 2014
for details.

Due to the absence of positive jumps, we can define the Laplace transform of
X_{1}
. We denote
\psi(\beta)
as the Laplace exponent of the process, that is,
\psi(\beta)=\log(\mathbb{E}(e^{\beta X_{1}}))
for
\beta\geq 0
. For such
\beta
we have that

\displaystyle\psi(\beta)=-\mu\beta+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\beta^{2}+\int_{(-\infty,0)}(e^{\beta y}-1-\beta y\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}})\Pi(\textup{d}y).

The function
\psi
is infinitely often differentiable and strictly convex function on
(0,\infty)
with
\psi(\infty)=\infty
. In particular,
\psi^{\prime}(0+)=\mathbb{E}(X_{1})\in[-\infty,\infty)
determines the behaviour of
X
at infinity. When
\psi^{\prime}(0+)>0
the process
X
drifts to infinity, i.e.,
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}X_{t}=\infty
; when
\psi^{\prime}(0+)<0
,
X
drifts to minus infinity and the condition
\psi^{\prime}(0+)=0
implies that
X
oscillates, that is,
\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}X_{t}=-\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}X_{t}=\infty
. We denote by
\Phi
the right-inverse of
\psi
, i.e.

\displaystyle\Phi(q)=\sup\{\beta\geq 0:\psi(\beta)=q\},\qquad q\geq 0.

In the particular case that
X
drifts to infinity, we have that
\psi^{\prime}(0+)>0
which implies that
\psi
is strictly increasing and then
\Phi
is the usual inverse with
\Phi(0)=0
.

It turns out that the path variation of Lévy processes is characterised by its Lévy triplet. Indeed, for each
t>0
, the paths of
X
have finite variation on
(0,t]
, if and only if
\sigma=0
and
\int_{(-1,0)}|x|\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
. Since the path variation of
X
does not depend on
t>0
, we just simply say that
X
is of (in)finite variation.

Denote by
\tau_{a}^{+}
the first passage time above the level
a>0
,

\displaystyle\tau_{a}^{+}=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}>a\}.

The Laplace transform of
\tau_{a}^{+}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(e^{-q\tau_{a}^{+}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{a}^{+}<\infty\}})=e^{-\Phi(q)a},\qquad a>0.

(2)

An important family of functions for spectrally negative Lévy processes consists of the scale functions, usually denoted by
W^{(q)}
and
Z^{(q)}
. For all
q\geq 0
, the scale function
W^{(q)}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
is such that
W^{(q)}(x)=0
for all
x<0
and it is characterised on the interval
[0,\infty)
as the strictly increasing and continuous function with Laplace transform given by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\beta x}W^{(q)}(x)\textup{d}x=\frac{1}{\psi(\beta)-q},\qquad\text{ for }\beta>\Phi(q).

The function
Z^{(q)}
is defined for all
q\geq 0
by

\displaystyle Z^{(q)}(x):=1+q\int_{0}^{x}W^{(q)}(y)\textup{d}y,\qquad\text{ for }x\in\mathbb{R}.

For the case
q=0
we simply denote
W=W^{(0)}
. When
X
has paths of infinite variation,
W^{(q)}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
and
W^{(q)}(0)=0
for all
q\geq 0
, otherwise
W^{(q)}(0)=1/d
for all
q\geq 0
, where

\displaystyle d:=-\mu-\int_{(-1,0)}x\Pi(\textup{d}x).

Note that since processes with monotone paths are excluded from the definition of spectrally negative processes, we necessarily have that
d>0
when
X
is of bounded variation.

For all
q\geq 0
,
W^{(q)}
has left and right derivatives. Moreover, when
X
is of infinite variation we have that
W^{(q)}\in C^{1}((0,\infty))
with right-derivative at zero given by
W^{(q)^{\prime}}(0)=2/\sigma^{2}
. When
X
is of finite variation
W^{(q)}\in C^{1}((0,\infty))
when
\Pi
has no atoms. Henceforth, we will assume that when
X
is of finite variation, the Lévy measure
\Pi
has no atoms. Furthermore, for each
x\geq 0
and
q\geq 0
,
W^{(q)}
has the following representation

\displaystyle W^{(q)}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}q^{k}W^{*(k+1)}(x),

(3)

where
W^{*(k+1)}
is the
(k+1)
-th convolution of
W
with itself. Various fluctuation identities for spectrally negative Lévy processes have been found in terms of the scale functions. Here we list some that will be useful in later sections. Denote by
\tau_{x}^{-}
the first passage time below the level
x\leq 0
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\tau_{x}^{-}=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}<x\}.

For any
q\geq 0
and
x\leq a
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(e^{-q\tau_{a}^{+}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}>\tau_{a}^{+}\}}\right)=\frac{W^{(q)}(x)}{W^{(q)}(a)}.

(4)

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
q\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}(e^{-q\tau_{0}^{-}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})=Z^{(q)}(x)-\frac{q}{\Phi(q)}W^{(q)}(x),

(5)

where we understand
q/\Phi(q)
in the limiting sense when
q=0
. Since
X
has only negative jumps, we have that it only creeps upwards, that is,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{\tau_{x}^{+}}=x,\tau_{x}^{+}<\infty)=1

(6)

for any
x>0
. Moreover,
X
creeps downwards if and only if
\sigma>0
with probability given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}=0,\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left(W^{\prime}(x)-\Phi(0)W(x)\right)

(7)

for any
x>0
.

Denote by
\underline{X}_{t}=\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}X_{s}
and
\overline{X}_{t}=\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}X_{s}
the running infimum and running maximum of the process
X
up to time
t>0
, respectively. For
q\geq 0
, let
\mathbf{e}_{q}
be an exponential random variable with mean
1/q
independent of
X
, where we understand that
\mathbf{e}_{q}=\infty
almost surely when
q=0
. Then
\overline{X}_{\mathbf{e}_{q}}
is exponentially distributed with parameter
\Phi(q)
and the Laplace transform of
\underline{X}_{\mathbf{e}_{q}}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(e^{\beta\underline{X}_{\mathbf{e}_{q}}})=\frac{q}{\Phi(q)}\frac{\Phi(q)-\beta}{q-\psi(\beta)},\qquad\beta\geq 0.

(8)

Denote by
\sigma_{x}^{-}
the first time the process
X
is below or equal to the level
x
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}^{-}=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}\leq x\}.

(9)

It is easy to show that the mapping
x\mapsto\sigma_{x}^{-}
is non-increasing, right-continuous with left limits. The left limit is given by
\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\sigma_{x-h}^{-}=\tau_{x}^{-}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(e^{-q\sigma_{x}^{-}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{x}^{-}<\infty\}})=\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{e}_{q}>\sigma_{x}^{-})=\mathbb{P}(\underline{X}_{\mathbf{e}_{q}}\leq-x)

for all
x\leq 0
, and the fact that the random variable
\underline{X}_{\mathbf{e}_{q}}
is continuous on
(-\infty,0)
, we have that the stopping times
\sigma_{x}^{-}
and
\tau_{x}^{-}
have the same distribution, for any
x>0
. When
X
is of infinite variation,
X
enters instantly to the set
(-\infty,0)
whilst in the finite variation case, there is a positive time before the process enters it. That implies that in the infinite variation case,
\tau_{0}^{-}=\sigma_{0}^{-}=0
almost surely. Note that in the finite variation case, since the time
t=0
is excluded from the definition of
\sigma_{0}^{-}
, from the fact that
0
is irregular for
(-\infty,0]
(see discussion in
Kyprianou 2014
on p. 157) and due to equation (
7
) we have that
\sigma_{0}^{-}=\tau_{0}^{-}>0
a.s.

Let
q>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
.
The
q
-potential measure of
X
killed on exiting
[0,a]
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-qt}\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{t}\in\textup{d}y,t<\tau_{a}^{+}\wedge\tau_{0}^{-})\textup{d}t

is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure and it has a density given by

\displaystyle\frac{W^{(q)}(x)W^{(q)}(a-y)}{W^{(q)}(a)}-W^{(q)}(x-y),\qquad x,y\in[0,a].

(10)

Similarly, the
q
-potential measure of
X
killed on exiting
(-\infty,a]
and the
q
-potential measure of
X
are absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure with a density given by

\displaystyle e^{-\Phi(q)(a-x)}W^{(q)}(a-y)-W^{(q)}(x-y),\qquad x,y\leq a,

(11)

and

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(q)e^{-\Phi(q)(y-x)}-W^{(q)}(x-y),\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{R},

(12)

respectively.
In the case when
X
drifts to infinity these expression are also valid for
q=0
.

For any
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
g_{t}^{(x)}
the last time that the process is below
x
before time
t
, i.e.,

\displaystyle g_{t}^{(x)}=\sup\{0\leq s\leq t:X_{s}\leq x\},

(13)

with the convention
\sup\emptyset=0
. We simply denote
g_{t}:=g_{t}^{(0)}
for all
t\geq 0
.
Note that when
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\geq 0)=\rho
for some
\rho\in(0,1)
, then
g_{t}/t
follows the generalised arcsine law with parameter
\rho
, see Theorem 13 in
Bertoin 1998
.
The last-hitting time of zero is of key importance in the study of Azéma’s martingale (see
Azéma and Yor 1989
).
We also define, for each
t\geq 0
,
U_{t}^{(x)}
as the time spent by
X
above the level
x
before time
t
since the last visit to the interval
(-\infty,x]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle U_{t}^{(x)}:=t-g_{t}^{(x)},\qquad t\geq 0.

It turns out that for our optimal prediction problem

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}(|\tau-g|^{p}),

for
p>1
, the process
U_{t}=U_{t}^{(0)}
plays a vital role. It can be readily seen that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the process
\{U_{t}^{(x)},t\geq 0\}
is not a Markov process. We now list a number of results from
Baurdoux and
Pedraza 2022
concerning
U=\{U_{t},t\geq 0\}
. The strong Markov property holds for the two dimensional process
(U,X)=\{(U_{t},X_{t}),t\geq 0\}
with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t},t\geq 0\}
and state space given by

\displaystyle E=\{(u,x):u>0\text{ and }x>0\}\cup\{(u,x):u=0\text{ and }x\leq 0\}.

Then, there exists a family of probability measures
\{\mathbb{P}_{u,x},(u,x)\in E\}
such that for any
A\in\mathbb{B}(E)
, Borel set of
E
, we have that
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}((U_{\tau+s},X_{\tau+s})\in A|\mathcal{F}_{\tau})=\mathbb{P}_{U_{\tau},X_{\tau}}((U_{s},X_{s})\in A)
. For each
(u,x)\in E
,
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
can be written in terms of
\mathbb{P}_{x}
via

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(h(U_{s},X_{s})):=\mathbb{E}_{x}(h(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}>s\}})+\mathbb{E}_{x}(h(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}\leq s\}}),

(14)

for any positive measurable function
h
. Note that the stochastic process
(U,X)
is a semimartingale so that Itô formula is known (see e.g. Theorem IV.71 in
Protter 2005
). However, given the strong dependence between
U
and
X
, we can give a more explicit formula in terms of the dynamics of
X
(see Theorem 3.3 in
Baurdoux and
Pedraza 2022
). Let
F:E\mapsto\mathbb{R}
a continuous function that satisfies:

i)

The mapping
x\mapsto F(0,x)
is
C^{1}
on
(-\infty,0)
such that, when
X
is of infinite variation, the second derivative
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}F(0,x)
exists and is continuous on
(-\infty,0)
;

ii)

The mapping
(u,x)\mapsto F(u,x)
is
C^{1,1}
on
(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
such that, when
X
is of infinite variation, the second derivative
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}F(u,x)
exists and is continuous on
(0,\infty)
, for all
u\geq 0
;

iii)

In the case that
\sigma>0
,
F
is such that
\lim_{h\downarrow 0}F(u,h)=F(0,0)
for all
u>0
and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(0,0+)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(0,0-).

(15)

Then we have the following version of Itô formula

\displaystyle F(U_{t},X_{t})

\displaystyle=F(U_{0},X_{0})+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}F(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>0\}}\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(U_{s-},X_{s-})\textup{d}X_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}F(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{[0,t]}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\left(F(U_{s},X_{s^{-}}+y)-F(U_{s-},X_{s^{-}})-y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(U_{s-},X_{s^{-}})\right)N(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}y)

(16)

Moreover, if in addition
F
is a bounded function, the infinitesimal generator
\mathcal{A}_{U,X}
of the process
(U,X)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{U,X}(F)(u,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}F(u,x)\mathbb{I}_{\{x>0\}}-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(u,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}F(u,x)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\left(F(u,x+y)-F(u,x)-y\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(u,x)\right)\mathbb{I}_{\{x+y>0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\left(F(0,x+y)-F(0,x)-y\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(0,x)\right)\mathbb{I}_{\{x\leq 0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\left(F(0,x+y)-F(u,x)-y\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(u,x)\right)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<x<-y\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{F}(u,x)-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\widetilde{F}(u,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\widetilde{F}(u,x)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\left(\widetilde{F}(u,x+y)-\widetilde{F}(u,x)-y\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\widetilde{F}(u,x)\right)\Pi(\textup{d}y),

(17)

where
\tilde{F}
is a function that extends
F
to the set
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}(u,x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}F(u,x),&\text{for }u>0\text{ and }x>0,\\
F(0,x),&\text{for }u\geq 0\text{ and }x\leq 0,\\
F(0,0),&\text{for }u=0\text{ and }x>0.\end{array}\right.

In addition, we provide a formula to calculate an integral involving the process
\{(U_{t},X_{t}),t\geq 0\}
with respect to time in terms of the excursions of
X
above and below zero (see Theorem 3.6 in
Baurdoux and
Pedraza 2022
). Let
K:E\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a left-continuous function in each argument. Assume that there exists a non-negative function
C:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that
u\mapsto C(u,x)
is a monotone function for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
|K(u,x)|\leq C(u,x)
and
\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-qr}C(U_{r},X_{r}+y)\textup{d}r\right)<\infty
for all
(u,x)\in E
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-qr}K(U_{r},X_{r})\textup{d}r\right)=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}}e^{-q\tau_{0}^{-}}K^{-}(X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}-\varepsilon)\right)+K^{+}(0,\varepsilon)}{\psi^{\prime}(\Phi(q))W^{(q)}(\varepsilon)},

(21)

where
K^{+}
and
K^{-}
are given by

\displaystyle K^{+}(u,x)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}e^{-qr}K(u+r,X_{r})\textup{d}r\right)},

\displaystyle K^{-}(x)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}e^{-qr}K(0,X_{r})\textup{d}r\right)},

for all
(u,x)\in E
. As a direct application of the aforementioned formula, we can calculate a density of the
q
-potential measure of
(U,X)
(see Corollary 3.10 in
Baurdoux and
Pedraza 2022
). For any
v,y>0
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-qr}\mathbb{P}(U_{r}\in\textup{d}v,X_{r}\in\textup{d}y)\textup{d}r

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\prime}(q)\frac{y}{v}e^{-qv}\mathbb{P}(X_{v}\in\textup{d}y)\textup{d}v

(22)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\prime}(q)e^{-qv}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{y}^{+}\in\textup{d}v)\textup{d}y,

where the last equality follows from Kendall’s identity (see e.g. Exercise 6.10 in
Kyprianou 2014
).

We conclude this section by collecting some additional results about the last passage time

\displaystyle g=g_{\infty}=\sup\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq 0\}.

(23)

The Laplace transform of
g
was found in
Chiu and Yin 2005
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}(e^{-qg})=e^{\Phi(q)x}\Phi^{\prime}(q)\psi^{\prime}(0+)+\psi^{\prime}(0+)(W(x)-W^{(q)}(x)),\qquad q\geq 0.

(24)

The distribution function of
g
under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
is found by observing that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(g\leq\gamma)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{u+\gamma}>0\text{ for all }u\in(0,\infty))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{u+\gamma}>0\text{ for all }u\in(0,\infty)|\mathcal{F}_{\gamma}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(\mathbb{P}_{X_{\gamma}}(\sigma_{0}^{-}=\infty))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(\mathbb{P}_{X_{\gamma}}(\tau_{0}^{-}=\infty))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(X_{\gamma})),

(25)

where we used the tower property of conditional expectation in the second equality, the Markov property of Lévy processes in the third and that
\sigma_{0}^{-}
and
\tau_{0}^{-}
have the same distribution (see discussion below equation (
9
)). Note that the law of
g
under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
may have an atom at zero given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(g=0)=\mathbb{P}_{x}(\sigma_{0}^{-}=\infty)=\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{0}^{-}=\infty)=\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(x).

For our optimal prediction problem, we require the
p
-th moment of
g
to be finite. The following result is from
Doney and Maller 2004
(see Theorem 1, Theorem 4, Theorem 5 and Remark (ii)).

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
X
be a spectrally negative Lévy process drifting to infinity. Then, for a fixed
p>0
, the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})<\infty
for some (hence every)
x\leq 0
;

2.

\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|x|^{1+p}\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
;

3.

\mathbb{E}((-\underline{X}_{\infty}^{p}))<\infty
;

4.

\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{p+1})<\infty
for some (hence every)
x\leq 0
;

5.

\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{-})^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})<\infty
for some (hence every)
x\geq 0
.

The next lemma states that when
\tau_{0}^{+}
has finite
p
-th moment under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
, then the function
\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{p})
has a polynomial bound in
x
. It will be of use later to deduce a lower bound for the value function of our optimal prediction problem. Its proof can be found in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
p>0
and suppose
\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{p+1})<\infty
for some
x\leq 0
. Then, for each
0\leq r\leq p
, there exist non-negative constants
A_{r}
and
C_{r}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{r})\leq A_{r}+C_{r}|x|^{r}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{r})\leq 2^{r}[\mathbb{E}(g^{r})+A_{r}]+2^{r}C_{r}|x|^{r},\qquad x\leq 0.

Here
\lfloor p\rfloor
denotes the integer part of
p
.

The following lemma shows some properties of the function
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})
. The proof is included in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
p>0
and assume that
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|x|^{p+1}\Pi(dx)<\infty
. Then
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})
is a non-increasing, non-negative and continuous function. Moreover,

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow-\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})=\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})=0.

We state a basic inequality which is used throughout the paper.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
q>0
. Then we have that
(a+b)^{q}\leq 2^{q}(a^{q}+b^{q})
for any
a>0
and
b>0
.

Since the function
x\mapsto x^{q}
is increasing on
(0,\infty)
, we have that

\displaystyle(a+b)^{q}\leq(2\max\{a,b\})^{q}\leq(2\max\{a,b\})^{q}+(2\min\{a,b\})^{q}=2^{q}(a^{q}+b^{q}).

The proof is complete.
∎We conclude this section with a technical result extracted from
Baurdoux and van
Schaik 2014
(see Lemma 5) that will be useful later.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
X
be any Lévy process drifting to
-\infty
. Denote
T_{+}(0)=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq 0\}
. Consider, for
a>0
and
b<0
, the optimal stopping problem

\displaystyle P(x)=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}[a\tau+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau\geq T_{+}(0)\}}b],\qquad\text{for }x\in\mathbb{R}.

Then there is an
x_{0}\in(-\infty,0)
so that
P(x)=0
for all
x\leq x_{0}
.

3
Optimal prediction problem

Denote by
V_{*}
the value of the optimal prediction problem, i.e.,

\displaystyle V_{*}=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}(|\tau-g|^{p}),

(26)

where
\mathcal{T}
is the set of all stopping times with respect to
\mathbb{F}
,
p>1
and
g
is the last zero of
X
given in (
23
). Since
g
is only
\mathcal{F}
measurable standard techniques of optimal stopping times are not directly applicable. However, there is an equivalence between the optimal prediction problem (
26
) and an optimal stopping problem. The next lemma, inspired by the work of
Urusov 2005
, states such equivalence.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
p>1
and let
X
be a spectrally negative Lévy process drifting to infinity such that
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|x|^{p+1}\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
. Consider the optimal stopping problem

\displaystyle V=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}G(s-g_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right),

(27)

where the function
G
is given by

\displaystyle G(u,x)=u^{p-1}\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(x)-\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})

for
u\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then we have that
V_{*}=pV+\mathbb{E}(g^{p})
and a stopping time minimises (
26
) if and only if it minimises (
27
).

Let
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
. Then the following equality holds

\displaystyle|\tau-g|^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}\varrho(s-g)\textup{d}s+g^{p},

(28)

where the function
\varrho
is defined by

\displaystyle\varrho(x)=p\left[\frac{(-x)^{p}}{x}\mathbb{I}_{\{x<0\}}+x^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{x\geq 0\}}\right].

Taking expectations in equation (
28
), using Fubini’s theorem and the tower property for conditional expectations we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(|\tau-g|^{p})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\varrho(s-g)\mathbb{I}_{\{s\leq\tau\}}\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}(g^{p})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{I}_{\{s\leq\tau\}}\mathbb{E}\left(\varrho(s-g)|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right)\textup{d}s\right]+\mathbb{E}(g^{p})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathbb{E}\left(\varrho(s-g)|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right)\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}(g^{p}).

To evaluate the conditional expectation inside the last integral, note that for all
t\geq 0
we can write the time
g
as

\displaystyle g=g_{t}\vee\sup\{s\in(t,\infty):X_{s}\leq 0\},

recalling that
g_{t}=g_{t}^{(0)}
defined in (
13
). Hence, using the Markov property for Lévy processes and the fact that
g_{s}
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}
measurable we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\varrho(s-g)|\mathcal{F}_{s})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\varrho\left(s-\left[g_{s}\vee\sup\{r\in(s,\infty):X_{r}\leq 0\}\right]\right)|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle=\varrho(s-g_{s})\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{r}>0\text{ for all }r\in(s,\infty)\}}|\mathcal{F}_{s})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}(\varrho(s-\sup\{r\in(s,\infty):X_{r}\leq 0\})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{r}\leq 0\text{ for some }r\in(s,\infty)\}}|\mathcal{F}_{s})

\displaystyle=\varrho(s-g_{s})\mathbb{P}_{X_{s}}(g=0)+\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(\varrho(-g)\mathbb{I}_{\{g>0\}})

\displaystyle=p(s-g_{s})^{p-1}\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(X_{s})-p\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1}).

Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(|\tau-g|^{p})=p\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}G(s-g_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}(g^{p}).

∎

Remark 3.2
.

A close inspection of the proof of Lemma
3.1
tells us that the function
\varrho
corresponds to the right derivative of the function
f(x)=|x|^{p}
. Therefore, using similar arguments we can actually extend the result to any convex function
d:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
. That is, under the assumption that
\mathbb{E}(d(0,g))<\infty
, the optimal prediction problem

\displaystyle V_{d}=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}(d(\tau,g))

is equivalent to the optimal stopping problem

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}G_{d}(g_{s},s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right],

where
G_{d}(\gamma,t,x)=\varrho_{d}(s,\gamma)\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(x)+\mathbb{E}_{x}(\varrho_{d}(s,g+s)\mathbb{I}_{\{g>0\}})
and
\varrho_{d}
is the right derivative with respect the first argument of
d
.

The next theorem states the solution to the optimal prediction problem. Note that its proof is rather lengthy so the next section is entirely dedicated to that purpose.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
p>1
and let
X
be a spectrally negative Lévy process drifting to infinity such that
\Pi
has no atoms and that
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|x|^{p+1}\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
. Then there exists a non-decreasing and continuous function
b:(0,\infty)\mapsto[0,\infty)
such that
b(u)\geq h(u):=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:G(u,x)\geq 0\}
for all
u\geq 0
,
\lim_{u\downarrow 0}b(u)=\infty
,
\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}b(u)=0
and the infimum in (
27
) (and hence in (
26
)) is attained by

\displaystyle\tau_{D}=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}\geq b(U_{t})\}.

(29)

Moreover, the function
b
is uniquely characterised as in Theorem
4.18
.

4
Solution to the optimal stopping problem

Throughout this section we are going to assume that
p>1
and that
X
is a spectrally negative Lévy process drifting to infinity such that
\Pi
has no atoms and
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|x|^{p+1}\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
. To solve the optimal stopping problem (
27
) using the general theory of optimal stopping (see e.g.
Peskir and Shiryaev 2006
), we have to extend it to an optimal stopping problem driven by a strong Markov process. For every
(u,x)\in E
, we define the optimal stopping problem

\displaystyle V(u,x)=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right],

(30)

where the function
G
is given by
G(u,x)=u^{p-1}\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(x)-\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
for any
u\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore we have that
V_{*}=pV(0,0)+\mathbb{E}(g^{p})
. Note that using the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{u,x}
we have that (
30
) takes the form

\displaystyle V(u,x)=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\left\{G(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}>s\}}+G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}\leq s\}}\right\}\textup{d}s\right).

(31)

As a consequence of Lemma
2.3
we have the following behaviour of the function
G
. For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the function
u\mapsto G(u,x)
is non-decreasing. In particular, when
x<0
,
u\mapsto G(u,x)=-\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
is a strictly negative constant. For fixed
u\geq 0
,
x\mapsto G(u,x)
is a non-decreasing right-continuous function which is continuous everywhere apart from possibly at
x=0
(since
W
is discontinuous at zero when
X
is of finite variation) such that for all
u\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow-\infty}G(u,x)=-\infty\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}G(u,x)=u^{p-1}\geq 0.

Moreover, we have that
\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}G(u,x)=\infty
and
G(0,x)=-\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})<0
for all
x\geq 0
. Recall that for any
u\geq 0
,

\displaystyle h(u)

\displaystyle=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:G(u,x)\geq 0\}.

(32)

From the description of
G
above we have that
h
is a non-negative and non-increasing function such that
h(u)<\infty
for all
u\in(0,\infty)
,
h(0)=\infty
and
\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}h(u)=0
. Moreover, since
W
is strictly increasing on
(0,\infty)
, the function

\displaystyle T(x):=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(x)}

is continuous and strictly decreasing on
[0,\infty)
. Then, there exists an inverse function
T^{-1}
which is continuous and strictly decreasing on
(0,u_{h}^{*}]
with

\displaystyle u_{h}^{*}:=\frac{\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(0)},

(33)

where we understand
1/0=\infty
when
X
is of infinite variation. Hence, we can write

\displaystyle h(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}T^{-1}(u^{p-1}),&u<(u_{h}^{*})^{\frac{1}{p-1}},\\
0,&u\geq(u_{h}^{*})^{\frac{1}{p-1}}.\end{array}\right.

Therefore, since
T^{-1}(u_{h}^{*}-)=0
, we conclude that
h
is a continuous function on
[0,\infty)
. From the definition of
h
we clearly have that
G(u,x)\geq 0
if and only if
x\geq h(u)
.

The facts above give us some intuition about the optimal stopping rule for the optimal stopping problem (
30
). Since we are dealing with a minimisation problem, before stopping, we want the process
(U,X)
to be in the set in which
G
is negative as much as possible. Then, the fact that
G(U_{t},X_{t})
is strictly negative when
X_{t}<h(U_{t})
suggests that it is never optimal to stop on this region. When
X_{t}>h(U_{t})
, we have that
G(U_{t},X_{t})\geq 0
but with strictly positive probability
(U,X)
can enter the set in which
G
is negative. Moreover,
t\mapsto U_{t}
is strictly increasing when
X
is in the positive half line so that
t\mapsto h(U_{t})
gets closer to zero when the current excursion away from
(-\infty,0]
is sufficiently large. Then,
G(U_{t},X_{t})\geq 0
even when
X_{t}
is relatively close to zero. That suggests that stopping is optimal when the current excursion away from
(-\infty,0]
is large, or
X
takes sufficiently large values. Then we infer the existence of a non-negative curve
b\geq h
such that it is optimal to stop when
X
crosses above
b(U_{t})
. We will formally show in the next Lemmas the existence of such a boundary.

Note that if there exists a stopping time
\tau
for which the expectation of the right-hand side of (
30
) is minus infinity, then
V
would also be minus infinity. The following Lemma provides the finiteness of a lower bound of
V
that will ensure that
V
only takes finite values. Its proof is included in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 4.1
.

We have that

\displaystyle 0\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right)<\infty\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

We now prove the finiteness of the function
V
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For every
(u,x)\in E
we have that
V(u,x)\in(-\infty,0]
. In particular,
V(u,x)<0
for
(u,x)\in B:=\{(u,x)\in E:x<h(u)\}
, where
h
is defined in (
32
).

By taking the stopping time
\tau=0
we deduce that for all
(u,x)\in E
,
V(u,x)\leq 0
. In order to check that
V(u,x)>-\infty
we use that
G(u,x)\geq-\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
to get

\displaystyle V(u,x)=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right]\geq-\sup_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right],

for all
(u,x)\in E
. Hence by Lemma
4.1
we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)\geq-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right]>-\infty

(34)

for all
(u,x)\in E
. Using standard arguments we can prove that
V(u,x)<0
when
(u,x)\in B
. Indeed, from the definition of
h
, we have that if
(u,x)\in B
then
G(u,x)<0
. Take
(u,x)\in B
and consider the stopping time

\displaystyle\tau_{B}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:(U_{t},X_{t})\in E\setminus B\}.

Note that under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
we have
\tau_{B}>0
. Then, for all
s<\tau_{B}
we have that
(U_{s},X_{s})\in B
which implies that
G(U_{s},X_{s})<0
. Hence, by the definition of
V
, we see that

\displaystyle V(u,x)\leq\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau_{B}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right]<0.

∎

Remark 4.3
.

Note that we have that
h(0)=\infty
, which implies that
(0,0)\in B
and then, from the Lemma above,
V(0,0)<0
. Moreover, from Lemma
3.1
we have that
pV(0,0)+\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})=V_{*}\geq 0
which implies that

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})}{p}\leq V(0,0)<0.

Now we prove some basic properties of
V
.

Lemma 4.4
.

We have the following monotonicity property of
V
. For all
(u,x),(v,y)\in E
such that
u\leq v
and
x\leq y
we have that
V(u,x)\leq V(v,y)
.

From equation (
31
) we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\left\{G(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}>s\}}+G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}\leq s\}}\right\}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\left\{G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}>s\}}+G(U_{s}^{(-x)},X_{s}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq s\}}\right\}\textup{d}s\right),

where
\sigma_{-x}^{-}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq-x\}
and
U_{s}^{(-x)}=s-\sup\{0\leq t\leq s:X_{t}\leq-x\}
. Recall that for all
s\geq 0
,
x\mapsto U_{s}^{(-x)}
and
x\mapsto\sigma_{-x}^{-}
are non-decreasing and that the function
G
is non-decreasing in each argument. Define the function

\displaystyle G^{*}(u,x):=G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}>s\}}+G(U_{s}^{(-x)},X_{s}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq s\}}.

We show by cases that the function
G^{*}
is non-decreasing in each argument. Take
x\leq y
and
0\leq u\leq v
. First, we suppose that
\omega\in\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}>s\}\subset\{\sigma_{-y}^{-}>s\}
. Since
G
is non-decreasing in each argument we then have

\displaystyle G^{*}(u,x)(\omega)=G(u+s,X_{s}(\omega)+x)\leq G(v+s,X_{s}(\omega)+y)=G^{*}(v,y)(\omega).

Similarly, if
\omega\in\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq s\}\cap\{\sigma_{-y}^{-}\leq s\}
we have that

\displaystyle G^{*}(u,x)(\omega)=G(U_{s}^{(-x)}(\omega),X_{s}(\omega)+x)\leq G(U_{s}^{(-y)}(\omega),X_{s}(\omega)+y)=G^{*}(v,y)(\omega).

Lastly, take
\omega\in\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq s\}\cap\{\sigma_{-y}^{-}>s\}
. Then using the fact that
U_{s}^{(-x)}=s-g_{s}^{(-x)}\leq s\leq v+s
and the monotonicity of
G
we get

\displaystyle G^{*}(u,x)(\omega)=G(U_{s}^{(-x)}(\omega),X_{s}(\omega)+x)\leq G(v+s,X_{s}(\omega)+y)=G^{*}(v,y)(\omega).

All this together implies that the function
G^{*}(u,x)
is non-decreasing in each argument for all
u\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, in particular for all
(u,x)\in E
. Hence, the claim on
V
holds.
∎In the following Lemma, we give an expression for
V(0,x)
when
x<0
, in terms of
V(0,0)
, and we use it to give a lower bound for
V
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For any
x\leq 0
we have that

\displaystyle V(0,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}G(0,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+V(0,0)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{-x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z+V(0,0).

(35)

Moreover, for all
(u,x)\in E
we have that there exist non-negative constants
A^{\prime}_{p-1}
and
C^{\prime}_{p-1}
such that

\displaystyle V(u,x)\geq-A^{\prime}_{p-1}-C^{\prime}_{p-1}|x|^{p}+V(0,0).

(36)

Let
x<0
, using the Markov property and a dynamic programming argument we can write for all
x<0
,

\displaystyle V(0,x)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\tau_{0}^{+}}G(0,X_{s})\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{+}<\tau\}}\int_{\tau_{0}^{+}}^{\tau}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\tau_{0}^{+}}G(0,X_{s})\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{+}<\tau\}}V(0,0)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}G(0,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+V(0,0),

where the last equality follows since
G(0,x)\leq 0
for all
x\leq 0
and
V(0,0)\leq 0
, and hence the infimum is attained for any
\tau\geq\tau_{0}^{+}
. Using the fact that
G(0,x)=-\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
, for all
x<0
, and Fubini’s theorem we get that

\displaystyle V(0,x)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right)+V(0,0)

\displaystyle=-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{s}\in\textup{d}z,s<\tau_{0}^{+})\textup{d}s+V(0,0).

Using the
0
-potential measure of
X
killed on exiting the interval
(-\infty,0]
(see equation (
11
)) and Fubini’s theorem, we obtain that

\displaystyle V(0,x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})[W(z)-W(x+z)]\textup{d}z+V(0,0)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})\int_{x+z}^{z}W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z+V(0,0)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\int_{u}^{u-x}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}z+V(0,0)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{-x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z+V(0,0).

From equation (
35
) and the fact that
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
is non-increasing and bounded from above by a polynomial (see Lemmas
2.2
and
2.3
) we have the inequalities for
x<0
,

\displaystyle V(0,x)

\displaystyle\geq x\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x-u}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u+V(0,0)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}2^{p-1}[\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})+A_{p-1}]x+\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}2^{p-1}C_{p-1}x\mathbb{E}(|x+\underline{X}_{\infty}|^{p-1})

\displaystyle\qquad+V(0,0)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}2^{p-1}[\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})+A_{p-1}+2^{p-1}C_{p-1}\mathbb{E}((-\underline{X}_{\infty})^{p-1})]x

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}2^{p-1}C_{p-1}|x|^{p}+V(0,0),

where we used that
\mathbb{P}(-\underline{X}_{\infty}\in\textup{d}u)=\psi^{\prime}(0+)W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u
. Hence (
36
) follows for
x<0
. The general statement holds since
V
is non-decreasing in each argument.
∎Define the set
D:=\{(u,x)\in E:V(u,x)=0\}
. From Lemma
4.2
we know that
V(u,x)<0
for all
(u,x)\in E
such that
x<h(u)
. Hence if
(u,x)\in D
we have that
x\geq h(u)\geq 0
. We then define the function
b:(0,\infty)\mapsto\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle b(u)=\inf\{x>0:V(u,x)=0\},

where
\inf\emptyset=\infty
and
\inf(0,\infty)=0
. Then it directly follows that
b(u)\geq h(u)\geq 0
for all
u>0
. Moreover, since
h(0)=\infty
we have that
\lim_{u\downarrow 0}b(u)=\infty
. Furthermore, since
V
is monotone in each argument we deduce that
u\mapsto b(u)
is non-increasing and
V(u,x)=0
for all
x>b(u)
. We then have the following Lemma.

Lemma 4.6
.

The function
b:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is non-increasing with
0\leq h(u)\leq b(u)
. We have that
\lim_{u\downarrow 0}b(u)=\infty
and
b(u)<\infty
for all
u>0
.

We show that for each
u>0
,
b(u)<\infty
. Fix
u>0
and take
x>y>0
. By a dynamic programming argument we obtain that

\displaystyle V(

\displaystyle u,x)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{y}^{-}<\tau\}}V(U_{\sigma_{y}^{-}},X_{\sigma_{y}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{y}^{-}<\tau\}}V(0,0)+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{y}^{-}<\tau,X_{\sigma_{y}^{-}}\leq 0\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{y}^{-}})\right),

where the inequality follows since
V
is non-positive and non-decreasing. By the compensation formula for Poisson random measures (see (
1
)), we have that for any stopping time
\tau
(we assume without loss of generality that
\tau<\infty
a.s.),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{y}^{-}<\tau,X_{\sigma_{y}^{-}}<0\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{y}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s-}+z)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s-}+z\leq 0\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{s\leq\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-}\}}N(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}z)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s}+z)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+z\leq 0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}z)\textup{d}s\right).

Hence, from the equation above, since
G
and
V
are non-decreasing in each argument,
V\leq 0
and
X_{s}>y
for all
s<\sigma_{y}^{-}
we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)\geq\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left((\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-})\left[G(u,y)+\int_{(-\infty,-y)}V(0,z)\Pi(\textup{d}z)\right]+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{y}^{-}<\tau\}}V(0,0)\right).

Note that from equation (
36
) and Lemma
2.1
, the integral with respect to
\Pi(\textup{d}z)
above is finite so we can choose
y
sufficiently large such that
a:=G(u,y)+\int_{(-\infty,-y)}V(0,z)\Pi(\textup{d}z)\geq 0
. Take any stopping time
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
, since
\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-}
is also a stopping time we have that

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}

\displaystyle\left(a\tau+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau\geq\sigma_{y}^{-}\}}V(0,0)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(a(\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-})+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-}\geq\sigma_{y}^{-}\}}V(0,0)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(a(\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-})+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau>\sigma_{y}^{-}\}}V(0,0)\right),

where the last inequality follows since
V(0,0)\leq 0
. Hence, we deduce that for
x>y>0
and
u>0
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(a(\tau\wedge\sigma_{y}^{-})+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{y}^{-}<\tau\}}V(0,0)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(a\tau+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau\geq\sigma_{y}^{-}\}}V(0,0)\right).

Then from Lemma
2.5
we have that (since
V(0,0)\leq 0
and
-X
drifts to
-\infty
) there exists a value
x_{0}(u)<0
such that the right-hand side of the equation above vanishes for all
y-x\leq x_{0}(u)
. Hence, we have that
V(u,x)=0
for all
x\geq y-x_{0}(u)
and then
b(u)<\infty
.

∎Let
(u,x)\in E
. We define, under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
, the stopping times

\displaystyle\tau_{D}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t\geq 0:(U_{t},X_{t})\in D\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq b(U_{t})\},

\displaystyle\tau_{b}^{v,y}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}+y\geq b(v+t)\},\qquad v>0\text{ and }y\in\mathbb{R},

(37)

and for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{x}
, the stopping time

\displaystyle\tau_{b}^{g,y}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}+y\geq b(U_{t}^{(-y)})\},\qquad\text{ }y\in\mathbb{R}.

(38)

Note that for any
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
v>0
, the stopping time
\tau_{b}^{v,y}
does not depend on the process
U
and hence for any measurable function
f
, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(f(\tau_{b}^{v,y}))=\mathbb{E}_{x}(f(\tau_{b}^{v,y}))
. Hence, for any
(u,x)\in E
and any measurable function
f
, it can be seen that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(f(\tau_{D}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(f(\tau_{b}^{u,0})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u,0}\leq\sigma_{0}^{-}\}})+\mathbb{E}_{x}(f(\tau_{b}^{g,0})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u,0}>\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}).

Now we introduce a technical lemma that ensures that the stopping time
\tau_{D}
has moments of order
p
. The proof can be found in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 4.7
.

For all
(u,x)\in E
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}((\tau_{D})^{p})<\infty.

Now we are ready to show (using the general theory of optimal stopping) that
\tau_{D}
is an optimal stopping time for (
30
).

Lemma 4.8
.

An optimal stopping time for (
30
) is given by
\tau_{D}
, the first entrance of
(U,X)
to the closed set
D
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\tau_{D}=\inf\{t\geq 0:(U_{t},X_{t})\in D\}.

Then the function
V
takes the form

\displaystyle V(u,x)=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right),\qquad(u,x)\in E.

Note that it follows from Lemma
4.7
that
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}(\tau_{D}<\infty)=1
for all
(u,x)\in E
. Then, using a dynamic programming argument, we deduce that

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{D}<\tau\}}V(U_{\tau_{D}},X_{\tau_{D}})\right)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right),

where in the last equality we used that
V(u,x)=0
on
D
.

Since
W(x)\leq 1/\psi^{\prime}(0+)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that
|G(u,x)|\leq u^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
. Then, for any
(u,x)\in E
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}

\displaystyle\left[\sup_{t\geq 0}\left|\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\right|\textup{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}[(U_{s})^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}(u+s)^{p-1}\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2^{p-1}[u^{p-1}+\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}[(\tau_{D})^{p}]]+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad<\infty,

where in the second inequality we used that
U_{s}\leq u+s
for all
s\geq 0
, under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
, and on the last equality follows from Lemmas
4.1
and
4.7
.

From equation (
35
) we have that
V(0,x)
is continuous on
(-\infty,0)
and left continuous at
0
(and hence upper semi-continuous on
(-\infty,0)
). Next, we show that
V
is upper semi-continuous on
([0,\infty)\times[0,\infty))\cap E
. Note that since
V
is non-decreasing in each argument, we have that for any
u>0
and
x>0
or
u=0=x
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{E\ni(v,y)\rightarrow(u,x)}V(v,y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}[\sup\{V(v,y):(v,y)\in\mathbf{B}((u,x),\varepsilon)\cap E\}]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}V(u+\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon),

where
\mathbf{B}((u,x),\varepsilon)
is the ball with center
(u,x)
with radius
\varepsilon
. Hence, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}V(u+\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)\leq V(u,x)

for all
(u,x)\in([0,\infty)\times[0,\infty))\cap E
. Take
\delta>0
and define the stopping time

\displaystyle\tau_{\delta}(x):=\inf\{t\geq\delta:X_{t}\geq b(\delta)+x\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t+\delta}\geq b(\delta)+x\}+\delta.

Note that, by conditioning at the filtration at time
\delta
and from Lemmas
2.2
and
2.4
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}(\tau_{\delta}(0)^{p})=\mathbb{E}_{x}(\mathbb{E}_{X_{\delta}}[(\tau_{b(\delta)}^{+}+\delta)^{p}])\leq 2^{p}(A_{p}+\delta)+2^{p}C_{p}\mathbb{E}(|X_{\delta}+x-b(\delta)|^{p})<\infty,

where
A_{p}
and
C_{p}
are non-negative constants and the last inequality follows from Theorem 3.8 in
Kyprianou 2014
(since
x^{p}\vee 1
is sub-multiplicative), and by assumption
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|y|^{p+1}\Pi(\textup{d}y)<\infty
. Using once again a dynamic programming argument we have that for any
u>0
and
x>0
,

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{\delta}(0)}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{\delta}(0)\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{\delta}(0)<\tau\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{\delta}(0)<\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}V(U_{\tau_{\delta}(0)},X_{\tau_{\delta}(0)})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma_{-x}^{-}\wedge\tau_{\delta}(-x)}G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}<\tau\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}<\tau_{\delta}(-x)\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x}^{-}}+x)\bigg),

(39)

where in the last equality we used that, on the event
\{\tau_{\delta}(0)<\sigma_{0}^{-}\}
, we have
(U_{\tau_{\delta}(0)},X_{\tau_{\delta}(0)})=(u+\tau_{\delta}(0),X_{\tau_{\delta}(0)})\in D
due to the fact that
u+\tau_{\delta}(0)\geq u+\delta\geq\delta
and then
X_{\tau_{\delta}(0)}\geq b(\delta)\geq b(u+\tau_{\delta}(0))
. Recall that
G(u,x)\leq u^{p-1}
and note that for any
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma_{-x}^{-}\wedge\tau_{\delta}(-x)}(u+s)^{p-1}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}[\mathbb{E}((\tau_{\delta}(-x)+u)^{p}-u^{p}]<\infty.

Hence, by Fatou’s Lemma, we see that for any
(u,x)\in([0,\infty)\times[0,\infty))\cap E
and
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}V(u+\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}\wedge\tau_{\delta}(-x-\varepsilon)}G(u+\varepsilon+s,X_{s}+x+\varepsilon)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x+\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{\delta}(-x-\varepsilon)\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}}+x+\varepsilon)\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\tau}\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}G(u+\varepsilon+s,X_{s}+x+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{s<\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{s<\tau_{\delta}(-x-\varepsilon)\}}\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}-\tau_{\delta}(-x-\varepsilon)<0\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}}+x+\varepsilon)\bigg).

It is easy to show that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\tau_{\delta}(-x-\varepsilon)\uparrow\tau_{\delta}(-x)
and
\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}\downarrow\tau_{-x}^{-}=\sigma_{-x}^{-}
a.s., whenever
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
(so that
\sigma_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-}-\tau_{\delta}(-x-\varepsilon)\downarrow\sigma_{-x}^{-}-\tau_{\delta}(-x)
a.s.). Hence, by continuity of
y\mapsto V(0,y)
, right-continuity of
X
and of the mappings
u,x\mapsto G(u,x)
and
y\mapsto\mathbb{I}_{\{y<T\}}
, for any
T\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}V(u+\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma_{-x}^{-}\wedge\tau_{\delta}(-x)}G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}<\tau\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}<\tau_{\delta}(-x)\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x}^{-}}+x)\bigg).

Thus, taking infimum over all
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
in the equation above and from (
39
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}V(u+\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)\leq V(u,x)

for any
(u,x)\in([0,\infty)\times[0,\infty))\cap E
. Therefore, we conclude that
V
is upper semi-continuous, and from general results of optimal stopping (see e.g. Corollary 2.9 in
Peskir and Shiryaev 2006
), we deduce that
\tau_{D}
is an optimal stopping time for
V
. The proof is complete.

∎

Using the fact that
\tau_{D}
is optimal, we can then give a representation of
V
in terms of the measure
\mathbb{P}
and the stopping times
\tau_{b}^{u,x}
and
\tau_{b}^{g,x}
, defined in (
37
) and (
38
), respectively. For any
(u,x)\in E
we can write

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\wedge\tau_{b}^{u,x}}G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq\tau_{b}^{u,x}\}}\int_{\sigma_{-x}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,x}}G(U_{s}^{(-x)},X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\wedge\tau_{b}^{u,x}}G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq\tau_{b}^{u,x}\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x}^{-}}+x)\right).

(40)

Note that in the last equality, we no longer have explicitly the process
\{U_{t}^{(-x)},t\geq 0\}
. This alternative representation of
V
in terms of the original measure
\mathbb{P}
will be useful to prove further properties of
b
and
V
.

The next lemma describes the limit behaviour of the function
b
.

Lemma 4.9
.

We have that

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}b(u)=0.

Note that, since the curve
b
is non-increasing and it is bounded from below by
\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}h(u)=0
, the limit
b^{*}:=\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}b(u)
exists and
b^{*}\geq 0
. We prove by contradiction that
b^{*}=0
. Suppose
b^{*}>0
and define the stopping time

\displaystyle\sigma_{*}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\notin(0,b^{*})\}.

Take
u>0
and
x\in(0,b^{*})
. From the fact that
b(u)\geq b^{*}>0
we have that
\sigma_{*}\leq\tau_{D}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}
under
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{*}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(V(U_{\sigma_{*}},X_{\sigma_{*}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{*}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(V(u+\sigma_{*},X_{\sigma_{*}})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{\sigma_{*}}>0\}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(V(0,X_{\sigma_{*}})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{\sigma_{*}}\leq 0\}}\right),

(41)

where in the last equality we used the Markov property of the two-dimensional process
\{(U_{t},X_{t}),t\geq 0\}
. For a fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the function
u\mapsto V(u,x)
is non-decreasing and bounded from above by zero, thus we have that
\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}V(u,x)
exists and
-\infty<\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}V(u,x)\leq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. By the dominated convergence theorem, we also conclude that
-\infty<\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(V(u+\sigma_{*},X_{\sigma_{*}})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{\sigma_{*}}>0\}}\right)\leq 0
. Moreover, using Fatou’s lemma (take
G(u+s,X_{s})-G(0,0)\geq 0
for all
s<\sigma_{*}
and note that
\mathbb{E}(\sigma_{*})<\mathbb{E}(\tau_{D})<\infty
) and the fact that
\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}G(u,x)=\infty
we deduce that

\displaystyle\liminf_{u\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{*}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)\geq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{*}}\liminf_{u\rightarrow\infty}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)=\infty.

Hence, taking
u\rightarrow\infty
in (
4
) we get that

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}V(u,x)=\infty.

Which yields the desired contradiction. Therefore, we conclude that
b^{*}=0
.
∎In the following, we proceed to analyse the continuity properties of
b
and
V
. Note that, by using standard arguments (from the fact that
D
is a closed set), we can show that the function
b
is right continuous. It turns out that
b
is continuous, the proof of this fact makes use of a variational inequality and will be proved later.

Now we are ready to show the continuity of the value function
V
. The proof is rather long and technical so is included in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 4.10
.

The function
V
is continuous on
E
. Moreover, in the case that
X
is of infinite variation we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{h\downarrow 0}V(u,h)=V(0,0)

for all
u>0
.

We know that
D
is a closed set, so
b
is a right-continuous function. To show left continuity, we use a variational inequality that is satisfied by the value function
V
. We will dedicate the upcoming paragraphs to introducing that.

It is well known that for every optimal stopping problem, there is an associated free boundary problem, which is stated in terms of the infinitesimal generator (see e.g.
Peskir and Shiryaev 2006
, Chapter III). In this case, provided that the value function is smooth enough, we have that
V
solves the Dirichlet/Poisson problem. That is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{U,X}(V)=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})=-G\qquad\text{in }E\setminus D,

where
\mathcal{A}_{U,X}
corresponds to the infinitesimal generator of the process
(U,X)
given in (
17
) and
\mathcal{A}_{X}
is the infinitesimal generator of
X
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})

\displaystyle=-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\widetilde{V}(u,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\widetilde{F}(u,x)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\left(\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)-\widetilde{V}(u,x)-y\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\widetilde{V}(u,x)\right)\Pi(\textup{d}y),

whilst
\widetilde{V}
is the extension of
V
to the set
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}(u,x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}V(u,x),&\text{for }u>0\text{ and }x>0,\\
V(0,x),&\text{for }u\geq 0\text{ and }x\leq 0,\\
V(0,0),&\text{for }u=0\text{ and }x>0.\end{array}\right.

However, in our setting, it turns out to be challenging to show the smoothness of the function
V
. Indeed, from
Garroni and Menaldi 2002
it could be checked that when the diffusion component is non-degenerate (that is
\sigma>0
), the function
V
is
C^{1,2}
on the set
C^{+}=\{(u,x)\in E:0<x<b(u)\}
(cf.
Bayraktar and
Xing 2012
). However, for the case
\sigma=0
differentiability of
V
might fail, even in the finite variation case (see e.g.
Cont and Voltchkova 2005
). It turns out that
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
showed that we could state an analogous (in)equality in the sense of distributions.

In Appendix
B
we recall some facts and notation from the theory of distributions (see also
Friedlander
et al. 1998
or
Rudin 1991
for further details). Since
V
is continuous on
E
we have that
\widetilde{V}
is a locally integrable function in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
(note that
\widetilde{V}
may be discontinuous at points of the form
(u,0)
for
u>0
when
X
is of finite variation) so we can define
\widetilde{V}
as a distribution in any open set
O\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
via the functional

\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto\langle\widetilde{V},\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{V}(u,x)\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u,

where
\varphi
is taken from the set of test functions with compact support in
O
. The derivatives of the distribution
\widetilde{V}
are defined as

\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto\langle\frac{\partial^{i+j}}{\partial u^{i}\partial x^{j}}\widetilde{V},\varphi\rangle=(-1)^{i+j}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{V}(u,x)\frac{\partial^{i+j}}{\partial u^{i}\partial x^{j}}\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u.

Moreover, provided that the function
(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)
is locally integrable in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, the functional
B_{X}(\widetilde{V})
defined by

\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto\langle B_{X}(\widetilde{V}),\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{V}(u,x)B_{X}^{*}(\varphi)(u,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u,

defines a distribution on
O
(see Lemma
B.4
), where

\displaystyle B_{X}^{*}(\varphi)(u,x)=\int_{(-\infty,0)}[\varphi(u,x-y)-\varphi(u,x)+y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}]\Pi(\textup{d}y).

We have the following Lemma that ensures that the integrability conditions for
\widetilde{V}
are satisfied so then
B_{X}(\widetilde{V})
is indeed a distribution.

Lemma 4.11
.

The function

\displaystyle(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)

is locally integrable in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.

First note that from equation (
36
) we have that for any
x\leq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,-1)}V(0,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\geq-A^{\prime}_{p-1}\Pi(-\infty,-1]-C^{\prime}_{p-1}\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|x+y|^{p}\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad+V(0,0)\Pi(-\infty,-1)

\displaystyle>-\infty,

where we used the fact that
\Pi(-\infty,-1)<\infty
and Lemma
2.1
. Moreover, since
V
is non-decreasing in each argument we have that for any
u>0
and
x>0
that

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,-1)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\geq\int_{(-\infty,-1)}V(0,y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)>-\infty.

Hence we conclude that
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)>-\infty
for any
(u,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Since
V
is continuous on
E
and the definition of
\widetilde{V}
we have that the mapping
(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)
is locally integrable.
∎Hence, we can define the operator
\mathcal{A}_{X}
in the sense of distributions by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})=-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\widetilde{V}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\widetilde{V}+B_{X}(\widetilde{V}).

The next lemma is an extension of Proposition 2.5 (see also Theorem 2.8) in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
.

Lemma 4.12
.

The distribution
\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G
is a non-negative distribution on
(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
. Moreover, we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G=0
on the set
C^{+}:=\{(u,x)\in(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty):0<x<b(u)\}
and
\mathcal{A}_{X}(V(0,\cdot))+G(0,\cdot)=0
on
(-\infty,0)
in the sense of distributions.

From the general theory of optimal stopping we have that (see
Peskir and Shiryaev 2006
, Theorem 2.4) for every
(u,x)\in E
, the stochastic process
\{Z_{t},t\geq 0\}
is a sub-martingale under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
, where

\displaystyle Z_{t}=V(U_{t},X_{t})+\int_{0}^{t}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s.

Moreover, we have that the stopped process
\{Z_{t\wedge\tau_{D}},t\geq 0\}
is a martingale under
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
for all
(u,x)\in E
. Then, from Doob’s stopping time theorem, we have that for every
(u,x)\in E
, the process
\{Z_{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}},t\geq 0\}
is a sub-martingale and
\{Z_{t\wedge\tau_{D}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}},t\geq 0\}
is a martingale under
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
. From the fact that
U_{t}=0
if and only if
X_{t}\leq 0
we have that, under
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
,

\displaystyle Z_{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}

\displaystyle=V(U_{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}},X_{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}})+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=V(u+t,X_{t})\mathbb{I}_{\{t<\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}+V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}\leq t\}}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}(u+t,X_{t})\mathbb{I}_{\{t<\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}+\widetilde{V}(u+\sigma_{0}^{-},X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}\leq t\}}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}(u+t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-},X_{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}})+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s

for every
u>0
and
x>0
. Hence, using an analogous argument as in Proposition 2.5 in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
(see also Proposition
B.6
), we have that
\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G
is a non-negative distribution on
(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
. Similarly, we have that for any
u>0
and
x>0
such that
x<b(u)
,

\displaystyle Z_{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{D}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}(u+t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{D},X_{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{D}})+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{D}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s.

Therefore, from Proposition
B.6
, we have that
\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G=0
on
C^{+}
in the sense of distributions. Lastly, since
b
is non-negative, we have that
\tau_{0}^{+}\leq\tau_{D}
. Hence, under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
, for any
x<0
, we have that
\{Z_{t\wedge\tau_{0}^{+}},t\geq 0\}
is a martingale. Moreover, since
X_{t}\leq 0
for all
t<\tau_{0}^{+}
we have that

\displaystyle Z_{t\wedge\tau_{0}^{+}}

\displaystyle=V(0,X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}^{+}})+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{0}^{+}}G(0,X_{s})\textup{d}s.

Then, as in Proposition 2.5 in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
, from Proposition
B.6
we deduce that
\mathcal{A}_{X}(V(0,\cdot))+G(0,\cdot)=0
in the sense of distributions on the set
(-\infty,0)
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

i)

In
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
the definition of the infinitesimal generator in the sense of distributions assumes that the value function is a bounded Borel measurable function. In our setting such condition can be relaxed by the fact that
(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
is a locally integrable function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.

ii)

We note that similar as in (
17
) the infinitesimal generator of
(U,X)
can be defined as
\mathcal{A}_{U,X}(V):=\partial/\partial u\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})
in the sense of distributions, where
\mathcal{A}_{X}
corresponds to the infinitesimal generator of
X
(seen as a distribution).

Let
\text{int}(D)
be the interior of the set
D
. For
(u,x)\in\text{int}(D)
we define the function

\displaystyle\Lambda(u,x):=\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)+G(u,x).

The next lemma states some basic properties of the function
\Lambda
.

Lemma 4.14
.

The function
\Lambda
is such that
0<\Lambda(u,x)<\infty
for all
(u,x)\in\text{int}(D)
. Moreover, it is strictly increasing in each argument and continuous in the interior of the set
D
. Furthermore,
\Lambda=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G
on
\text{int}(D)
in the sense of distributions.

It follows from Lemma
4.11
and the fact that
V
vanishes in
D
that
|\Lambda(u,x)|<\infty
for all
(u,x)\in E
. The fact that
\Lambda
is continuous on
D
follows from the continuity of
V
and
G
, the dominated convergence theorem and the fact that
\Pi
has no atoms. Moreover,
\Lambda
is strictly increasing in each argument on
D
since
V
is non-decreasing in each argument and
G
is strictly increasing in each argument on
D
. Then, we show that
\partial/\partial u\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G=\Lambda
on in the interior of
D
. Let
\varphi
be a
C^{\infty}
function with compact support on the interior of
D
. We have that

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial u}

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\widetilde{V}(u,x)[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\varphi(u,x)+B_{X}^{*}(\varphi)(u,x)]\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G(u,x)\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{b(u)}\widetilde{V}(u,x)[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\varphi(u,x)+B_{X}^{*}(\varphi)(u,x)]\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{b(u)}^{\infty}\widetilde{V}(u,x)[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\varphi(u,x)+B_{X}^{*}(\varphi)(u,x)]\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{b(u)}G(u,x)\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{b(u)}^{\infty}G(u,x)\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{b(u)}\widetilde{V}(u,x)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\varphi(u,x-y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{b(u)}^{\infty}G(u,x)\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u,

where the last equality follows since
V(u,x)=0
for all
x\geq b(u)
, and since
\varphi
has support on the interior of
D
, so that, for any
x\leq b(u)
we have
\varphi(u,x)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\varphi(u,x)=0
and

\displaystyle B_{X}^{*}(\varphi)(u,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,0)}[\varphi(u,x-y)-\varphi(u,x)+y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}]\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,0)}\varphi(u,x-y)\Pi(\textup{d}y).

Moreover, by the change of variable
z=x-y
, we see that for any
u>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{b(u)}\widetilde{V}(u,x)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\varphi(u,x-y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x)\varphi(u,x-y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,z+y)\varphi(u,z)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{b(u)}^{\infty}\varphi(u,z)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,z+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z,

where we used again that
V
vanishes on
D
and
\varphi
has support on the interior of
D
. Thus, we obtain that

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{b(u)}^{\infty}\varphi(u,z)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,z+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{b(u)}^{\infty}G(u,z)\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}z\textup{d}u

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{b(u)}^{\infty}\Lambda(u,x)\varphi(u,z)\textup{d}z\textup{d}u

\displaystyle=\langle\Lambda,\varphi\rangle.

Then we have that
\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G=\Lambda
on in the interior of
D
. Moreover from Lemma
4.12
and continuity of
\Lambda
we conclude that
\Lambda(u,x)\geq 0
for all
(u,x)\in\text{int}(D)
. In particular, is strictly positive in the interior of
D
since it is strictly increasing in that set.
∎

It turns out that the function
b
is continuous, its proof is analogous to the one presented in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
(see Theorem 4.2) in the American option setting.

Lemma 4.15
.

The function
b
is continuous.

We already know, from the fact that
D
is closed, that
b
is right-continuous. We then show the left continuity of
b
. We proceed by contradiction. Suppose there is a point
u_{*}>0
such that
b(u_{*}-):=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}b(u_{*}-h)>b(u_{*})
. Then, since
b
is non-decreasing, we have for all
(u,x)\in(0,u_{*})\times(b(u_{*}),b(u_{*}-))
that
V(u,x)<0
. Thus,
(0,u_{*})\times(b(u_{*}),b(u_{*}-))\subset C^{+}
. From Lemma
4.12
