we obtain that
\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G=0
in
(0,u_{*})\times(b(u_{*}),b(u_{*}-))
. Hence, for any non-negative
C^{\infty}
function
\varphi
with compact support in
(0,u_{*})\times(b(u_{*}),b(u_{*}-))
, we have that

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V},\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}\widetilde{V}(u,x)\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}u\textup{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}\widetilde{V}(\textup{d}u,x)\varphi(u,x)\textup{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 0,

where we used the fact that for each
x>0
,
u\mapsto\widetilde{V}(u,x)=V(u,x)
is non-decreasing. Hence, we conclude that
\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V})+G
is a non-positive distribution on
(0,u_{*})\times(b(u_{*}),b(u_{*}-))
.
Thus, by continuity of
\widetilde{V}=V
and
G
on
(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
, we have for any
u\in(0,u_{*})
and any non-negative test function
\psi
with compact support in
(b(u_{*}),b(u_{*}-))
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\widetilde{V}(u,x)\left[-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\psi(x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\psi(x)+B_{X}^{*}(\psi)(x)\right]+G(u,x)\psi(x)\right\}\textup{d}x\leq 0,

where
B_{X}^{*}(\psi)(x)=\int_{(-\infty,0)}(\psi(x-y)-\psi(x)+y\frac{d}{dx}\psi(x)\mathbb{I}_{\{|y|\leq 1\}})\Pi(\textup{d}y)
. Taking
u\uparrow u_{*}
in the equation above, using the fact that
\widetilde{V}(u_{*},x)=0
for all
x\geq b(u_{*})
, and since
\psi
has compact support in
(b(u_{*}),b(u_{*}-))
we get that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{u\uparrow u_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\widetilde{V}(u,x)\left[-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\psi(x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\psi(x)+B_{X}^{*}(\psi)(x)\right]+G(u,x)\psi(x)\right\}\textup{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{b(u_{*})}\widetilde{V}(u_{*},x)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\psi(x-y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x+\int_{b(u_{*})}^{b(u_{*}-)}G(u_{*},x)\psi(x)\textup{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{b(u_{*})}^{b(u_{*}-)}\psi(x)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u_{*},x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x+\int_{b(u_{*})}^{b(u_{*}-)}G(u_{*},x)\psi(x)\textup{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{b(u_{*})}^{b(u_{*}-)}\psi(x)\Lambda(u_{*},x)\textup{d}x

\displaystyle>0,

where the strict inequality follows from the fact that
\Lambda
is strictly positive in each argument in
D
(see Lemma
4.14
). Hence we have got a contradiction and
b(u-)=b(u)
for all
u>0
. Therefore
b
is a continuous function.
∎

From Lemma
4.9
we know that
b(u)
converges to zero when
u
tends to infinity. Moreover, from the discussion about
h
after equation (
32
), we know that in case that
X
is of finite variation, there exists a value
u_{h}^{*}<\infty
for which
h(u)=0
for all
u\geq u^{*}_{h}
. That suggests a similar behaviour for
b
. The next lemma addresses that conjecture.

Lemma 4.16
.

Define
u_{b}=\inf\{u>0:b(u)=0\}
. If
X
is of infinite variation or finite variation and infinite activity (that is
\Pi(-\infty,0)=\infty
) we have that
u_{b}=\infty
. Otherwise,
u_{b}=u^{*}
, where
u^{*}
is the unique solution to

\displaystyle G(u,0)+\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)=0.

(45)

From the fact that
h(u)>0
for all
u>0
when
X
is of infinite variation and the inequality
b(u)\geq h(u)
, we have that the assertion is true for this case. Suppose that
X
has finite variation with infinite activity, that is,
\Pi(-\infty,0)=\infty
, and assume that
u_{b}<\infty
. Then, since
b
is non-increasing, we have that
b(u)=0
for all
u>u_{b}
and thus
V(u,x)=0
for all
x>0
and
u>u_{b}
. From Lemma
4.14
we deduce that

\displaystyle G(u,x)+\int_{(-\infty,-x)}V(0,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\geq 0

for all
x>0
and for all
u>u_{b}
. Taking
x\downarrow 0
in the equation above and using the expression for
V(0,z)
(when
z<0
) given in (
35
) we have that for any
u>u_{b}
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq G(u,0)-\lim_{x\downarrow 0}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\int_{0}^{-x+y}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{x\downarrow 0}V(0,0)\Pi(-\infty,-x)

\displaystyle=-\infty

which is a contradiction and then
u_{b}=\infty
. Next, assume that
X
has finite variation with
\Pi(-\infty,0)<\infty
. Assume that
b(u^{*})>0
, then
V(u^{*},x)<0
for
x\in(0,b(u^{*}))
. Moreover, since
V\leq 0
and using the compensation formula for Poisson random measures (see (
1
)) we have that for all
u>0
and
x<b(u)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(V(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\tau_{D}\}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s-}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{\underline{X}_{s-}>0,X_{s-}+y<0\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{s\leq\tau_{D}\}}N(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}y)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{\underline{X}_{s-}>0\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<0\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{s\leq\tau_{D}\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{D}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right).

Then, from the Markov property we have that for any
0\leq x<b(u^{*})
,

\displaystyle V(u^{*},x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u^{*},x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}\wedge\tau_{0}^{-}}G(u^{*}+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u^{*},x}(V(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\tau_{D}\}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u^{*},x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}\wedge\tau_{0}^{-}}\left[G(u^{*}+s,X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle>0,

where the strict inequality follows from the fact that that
X
is of finite variation and then
\tau_{D}\wedge\tau_{0}^{-}>0
, the definition of
u^{*}
and the fact that
G
and
V
are non-decreasing in each argument. Then, we are contradicting the fact that
V(u^{*},x)<0
and we conclude that
b(u^{*})=0
so that
u_{b}\leq u^{*}
. Moreover, from Lemma
4.14
we know that for all
u>u_{b}
,

\displaystyle G(u,x)+\int_{(-\infty,-x)}V(0,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\geq 0\text{ for all }x>0.

Taking
x\downarrow 0
we get that for all
u\geq u_{b}
,
G(u,0)+\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\geq 0
. The latter implies that
u^{*}\leq u_{b}
(since
u\mapsto G(u,0)
is strictly increasing). Therefore we conclude that
u^{*}=u_{b}
and the proof is complete.
∎

As we mentioned before, proving the smoothness of
V
is challenging. However, it is possible to show that the derivatives of
V
at the boundary exist and are equal to zero.
Recall from Lemma
4.16
that when
X
is of infinite variation or finite variation with infinite activity we have that
b(u)>0
for all
u>0
. In the case that
X
is of finite variation we have that
b(u)>0
only if
u<u_{b}
where
u_{b}
is the solution to (
45
). In such cases, we can guarantee that the derivatives of
V
exist at the boundary and are equal to zero, which is proven in the following Theorem. Since the proof is rather long and technical, it can be found in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Suppose that
u>0
is such that
b(u)>0
. Then the first partial derivatives of
V(u,x)
exist at the point
x=b(u)
and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(u,b(u))=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial u}V(u,b(u))=0.

Recall from equation (
35
) that when
x<0
,

\displaystyle V(0,x)=-\int_{0}^{-x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z+V(0,0).

Note that the first term on the right-hand side of the equation above does not depend on the boundary
b
. Then, for
x<0
, the value function
V(0,x)
is characterised by the value
V(0,0)
. Moreover, from Lemma
4.16
we know that when
X
is of finite variation with
\Pi(-\infty,0)<\infty
, the value
u_{b}
is the unique solution to

\displaystyle G(u,0)-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\int_{0}^{-y}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-v-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(v)\textup{d}v\textup{d}z\Pi(\textup{d}y)+V(0,0)\Pi(-\infty,0)=0,

otherwise,
u_{b}=\infty
. Then if
X
is of finite variation with finite activity,
u_{b}
is also characterised by the value
V(0,0)
, where we know from Remark
4.3
that

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})}{p}\leq V(0,0)<0.

The next theorem gives a characterisation of the value function
V
on the set
(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
, the boundary
b
and the values
V(0,0)
and
u_{b}
as unique solutions of a system of non-linear integral equations within a class of functions. The method of proof is deeply inspired by the ideas of
du Toit, Peskir and
Shiryaev 2008
. However, the presence of jumps adds an important level of difficulty. In particular, when
\Pi\neq 0
, the inequality (see Lemma
4.14
)

\displaystyle\Lambda(u,x)=G(u,x)+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)>0,

for all
(u,x)\in\text{int}(D)
, is a necessary condition for the stochastic process
\{V(U_{t},X_{t})+\int_{0}^{t}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s,t\geq 0\}
to be a submartingale.

Theorem 4.18
.

Let
p>1
and
X
be a spectrally negative Lévy process drifting to infinity such that its Lévy measure
\Pi
has no atoms and
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|x|^{p+1}\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
. For all
u>0
and
x>0
, the function
V
can be written as

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(u+s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}+y<b(u+s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\left[G(u+s,X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,-X_{s})}V(0,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right),

(46)

the value
V(0,0)
satisfies

\displaystyle V(0,

\displaystyle 0)=-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(s,X_{s})\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}<b(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}+y<b(s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y\leq 0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right),

(47)

whilst the curve
b
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(b(u))

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{b(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(u+s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}+y<b(u+s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{b(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\left[G(u+s,X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,-X_{s})}V(0,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

(48)

for all
u<u_{b}
, where for
x\leq 0
, the function
V(0,x)
depends on
V(0,0)
via (
35
). For
u\geq u_{b}
, we have
b(u)=0
, where
u_{b}=\infty
in the case
X
is of infinite variation or finite variation with
\Pi(-\infty,0)=\infty
. Otherwise,
u_{b}
is the unique solution to

\displaystyle G(u,0)-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\int_{0}^{-y}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z\Pi(\textup{d}y)+V(0,0)\Pi(-\infty,0)=0.

(49)

Moreover, in the case that there is a Brownian motion component (i.e.
\sigma>0
) we have that (
4.18
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(0,0)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{-}(0,0),

(50)

where
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(u,0)
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{-}(0,0)
are the right and left derivatives of
x\mapsto V(u,x)
and
x\mapsto V(0,x)
at zero, respectively and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(0,0)=\lim_{u\downarrow 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(u,0)
.

Furthermore, the quadruplet
(V,b,V(0,0),u_{b})
is uniquely characterised by the equations above, where
V
is considered in the class of non-positive continuous functions such that

\displaystyle\int_{(-x-b(u),-x)}

\displaystyle V(u,b(u)+x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,-x-b(u)]}V(0,b(u)+x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)+G(u,x+b(u))\geq 0

(51)

for all
u<u_{b}
and
x>0
and
b
is considered in the class of non-increasing functions with
b\geq h
whereas
-\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{E}(g^{p})\leq V(0,0)<0
.

Since the proof of Theorem
4.18
is rather long, we break it into a series of Lemmas. The next section is entirely dedicated to that purpose.

5
Proof of Theorem
4.18

First, we show that the relevant quantities are integrable. The proof of the Lemma is long, and then it is included in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 5.1
.

We have that for all
(u,x)\in E
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|G(U_{s},X_{s})|\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)<\infty,

(52)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\right)>-\infty.

(53)

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\right)=0.

(54)

Furthermore, when
X
is of finite variation with finite activity (that is,
\Pi(-\infty,0)<\infty))
we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)=0.

(55)

Next, we show that
V
satisfies the alternative representation mentioned in the infinite variation case or finite variation case with infinite activity.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that
X
is of infinite variation of finite variation with infinite activity. Then we have that
V
and
b
satisfy equations (
46
) and (
48
).

Recall that
V
is continuous on
E
and, when
X
is of infinite variation, we have that for any
u>0
,
\lim_{x\downarrow 0}V(u,x)=V(0,0)
(see Lemma
4.10
), implying that
\widetilde{V}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. We follow an analogous argument as
Lamberton and Mikou 2013
(see Theorem 3.2). Let
\rho
be a positive
C^{\infty}
function with support in
[0,1]\times[0,1]
and
\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho(v,y)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y=1
. For
n\geq 1
, define
\rho_{n}(v,y)=n^{2}\rho(nv,ny)
, then
\rho_{n}
is
C^{\infty}
and has compact support in
[0,1/n]\times[0,1/n]
. The function defined by
\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x):=(\widetilde{V}*\rho_{n})(u,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{V}(u-v,x-y)\rho_{n}(v,y)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y
is a
C^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
function such that the derivatives of
\widetilde{V}_{n}
and the function
(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)
are bounded in the set
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Moreover, we have that
\widetilde{V}_{n}\uparrow\widetilde{V}
on
E
when
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Similar as in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
(see proof of Proposition 2.5), due to equation (
106
), we have that for all
(u,x)\in[(1/n,\infty)\times(1/n,\infty)]\cap C^{+}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x)+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V}_{n})(u,x)=-(G*\rho_{n})(u,x),

(56)

where
\mathcal{A}_{X}
is the infinitesimal generator of the process
X
. On the other hand, since
V
vanishes on
D
, we have that
\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x)=0
for
(u,x)\in D
and
n
sufficiently large. Hence, since
\Pi
has no atoms, we deduce that for any
(u,x)\in E
and
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u}

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x)+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V}_{n})(u,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{(-x,0)}\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)+\int_{(-\infty,-x)}\widetilde{V}_{n}(0,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y).

Therefore, by continuity of
V
on
E
and the dominated convergence theorem, we see that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x)+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V}_{n})(u,x)\right]=\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)

for any
(u,x)\in D
.

Next, let
u>0
and
x>0
fixed, and take
n>0
and
k>0
such that
u>1/n>0
and
x>k\geq 1/n>0
. We apply Itô formula to
\widetilde{V}_{n}(u+t\wedge\tau_{k-x}^{-},X_{t\wedge\tau_{k-x}^{-}}+x)
to get

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{n}

\displaystyle(u+t\wedge\tau_{k-x}^{-},X_{t\wedge\tau_{k-x}^{-}}+x)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x)+M_{t}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k-x}^{-}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}_{n}(u+s,X_{s}+x)+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V}_{n})(u+s,X_{s}+x)\right]\textup{d}s,

where
\{M_{t},t\geq 0\}
is a zero mean martingale. Taking expectations and from (
56
) we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{V}_{n}(u+t\wedge\tau_{k}^{-},X_{t\wedge\tau_{k}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k}^{-}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}_{n}(u+s,X_{s})+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V}_{n})(u+s,X_{s})\right]\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}_{n}(u,x)-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k}^{-}}(G*\rho_{n})(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k}^{-}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\widetilde{V}_{n}(u+s,X_{s})+\mathcal{A}_{X}(\widetilde{V}_{n})(u+s,X_{s})\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right),

where we used the fact that
b
is finite for all
u>0
, and that
\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{s}=b(u+s))=0
for all
s>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
when
X
is of infinite variation or finite variation with infinite activity (see
Sato 1999
, Theorem 27.4). Since
X_{t}\geq\underline{X}_{\infty}
for all
t>0
and
V
is non-decreasing in each argument we have that

\displaystyle 0\geq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\widetilde{V}_{n}(u+t\wedge\tau_{k}^{-},X_{t\wedge\tau_{k}^{-}})\right)\geq-A^{\prime}_{p-1}-C^{\prime}_{p-1}\mathbb{E}_{x-1}((-\underline{X}_{\infty})^{p})+V(0,0)

\displaystyle>-\infty,

where the second inequality follows from equation (
36
) and the last quantity is finite by Lemma
2.1
. Therefore, by the dominated convergence theorem and letting
n,t\rightarrow\infty
and
k\downarrow 0
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\widetilde{V}(u+\tau_{0}^{-},X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle=V(u,x)-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u+s,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

(57)

for all
u>0
and
x>0
. Note that, since
b(u)<\infty
for all
u>0
and
\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}b(u)=0
, we have that
\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{V}(u,x)=\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}V(u,x)=0
. Hence, since
\widetilde{V}(u,y)=V(0,y)
for any
u\geq 0
and
y\leq 0
, and
X
drifts to infinity we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\widetilde{V}(u+\tau_{0}^{-},X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(V(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}}\right)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}=0,\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(V(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}<0\}}\right)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s-}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s-}+y<0\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\underline{X}_{s-}\geq 0\}}N(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}y)\right)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(0,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<0\}}\textup{d}s\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right),

where in the second last equality, we used the probability of creeping given in (
7
) (note that
\Phi(0)=0
since
X
drifts to infinity) and in the last equality, the compensation formula for Poisson random measures. Then, from above and equation (
5
) we see that for any
u>0
and
x>0
,

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\widetilde{V}(u+\tau_{0}^{-},X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u+x,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(u+s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}+y<b(u+s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\left[G(u+s,X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,-X_{s})}V(0,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right),

where in the last equality we used that
V(u+s,X_{s}+y)=0
when
X_{s}+y\geq b(u+s)
. Moreover, we have that (
48
) follows directly from the equation above since
V(u,b(u))=0
for all
u>0
.
∎

We define an auxiliary function. For all
(u,x)\in E
, let

\displaystyle R(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right).

(58)

Note from Lemma
5.1
that
R
is a well-defined function.
The following Lemma shows that
R
coincides with
V
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For any
(u,x)\in E
we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right).

(59)

First, we assume that
X
is of infinite variation or finite variation with
\Pi(-\infty,0)=\infty
. Let
(u,x)\in E
, from the Markov property applied to the stopping time
\tau_{0}^{+}
, the fact that
b
is non-negative and equation (
35
), we get that for all
x<0
,

\displaystyle R(0,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}G(0,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+R(0,0)=V(0,x)+R(0,0)-V(0,0).

Similarly, using the Markov property at time
\tau_{0}^{-}
, we get that for any
u>0
and
x>0
,

\displaystyle R(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(R(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u+s,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=V(u,x)+\mathbb{E}_{x}([R(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})-V(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})]\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})

\displaystyle=V(u,x)+[R(0,0)-V(0,0)]\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty),

(60)

where the second equality follows from equation (
5
) and the last from the expression for
R(0,x)
deduced above. Then, applying the strong Markov property at time
\tau_{D}
to the definition of
R
(see (
58
)), and the fact that for any
s<\tau_{D}
we have that
X_{s}<b(U_{s})
, we get that for any
(u,x)\in E
such that
x<b(u)
,

\displaystyle R(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(R(U_{\tau_{D}},X_{\tau_{D}}))

\displaystyle=V(u,x)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(R(U_{\tau_{D}},X_{\tau_{D}})),

where we used that
\tau_{D}
is optimal for
V
. Since we are considering the infinite variation case or the finite variation case with infinite activity we have that
b(u)>0
for all
u>0
(see Lemma
4.16
), so then
X_{\tau_{D}}>0
and
U_{\tau_{D}}>0
. Hence, from equation (
60
) and the equation above we deduce that for any
(u,x)\in E
such that
x<b(u)
,

\displaystyle R(u,x)

\displaystyle=V(u,x)+[R(0,0)-V(0,0)]\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(\mathbb{P}_{X_{\tau_{D}}}(\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty)),

where we used that
(U_{\tau_{D}},X_{\tau_{D}})\in D
and that
V
vanishes on
D
. In particular, taking
u=0
and
x=0
in the equation above and rearranging the terms, we conclude that

\displaystyle 0=[R(0,0)-V(0,0)]\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{P}_{X_{\tau_{D}}}(\tau_{0}^{-}=\infty)).

Since
b(u)>0
for all
u>0
and
\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{0}^{-}=\infty)>0
for all
x>0
, the equation above implies that
R(0,0)=V(0,0)
, and then
V(u,x)=R(u,x)
in this case. For the finite variation with finite activity case, consider the sequence of stopping times,

\displaystyle\tau_{b}^{(1)}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq b(U_{t})\},

and for
k=1,2,\ldots
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{b}^{(k)}=\inf\{t\geq\tau_{b}^{(k)}:X_{t}<b(U_{t})\},

\displaystyle\tau_{b}^{(k+1)}=\inf\{t\geq\sigma_{b}^{(k)}:X_{t}\geq b(U_{t})\}.

Since
X
is of finite variation we have that
\tau_{b}^{(k)}<\sigma_{b}^{(k)}<\tau_{b}^{(k+1)}
for all
k\geq 1
. Let
u>0
and
x\geq b(u)
, by the Markov property applied to time
\tau_{b}^{(2)}
we get that

\displaystyle R(u,x)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{b}^{(1)}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{b}^{(1)}<\infty\}}\int_{\sigma_{b}^{(1)}}^{\tau_{b}^{(2)}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(R(U_{\tau_{b}^{(2)}},X_{\tau_{b}^{(2)}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{(2)}<\infty\}})

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{b}^{(1)}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{b}^{(1)}<\infty\}}V(U_{\sigma_{b}^{(1)}},X_{\sigma_{b}^{(1)}})\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(R(U_{\tau_{b}^{(2)}},X_{\tau_{b}^{(2)}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{(2)}<\infty\}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(R(U_{\tau_{b}^{(2)}},X_{\tau_{b}^{(2)}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{(2)}<\infty\}}),

where in the second inequality, we used the Markov property at time
\sigma_{b}^{(1)}
, the definition of
V
in terms of the stopping time
\tau_{D}
and in the last equality, we used the compensation formula for Poisson random measures. Using an induction argument we can verify that for all
x\geq b(u)
and
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle R(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(R(U_{\tau_{b}^{(n)}},X_{\tau_{b}^{(n)}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{(n)}<\infty\}}).

It can be shown that for any
(u,x)\in E
,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau_{b}^{(n)}=\infty

\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
-a.s. Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem, the fact that
\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}R(u,x)=0
(see (
54
) and (
55
)), that
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}U_{t}=t-g_{t}\geq\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}t-g=\infty
and that
X
drifts to infinity we get that

\displaystyle R(u,x)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(R(U_{\tau_{b}^{(n)}},X_{\tau_{b}^{(n)}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{(n)}<\infty\}})=0

for all
u>0
and
x\geq b(u)
. Next, take
x<b(u)
, applying the strong Markov property and using that
\tau_{b}^{(1)}
is optimal for
V
we get that

\displaystyle R(u,x)=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{(1)}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(R(U_{\tau_{b}^{(1)}},X_{\tau_{b}^{(1)}}))=V(u,x).

Hence, we conclude that for all
(u,x)\in E
,

\displaystyle V(u,x)=R(u,x).

The proof is now complete.
∎Now we are ready to show that in either case regarding the variation of
X
, the equations stated in Theorem
4.18
hold.

Lemma 5.4
.

The quadruplet
(V,b,V(0,0),u_{b})
satisfy equations (
46
)-(
49
) and equation (
4.18
).

We know from Lemma
5.2
that equations (
46
) and (
48
) hold in the infinite variation case or in the finite variation case with finite activity. Then suppose that
X
is of finite variation and
\Pi(-\infty,0)<\infty
. The strong Markov property applied at time
\tau_{0}^{-}
in (
5.3
) implies that (
5
) also holds in this case. Then, proceeding as in Lemma
5.2
(see argument below equation (
5
)) we see that (
46
) and (
48
) also hold in the finite variation case with finite activity. Moreover, the assertions about
u_{b}
and equation (
49
) follow from Lemma
4.16
, the lower bound for
V(0,0)
follows from Remark
4.3
and (
4.18
) holds due to Lemma
4.14
.

We now proceed to show that (
4.18
) is satisfied for
V(0,0)
. Taking
u=x=0
in (
5.3
) and using Fubini’s theorem we have that

\displaystyle V(0,0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(0,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}\leq 0\}}\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,0]}G(0,z)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{s}\in\textup{d}z)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(0,\infty)}\int_{(0,b(u))}G(u,z)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(U_{s}\in\textup{d}u,X_{s}\in\textup{d}z)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{(0,\infty)}\int_{(b(u),\infty)}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,z+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(U_{s}\in\textup{d}u,X_{s}\in\textup{d}z)\textup{d}s,

where in the second equality we used the fact that
b
is non-negative and that
U_{s}=0
if and only if
X_{s}\leq 0
. From the fact that
G(0,z)=-\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})
for any
z<0
and the formulas for the
0
-potential density of
X
and
(U,X)
(see equations (
12
) and (
22
)), respectively, we obtain that

\displaystyle V(0,0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(0,b(s))}G(s,z)\frac{z}{s}\mathbb{P}(X_{s}\in\textup{d}z)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(b(s),\infty)}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(s,z+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\frac{z}{s}\mathbb{P}(X_{s}\in\textup{d}z)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(s,X_{s})\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}<b(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(s,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right).

Then, equation (
4.18
) holds by recalling that
\widetilde{V}(u,x)=V(u,x)
when
u>0
and
x>0
, and
\widetilde{V}(u,x)=V(0,x)
when
x\leq 0
for any
u\geq 0
.
∎We finish the first part of the proof by showing that the derivative of
V
at
(0,0)
exists when there is a Brownian motion component.

Lemma 5.5
.

The function
V
satisfies equation (
50
) when
\sigma>0
.

We first note that the strict inequalities in the indicator functions in Equation (
5.3
) can be changed to inequalities when
x=u=0
. Indeed, due to (
22
) and Fubini’s theorem we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{(0,\infty)}\int_{(0,\infty)}G(v,y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<y<b(v)\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(U_{s}\in\textup{d}v,X_{s}\in\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{(0,\infty)}\int_{0}^{b(v)}G(v,y)\Phi^{\prime}(0)\mathbb{P}(\tau_{y}^{+}\in\textup{d}v)\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{(0,\infty)}\int_{(0,\infty)}G(v,y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<y\leq b(v)\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(U_{s}\in\textup{d}v,X_{s}\in\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}\leq b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right).

Similarly, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}\geq b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right).

Hence, from equation (
5.3
) and the dominated convergence theorem (see Lemma
5.1
) we obtain that

\displaystyle V(0,0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}\geq b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s}+\delta)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}\geq b(U_{s}+\delta)\}}\textup{d}s\right),

where we used that
G
and
V
are continuous (since
\sigma>0
and then
W
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
), that the mappings
s\mapsto\mathbb{I}_{\{x\geq s\}}
and
s\mapsto\mathbb{I}_{\{x<s\}}
are left-continuous and that
b(U_{s}+\delta)\uparrow b(U_{s})
when
\delta\downarrow 0
, for any
s\geq 0
. Then, using once again (
22
), Fubini’s theorem and the equation above we can easily see that

\displaystyle V(0,0)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}[K_{1}(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})+K_{2}(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})]\textup{d}s\bigg),

where for any
(u,x)\in E
,

\displaystyle K_{1}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=G(u,x)\mathbb{I}_{\{x\leq b(u)\}},

\displaystyle K_{2}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(u)\}}.

Therefore, from Lemma
A.3
we deduce that

\displaystyle V(0,0)=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}}K^{-}(\delta,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}-\varepsilon)\right)+K^{+}(\delta,\varepsilon)}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(\varepsilon)},

where for all
\delta>0
and
x\leq 0
,

\displaystyle K^{-}(\delta,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}[K_{1}(\delta,X_{r})+K_{2}(\delta,X_{r})]\textup{d}r\right)

and for all
\delta,x>0
,

\displaystyle K^{+}(\delta,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}[K_{1}(\delta+s,X_{r})+K_{2}(\delta+s,X_{r})]\textup{d}r\right).

Using the fact that
b
is non-negative and
W(x)=0
for all
x<0
(and then
G(\delta,x)=G(0,x)
for all
x<0
), we have that for all
x<0
,

\displaystyle K^{-}(\delta,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}G(\delta,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)=V(0,x)-V(0,0),

where the last equality follows from the expression of
V
in terms of the stopping time
\tau_{D}
. Moreover, from equation (
5
), we deduce that

\displaystyle K^{+}(\delta,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=V(\delta,\varepsilon)-\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}(V(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}}),

for all
\delta>0
and
x>0
. Hence, by substituting the expressions for
K^{+}
and
K^{-}
, rearranging the terms and by dominated convergence theorem we see that

\displaystyle V(0,0)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})-\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{V(\delta,\varepsilon)-V(0,0)\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty)}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})-\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(0,0)+V(0,0),

where in the last equality we used that
\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty)=1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(\varepsilon)
(see equation (
5
)) and the fact that
W^{\prime}(0)=2/\sigma^{2}
.
Therefore, from Lemma
A.4
we see that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})-\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{-}(0,0).

Then, we deduce that

\displaystyle V(0,0)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\left[-\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{-}(0,0)+\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(0,0)\right]+V(0,0),

and we conclude that (
50
) holds. The proof is now complete.
∎

Next, we show the uniqueness claim. Suppose that there exist continuous functions
H
and
c
on
E
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, respectively, and real numbers
H_{0}<0
and
u_{H}>0
such that the conclusions of the theorem hold. Specifically, suppose that
H
is a non-positive continuous real valued function on
E
,
c
is a continuous real valued function on
(0,\infty)
such that
c\geq h\geq 0
and
H_{0}\in(-\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{E}(g^{p}),0)
such that equations (
46
)-(
48
) hold. That is, we assume that
H
,
H_{0}
and
c
are solutions to the equations

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle(u,x)

\displaystyle=H_{0}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}H(u+s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}+y<c(u+s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\left[G(u+s,X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,-X_{s})}H(0,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right),

(61)

for
u>0
and
x>0
,

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z

(62)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(s,X_{s})\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}<c(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}H(s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}+y<c(s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}H(0,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y\leq 0)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right),

(63)

and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=H_{0}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(c(u))

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{c(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}H(u+s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}+y<c(u+s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{c(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\left[G(u+s,X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,-X_{s})}H(0,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right),

(64)

for
u<u_{H}
, where for any
x\leq 0
,

\displaystyle H(0,x)=-\int_{0}^{-x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z+H_{0}.

(65)

The value
u_{H}
is such that
u_{H}=\infty
when
X
is of infinite variation or
X
is of finite variation with infinite activity. Otherwise, let
u_{H}
be the solution of

\displaystyle G(u,0)-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\int_{0}^{-y}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z\Pi(\textup{d}y)+H_{0}\Pi(-\infty,0)=0.

(66)

Moreover, assume that
c(u)>0
for all
u<u_{H}
and
c(u)=0
for all
u\geq u_{H}
, and that

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,-x)}\widetilde{H}(u,x+c(u)+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)+G(u,c(u)+x)\geq 0

(67)

for all
u<u_{H}
and
x>0
, where
\widetilde{H}
is the extension of
H
to the set
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
as in (
2
). That is,

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(u,x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}H(u,x),&\text{for }u>0\text{ and }x>0,\\
H(0,x),&\text{for }u\geq 0\text{ and }x\leq 0,\\
H(0,0),&\text{for }u=0\text{ and }x>0.\end{array}\right.

Note that, using the same arguments as the ones used in Lemma
5.2
(see argument below Equation (
5
)), we can see that (
61
) and (
64
) are equivalent to

\displaystyle H(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(H(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(u+s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(u+s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

(71)

for all
(u,x)\in E
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{c(u)}

\displaystyle(H(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})+\mathbb{E}_{c(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{c(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(u+s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(u+s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

(72)

for any
u<u_{H}
. Following a similar proof than
du Toit and Peskir 2008
we show that
c=b
, which implies that
H=V
,
H_{0}=V(0,0)
and
u_{H}=u_{b}
.

First, we show that
H
has an alternative representation.

Lemma 5.6
.

For all
(u,x)\in E
we have that

\displaystyle H(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(U_{s})\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right).

(73)

Moreover, the same conclusion holds if, in the case that
\sigma>0
, instead of (
62
) we assume that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}H_{+}(0,0)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}H_{-}(0,0),

(74)

where
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}H_{+}(u,0)
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}H_{-}(0,0)
are the right and left derivatives of
x\mapsto H(u,x)
and
x\mapsto H(0,x)
at zero, respectively and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}H_{+}(0,0)=\lim_{u\downarrow 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}H_{+}(u,0)
.

Define for all
(u,x)\in E
the function

\displaystyle K(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(U_{s})\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right).

In a analogous way as in Lemma
5.4
, from (
12
) and (
22
), we have that for any spectrally negative Lévy process
X
,

\displaystyle K(0,0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(U_{s})\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(s,X_{s})\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{s}<c(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\frac{X_{s}}{s}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=H_{0}

\displaystyle=H(0,0).

Moreover, by the Markov property, the fact that
X
creeps upwards,
c
is a non-negative curve and the definition of
H(0,x)
, for
x<0
(see (
65
)), we have that for
u=0
and
x<0
,

\displaystyle K(0,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+K(0,0)=H(0,x).

(75)

Then, taking
u>0
and
x>0
, by the strong Markov property at time
\tau_{0}^{-}
and equation (
5
),

\displaystyle K(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(K(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(u+s,X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(u+s)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=H(u,x).

If, in the case that
\sigma>0
, we assume that
H
and
c
satisfy equations (
65
), (
5
), (
5
) and (
74
). From formula (
21
) (in a similar way as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
) we obtain that

\displaystyle K(0,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\left[-\frac{\partial}{\partial x}H_{-}(0,0)+\frac{\partial}{\partial x}H_{+}(0,0)\right]+H(0,0)

\displaystyle=H(0,0).

The rest of the proof remains unchanged.
∎Define the set
D_{c}=\{(u,x)\in E:x\geq c(u)\}
. We show in the following lemma that
H
vanishes in
D_{c}
so that
D_{c}
corresponds to the “stopping set” of
H
.

Lemma 5.7
.

We have that
H(u,x)=0
for all
(u,t)\in D_{c}
.

Note that, from equations (
5
) and (
5
), we know that
H(u,c(u))=0
for all
u\in(0,u_{H})
. Let
(u,x)\in D_{c}
such that
x>c(u)
and define
\sigma_{c}
as the first time that
(U,X)
exits
D_{c}
, that is,

\displaystyle\sigma_{c}=\inf\{s\geq 0:X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}.

From the fact that
X_{r}\geq c(U_{r})
for all
r<\sigma_{c}
, the Markov property and representation (
73
) of
H
we can see that

\displaystyle H(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(H(U_{\sigma_{c}},X_{\sigma_{c}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{c}<\infty\}})+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{c}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{c}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(U_{s})\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(H(U_{\sigma_{c}},X_{\sigma_{c}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{c}<\infty,X_{\sigma_{c}}<c(U_{s})\}})

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{c}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(U_{s})\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right),

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{\sigma_{c}}=c(u+\sigma_{c}))>0
only when
\sigma>0
and then
H(u,c(u))=0
for all
u>0
(since
u_{H}=\infty
in this case). Then, since
H\leq 0
, applying the compensation formula for Poisson random measures and the fact that
\sigma_{c}\leq\tau_{0}^{-}
(since
c(u)\geq 0
for all
u>0
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}(H(U_{\sigma_{c}},X_{\sigma_{c}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{c}<\infty\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{\sigma_{c}}<c(U_{s})\}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{r}\geq c(u+r)\text{ for all }r<s\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s-}+y<c(u+s)\}}\widetilde{H}(u+s,X_{s-}+y)N(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}y)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{r}\geq c(u+r)\text{ for all }r<s\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s-}+y<c(u+s)\}}\widetilde{H}(u+s,X_{s-}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{c}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y<c(U_{s})\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right).

Hence, we have that
H(u,x)=0
for all
(u,x)\in D_{c}
as claimed.
∎

The following Lemma states that
H
dominates the value function
V
.

Lemma 5.8
.

We have that
H(u,x)\geq V(u,x)
for all
(u,x)\in E
.

If
(u,x)\in D_{c}
we have the inequality

\displaystyle H(u,x)=0\geq V(u,x).

Now we show that the inequality also holds in
E\setminus D_{c}
. Consider the stopping time

\displaystyle\tau_{c}=\inf\{s\geq 0:X_{s}\geq c(U_{s})\}.

Then, using the Markov property and equation (
73
), we get that for all
(u,x)\in E
with
x<c(u)
,

\displaystyle H(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(H(U_{\tau_{c}},X_{\tau_{c}})\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{c}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{c}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(H(U_{\tau_{c}},c(U_{\tau_{c}}))\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{c}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right),

where in the second equality we used the fact
X
creeps upwards and
\tau_{c}<\infty
. Note that, since for any
t>0
,
X_{t}>0
if and only if
U_{t}>0
and
c(u)>0
for all
u<u_{H}
, we have that
U_{\tau_{c}}<u_{H}
so then
c(U_{\tau_{c}})>0
, and hence
H(U_{\tau_{c}},c(U_{\tau_{c}}))=0
. Therefore

\displaystyle H(u,x)=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{c}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)\geq V(u,x),

where the inequality follows from the definition of
V
as per (
30
).
∎It turns out that the fact that
H
dominates
V
implies that
b
dominates the curve
c
. This fact is shown in the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

We have that
b(u)\geq c(u)
for all
u>0
.

Note that in the case that
X
is of finite variation with
\Pi(-\infty,0)<\infty
, we have that
c(u)=0\leq b(u)
for all
u>u_{H}
. We proceed by contradiction. Suppose that there exists
u_{0}>0
such that
b(u_{0})<c(u_{0})
. Then, in the case that
X
is of finite variation with
\Pi(-\infty,0)<\infty
, it holds that
u_{0}<u_{H}
. Take
x>c(u_{0})
and consider the stopping time

\displaystyle\sigma_{b}

\displaystyle=\inf\{s>0:X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}.

Then, from the Markov property and the representation of
H
given in (
73
), we have that

\displaystyle H(u_{0},x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(H(U_{\sigma_{b}^{-}},X_{\sigma_{b}^{-}})\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{b}^{-}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{b}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right).

Moreover, since
V(u,x)=0
for
(u,x)\in D
and
0\geq H\geq V
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(H(U_{\sigma_{b}^{-}},X_{\sigma_{b}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(H(U_{\sigma_{b}^{-}},X_{\sigma_{b}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{\sigma_{b}^{-}}<b(U_{\sigma_{b}^{-}})\}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{b}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+y\leq b(U_{s})\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{b}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\right),

where the second equality follows from the compensation formula for Poisson random measures. Hence, combining the two equations above and from the fact that
x>c(u_{0})
, then
H(u_{0},x)=0
, we get

\displaystyle 0=\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{b}^{-}}\left[G(U_{s},X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right).

Due to the continuity of
b
and
c
, there exists a value
u_{1}
sufficiently small such that
c(v)>b(v)
for all
v\in[u_{0},u_{0}+u_{1})
. Thus, from Lemma
4.14
, the fact that
u\mapsto G(u,x)
is strictly increasing when
x>0
and the inequality
H\geq V
(see Lemma
5.8
), we have that for all
u>0
and
x>b(u)
,

\displaystyle G(u,x)+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\geq G(u,x)+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)>0,

where the strict inequality follows from Lemma
4.14
. Note that taking
x
sufficiently large we have that, under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{u_{0},x}
,
X
spends a positive amount of time between the curves
b(u)
and
c(u)
for
u\in[u_{0},u_{0}+u_{1}]
with positive probability. Thus, since
\sigma_{c}<\tau_{0}^{-}
the above facts imply that

\displaystyle 0=\mathbb{E}_{u_{0},x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{b}^{-}}\left[G(U_{s},X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)>0.

We have got a contradiction and then we have that
c(u)\leq b(u)
for all
u>0
.
∎Note that (
67
) and the definition of
u_{H}
given in (
66
) imply the inequality
G(u,x)+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\geq 0
for all
u>0
and
x>c(u)
. It can be shown that such inequality guarantees that the process
\{H(U_{t},X_{t})+\int_{0}^{t}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s,t\geq 0\}
is a
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
-submartingale, for all
(u,x)\in E
. We finish the proof by showing that indeed
c
corresponds to
b
.

Lemma 5.10
.

We have that
c(u)=b(u)
for all
u>0
and
V(u,x)=H(u,x)
for all
(u,x)\in E
.

Suppose that there exists
u>0
such that
c(u)<b(u)
and take
x\in(c(u),b(u))
. Then, by the Markov property and representation (
73
) we have that

\displaystyle H(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(H(U_{\tau_{D}},X_{\tau_{D}})\right)+\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right),

(76)

where
\tau_{D}=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}\geq b(U_{t})\}
. On the other hand, we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right).

Since
X_{\tau_{D}}=b(U_{\tau_{D}})\geq c(U_{\tau_{D}})
under
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
, we deduce from Lemma
5.7
that
H(U_{\tau_{D}},X_{\tau_{D}})=0
. Hence, using the inequality
H\geq V
(see Lemma
5.8
) in (
76
), we obtain that

\displaystyle 0\geq\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}\left[G(U_{s},X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{H}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>c(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)>0,

where the strict inequality follows by the inequality (
67
) and the continuity of
b
and
c
. This contradiction allows us to conclude that
c(u)=b(u)
for all
u>0
. Lastly, recall from the proof of Lemma
5.8
that
H(u,x)=0=V(u,x)
for all
x>c(u)=b(u)
, and

\displaystyle H(u,x)=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{c}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)=V(u,x)

for all
(u,x)\in E
such that
x<c(u)=b(u)
. The proof is now complete.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

A close inspection of the proof tells us that the assumption
H\leq 0
can be dropped when
\Pi\equiv 0
.

6
Examples

6.1
Brownian Motion with drift example

Suppose that
X_{t}
is given by

\displaystyle X_{t}=\mu t+\sigma B_{t},

where
\mu>0
,
\sigma>0
and
B=\{B_{t},t\geq 0\}
is a standard Brownian motion. Here, we consider the case
p=2
. Then, for any
(u,x)\in E
we have,

\displaystyle G(u,x)=u\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(x)-\mathbb{E}_{x}(g).

It is well known that for
\beta\geq 0
and
q\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\psi(\beta)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\beta^{2}+\mu\beta\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Phi(q)=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left[\sqrt{\mu^{2}+2\sigma^{2}q}-\mu\right].

Thus,
\psi^{\prime}(0+)=\mu
,
\Phi^{\prime}(0)=\frac{1}{\mu}
,
\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)=-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{3}}
and
\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)=3\sigma^{4}/\mu^{5}
. The scale function is (see e.g.
Kuznetsov, Kyprianou and
Rivero 2013
, on p. 102) given by

\displaystyle W(x)=\frac{1}{\mu}(1-\exp(-2\mu x/\sigma^{2})),\qquad x\geq 0.

An easy calculation shows that
W^{*(2)}
is given by

\displaystyle W^{*(2)}(x)=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}x[1+\exp(-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x)]-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\frac{1}{\mu}(1-\exp(-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x)),\qquad x\geq 0.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the value
\mathbb{E}_{x}(g)
can be calculated from (
24
) via differentiation to have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}(g)

\displaystyle=-\psi^{\prime}(0+)[\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0+)+x\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}]+\psi^{\prime}(0+)W^{*2}(x)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{x}{\mu},&x<0,\\
\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\exp(-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x)+\frac{x}{\mu}\exp(-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x),&x\geq 0.\end{array}\right.

and
\mathbb{E}(g^{2})=\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)\psi^{\prime}(0+)=3(\sigma/\mu)^{4}
. Moreover, it is well known that
X_{r}\sim N(\mu r,\sigma^{2}r)
for any
r\geq 0
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{r}\in\textup{d}z,\underline{X}_{r}\leq 0)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{2\mu}{\sigma^{2}}x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}r}}\phi\left(\frac{z+x-\mu r}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}r}}\right)\textup{d}z,

for any
r\geq 0
,
x\geq 0
and
z\geq 0
, where
\phi
is the density of a standard normal distribution. Hence we have that for any
x\geq 0
and
z\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{r}\in\textup{d}z,\underline{X}_{r}\geq 0)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}r}}\left[\phi\left(\frac{z-x-\mu r}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}r}}\right)-e^{-\frac{2\mu}{\sigma^{2}}x}\phi\left(\frac{z+x-\mu r}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}r}}\right)\right]\textup{d}z.

Then, we calculate for any
u>0
and
x>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}G(u+r,X_{r})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{r}<b(r+u)\}}\textup{d}r\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{b(r+u)}[(r+u)\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)-\mathbb{E}_{z}(g)]\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{r}\in\textup{d}z,\underline{X}_{r}\geq 0)\textup{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\{H(r,u,x,b(r+u))-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}H(r,u,-x,b(r+u))\right\}\textup{d}r,

where a lengthy but straightforward calculation gives

\displaystyle H(r,t,x,b)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{b}[(r+t)\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)-\mathbb{E}_{z}(g)]\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}r}}\phi\left(\frac{z-x-\mu r}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}r}}\right)dz

\displaystyle=(r+t)\left[\Psi\left(\frac{b-x-\mu r}{\sigma\sqrt{r}}\right)-\Psi\left(\frac{-x-\mu r}{\sigma\sqrt{r}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\left[\frac{x}{\mu}+t+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right]e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}\left[\Psi\left(\frac{b-x+\mu r}{\sigma\sqrt{r}}\right)-\Psi\left(\frac{-x+\mu r}{\sigma\sqrt{r}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r}}{\mu}e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}\left[\phi\left(\frac{b-x+\mu r}{\sigma\sqrt{r}}\right)-\phi\left(\frac{-x+\mu r}{\sigma\sqrt{r}}\right)\right].

From formula (
35
) we can deduce that

\displaystyle V(0,x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{-x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g)W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z+V(0,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{3\sigma^{2}}{2\mu^{3}}x-\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}x^{2}+V(0,0).

Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{-}(0,0)=\frac{3\sigma^{2}}{2\mu^{3}}.

From Theorem
4.18
we have that for
u>0
and
x>0
,

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\exp(-2\mu x/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\{H(r,u,x,b(r+u))-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}H(r,u,-x,b(r+u))\right\}\textup{d}r.

Therefore, the curve
b(u)
and
V(0,0)
satisfy the equations

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\exp(-2\mu b(u)/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\{H(r,u,x,b(r+u))-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}H(r,u,-x,b(r+u))\right\}\textup{d}r,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{3\sigma^{2}}{2\mu^{3}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(0,0),

for all
u>0
, where

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\sigma^{4}}{\mu^{4}}\leq V(0,0)<0.

Note that
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(0,0)
can be estimated via
[V(h_{0},h_{0})-V(0,0)]/h_{0}
for
h_{0}
sufficiently small.

We can approximate the integrals above by Riemann sums to implement a numerical approximation of
b
and
V
. Take
u>0
and
x>0
, by the dominated convergence theorem (see equations (
52
) and (
53
)) we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\exp(-2\mu x/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T-u}\left\{H(r,u,x,b(r+u))-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}H(r,u,-x,b(r+u))\right\}\textup{d}r

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\exp(-2\mu x/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{u}^{T}\left\{H(r-u,u,x,b(r))-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}H(r-u,u,-x,b(r))\right\}\textup{d}r

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\exp(-2\mu x/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\Big\{H((i-k+1)h_{n},u,x,b(ih_{n}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}H((i-k+1)h_{n},u,-x,b(ih_{n}))\Big\}h_{n},

where for
T>0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
fixed, we define
h_{n}:=T/n
, the value
k
is an integer such that
u\in[kh_{n},(k+1)h_{n})
, and we used that for each
T-u>0
, the functions
r\mapsto H(r,u,x,b(r+u))
and
r\mapsto H(r,u,-x,b(r+u))
are Riemann integrable on
[0,T-u]
.

Hence, take
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
T>0
sufficiently large such that
h_{n}=T/n
is small. For each
k\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we define
u_{k}=kh_{n}
. Then, from the equation above and continuity of
V
, we can approximate
V(u,x)
, for any
x>0
and
u\in[u_{k},u_{k+1})
, by

\displaystyle V_{h}(u_{k},x)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\exp(-2\mu x/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}[H(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},x,b(u_{i}))-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}x}H(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},-x,b(u_{i}))]h_{n}.

(77)

In particular, taking
x=b(u_{k})
we have that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\approx V(0,0)\exp(-2\mu b(u_{k})/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}[H(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},b(u_{k}),b(u_{i}))-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}b(u_{k})}H(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},-b(u_{k}),b(u_{i}))]h_{n}.

Moreover, from the definition of the right-derivative, we have

\displaystyle 0\approx\frac{3\sigma^{2}}{2\mu^{3}}-\frac{V(h_{n},h_{n})-V(0,0)}{h_{n}}\approx\frac{3\sigma^{2}}{2\mu^{3}}-\frac{V_{h}(h_{n},h_{n})-V(0,0)}{h_{n}},

(78)

where
V_{h}
is given in (
77
). Note that
V_{h}
also depends on
V(0,0)
and on the values
\{b(t_{i}),i=k,\ldots,n-1\}
. Then, for each
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
, equation (
78
) can be interpreted as a non-linear equation that depends on
V(0,0)
and
\{b(u_{i}),i=k,\ldots,n-1\}
. We then propose the following algorithm:

1.

Take
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
T>0
sufficiently large such that
h_{n}=T/n
is small. For each
k\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,n\}
, define
u_{k}=kh_{n}
.

2.

Take a value
V_{0}\in[-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\sigma^{4}}{\mu^{4}},0)
.

3.

Let
b_{n-1}>0
be the solution to the equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=V_{0}\exp(-2\mu b_{n-1}/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+[H(u_{1},u_{n-1},b_{n-1},b_{n-1})-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}b_{n-1}}H(u_{1},u_{n-1},-b_{n-1},b_{n-1})]h_{n}.

4.

For
1\leq k\leq n-2
, let
b_{k}>0
be the solution to the equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=V_{0}\exp(-2\mu b_{k}/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}[H(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},b_{k},b_{i})-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}b_{k}}H(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},-b_{k},b_{i})]h_{n}.

Note that in this step we calculate backwards the values
\{b_{k},k=1,2,\ldots,n-2\}
.

5.

Calculate the quantity

\displaystyle R^{V_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{3\sigma^{2}}{2\mu^{3}}+\frac{V_{0}}{h_{n}}-\frac{1}{h_{n}}\exp(-2\mu h_{n}/\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{h_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}[H(u_{i},u_{1},h_{n},b_{i})-e^{-2\mu/\sigma^{2}h_{n}}H(u_{i},u_{1},-h_{n},b_{i})]h_{n}.

If the value of
R^{V_{0}}\approx 0
then stop the algorithm, otherwise, go back to step 2 with a different choice of
V_{0}
.

The sequence
\{b_{k},k=1,\ldots,n-1\}
is a numerical approximation of the sequence
\{b(t_{k}),k=1,\ldots,n-1\}
, whereas
V_{0}
is an approximation of
V(0,0)
. Note that our algorithm is a simple method to approximate the curve b and to illustrate how Theorem
4.18
can be used for that purpose. Note that better methods are needed to achieve higher precision and shorter computation time. We show in Figure
3
a numerical calculation of the optimal boundary and the value function using the method above. The case considered is when
\mu=1/2
and
\sigma=1
.

Fig 3:
Numeric calculation of the optimal boundary and value function
V
for the Brownian motion with drift case.

6.2
Brownian motion with exponential jumps example

Consider the case
p=2
and when
X
is a Brownian motion with drift and exponential jumps, i.e.,
X=\{X_{t},t\geq 0\}
with

\displaystyle X_{t}=\mu t+\sigma B_{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{t}}Y_{i},\qquad t\geq 0,

where
\sigma>0
,
\mu>0
,
B=\{B_{t},t\geq 0\}
is a standard Brownian motion,
N=\{N_{t},t\geq 0\}
is an independent Poisson process with rate
\lambda>0
and
\{Y_{i},i\geq 1\}
is a sequence of independent exponential distributed random variables with parameter
\rho>0
independent of
B
and
N
. We further assume that
\mu\rho>\lambda
so
X
drifts to infinity. The Laplace exponent is given by for
\beta\geq 0
by

\displaystyle\psi(\beta)=\mu\beta+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\beta^{2}-\frac{\lambda\beta}{\rho+\beta}.

In this case the Lévy measure is given by
\Pi(\textup{d}x)=\lambda\rho e^{\rho x}\textup{d}x
, for all
x<0
. An easy calculation leads to
\psi^{\prime}(0+)=\mu-\lambda/\rho
. Using the identity
\psi(\Phi(q))=q
, for any
q\geq 0
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(0+)

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho}{\mu\rho-\lambda},

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0+)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma^{2}\rho^{3}+2\lambda\rho}{\left[\mu\rho-\lambda\right]^{3}},

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(0+)

\displaystyle=3\frac{\rho[\sigma^{2}\rho^{2}+2\lambda]^{2}}{[\mu\rho-\lambda]^{5}}+\frac{6\lambda\rho}{[\mu\rho-\lambda]^{4}}.

It is know that (see e.g.
Kuznetsov, Kyprianou and
Rivero 2013
, on p. 101) the scale function
W
is given by

\displaystyle W(x)=\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}+\frac{e^{\zeta_{1}x}}{\psi^{\prime}(\zeta_{1})}+\frac{e^{\zeta_{2}x}}{\psi^{\prime}(\zeta_{2})}

for
x\geq 0
, where

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\rho+\mu\right)+\sqrt{\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\rho-\mu\right)^{2}+2\sigma^{2}\lambda}}{\sigma^{2}},

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\rho+\mu\right)-\sqrt{\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\rho-\mu\right)^{2}+2\sigma^{2}\lambda}}{\sigma^{2}}.

By differentiating (
24
) with respect to
q
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}(g)

\displaystyle=-\psi^{\prime}(0+)[\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)+x\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}]+\psi^{\prime}(0+)W^{*2}(x)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\frac{\sigma^{2}\rho^{2}+2\lambda}{\left[\mu\rho-\lambda\right]^{2}}-\frac{\rho}{\mu\rho-\lambda}x,&\qquad x<0,\\
\frac{\sigma^{2}\rho^{2}+2\lambda}{\left[\mu\rho-\lambda\right]^{2}}-\frac{\rho}{\mu\rho-\lambda}x+(\mu-\lambda/\rho)W^{*2}(x),&\qquad x\geq 0.\end{array}\right.

Moreover, taking
x=0
and differentiating twice (
24
) with respect to the variable
q\geq 0
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(g^{2})=\psi^{\prime}(0+)\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)=3\frac{[\sigma^{2}\rho^{2}+2\lambda]^{2}}{[\mu\rho-\lambda]^{4}}+\frac{6\lambda}{[\mu\rho-\lambda]^{3}}.

For
x<0
, the value function is then given by

\displaystyle V(0,x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{-x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g)W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z+V(0,0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{-x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0+)+\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}(-u-z)\right]\psi^{\prime}(0+)W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z+V(0,0)

\displaystyle=\left[\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)(-x)+\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}\mathbb{E}(\underline{X}_{\infty})(-x)-\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}x^{2}/2\right]+V(0,0),

where in the last equality we used that
\psi(0+)W(x)=\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{0}^{-}=\infty)=\mathbb{P}(-\underline{X}_{\infty}\leq x)
and hence
\psi^{\prime}(0+)W^{\prime}(u)
is the density function of the random variable
\underline{X}_{\infty}
. From (
8
) we know that for any
\beta\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(e^{\beta\underline{X}_{\infty}})=\psi^{\prime}(0+)\frac{\beta}{\psi(\beta)}.

Hence, by differentiating and using the fact that
\Phi^{\prime}(q)=1/\psi^{\prime}(\Phi(q))
, we can see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\underline{X}_{\infty})=\frac{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle V(0,x)

\displaystyle=-\left[\frac{3}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)x+\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}x^{2}/2\right]+V(0,0)

for any
x<0
. Next, we calculate for any
x>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,-x)}

\displaystyle V(0,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{-x}\left[-\frac{3}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)(x+y)-\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}(x+y)^{2}/2+V(0,0)\right]\lambda\rho e^{\rho y}\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=\lambda e^{-\rho x}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\left[-\frac{3}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)y-\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}y^{2}/2+V(0,0)\right]\rho e^{\rho y}\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=\lambda e^{-\rho x}\left[\frac{3\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2\rho}-\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+V(0,0)\right].

Similarly, we have that for all
u>0
and
x>b(u)
,

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(u,x+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{0<x+y<b(u)\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle=e^{-\rho x}\int_{0}^{b(u)}V(u,y)\lambda\rho e^{\rho y}\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=e^{-\rho(x-b(u))}\int_{(-b(u),0)}V(u,y+b(u))\Pi(\textup{d}y).

Then, since
G
is non decreasing in each argument, condition (
4.18
) is satisfied if and only if

\displaystyle\int_{(-b(u),0)}

\displaystyle V(u,y+b(u))\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda e^{-\rho b(u)}\left[\frac{3\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2\rho}-\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+V(0,0)\right]+G(u,b(u))\geq 0

for all
u>0
. On the other hand, for any
u,x>0
, equation (
46
) reads as

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}e^{-\rho(X_{s}-b(u+s))}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s)\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int_{(-b(u+s),0)}V(u+s,y+b(u+s))\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}s\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}\left[G(u+s,X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,-X_{s})}V(0,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-b(u+s),0)}V(u+s,y+b(u+s))\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(e^{-\rho(X_{s}-b(u+s))}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(u+s),\underline{X}_{s}\geq 0\}}\right)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\left[G(u+s,X_{s})+\int_{(-\infty,-X_{s})}V(0,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(u+s),\underline{X}_{s}\geq 0\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{V}(u+s,b(u+s))F_{2}(s,x,b(u+s))\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}F_{1}(s,u,x,b(u+s),V(0,0))\textup{d}s,

where for any
s,u,x,b>0
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u,b)

\displaystyle:=\int_{(-b,0)}V(u,y+b)\Pi(\textup{d}y),

\displaystyle F_{1}(s,u,x,b,V_{0})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left(G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+x<b,\underline{X}_{s}+x\geq 0\}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left(\lambda e^{-\rho(X_{s}+x))}\left[\frac{3\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2\rho}-\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+V_{0}\right]\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+x<b,\underline{X}_{s}+x\geq 0\}}\right),

\displaystyle F_{2}(s,x,b)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left(e^{-\rho(X_{s}+x-b)}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+x>b,\underline{X}_{s}+x\geq 0\}}\right).

In summary, we have that
V
,
b
and
V(0,0)
satisfy the equations

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)+\int_{0}^{\infty}F_{1}(s,u,x,b(u+s),V(0,0))\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{V}(u+s,b(u+s))F_{2}(s,x,b(u+s))\textup{d}s,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(b(u))+\int_{0}^{\infty}F_{1}(s,u,b(u),b(u+s),V(0,0))\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{V}(u+s,b(u+s))F_{2}(s,b(u),b(u+s))\textup{d}s,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)+\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{+}(0,0),

for all
u,x>0
.

In a similar way as in the previous section, we can approximate the integrals above by Riemann sums to implement a numerical approximation of
b
and
V
. Take
u>0
and
x>0
, by the dominated convergence theorem (see equations (
52
) and (
53
)) we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)+\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}F_{1}((i-k+1)h_{n},u,x,b(ih_{n}),V(0,0))h_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad-\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\mathcal{V}((i+1)h_{n},b((i+1)h_{n}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times F_{2}((i-k+1)h_{n},x,b(ih_{n}))h_{n},

where
h_{n}:=T/n
, for
T>0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the value
k
is an integer such that
u\in[kh_{n},(k+1)h_{n})
, and we used that for each
T-u>0
, the functions
r\mapsto F_{1}(r,u,x,b(r+u),V(0,0))
and
r\mapsto\mathcal{V}(r+u,b(r+u))F_{2}(r,x,b(r+u))
are Riemann integrable on
[0,T-u]
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
T>0
sufficiently large such that
h_{n}=T/n
is small, for each
k\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we define
u_{k}=kh_{n}
. Then, from the equation above and continuity of
V
, we can approximate
V(u,x)
, for any
x>0
and
u\in[u_{k},u_{k+1})
, by

\displaystyle V_{h}(u_{k},x)

\displaystyle=V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)+\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}F_{1}(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},x,b(u_{i}),V(0,0))h_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\mathcal{V}(u_{i+1},b(u_{i+1}))F_{2}(u_{i-k+1},x,b(u_{i}))h_{n}.

(79)

In particular, taking
x=b(u_{k})
we have that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\approx V(0,0)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(b(u_{k}))+\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}F_{1}(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},b(u_{k}),b(u_{i}),V(0,0))h_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\mathcal{V}(u_{i+1},b(u_{i+1}))F_{2}(u_{i-k+1},b(u_{k}),b(u_{i}))h_{n}.

Moreover, from the definition of the right derivative we have that

\displaystyle 0\approx\frac{3}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)-\frac{V(h_{n},h_{n})-V(0,0)}{h_{n}}\approx\frac{3}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)-\frac{V_{h}(h_{n},h_{n})-V(0,0)}{h_{n}},

(80)

where
V_{h}
is given in (
79
). Note that, for each
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
,
V_{h}(u_{k},x)
depends on
V(0,0)
and on the values
\{b(t_{i}),i=k,\ldots,n\}
. Then, equation (
80
) can be interpreted as a non-linear equation that depends on
V(0,0)
and
\{b(u_{i}),i=k,\ldots,n\}
. The functions
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
can be estimated by simulating the process
\{(X_{t},\underline{X}_{t}),t\geq 0)\}
(see e.g.
Kuznetsov et al. 2011
, Theorem 4 and Remark 3). For
V_{0}<0
,
x\geq 0
,
\mathbf{a}_{k}=(a_{i},i=k,\ldots,n)
with
a_{i}\geq 0
and
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we define the following auxiliary functions,

\displaystyle V_{h}^{0}(u_{k},x,\mathbf{a}_{k},V_{0})

\displaystyle=V_{0}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(x)+\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}F_{1}(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},x,a_{i},V_{0})h_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\mathcal{V}_{h}^{0}(u_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{i+1},V_{0})F_{2}(u_{i-k+1},x,a_{i})h_{n},

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{0}_{h}(u_{n},\mathbf{a}_{n},V_{0})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{0}_{h}(u_{k},\mathbf{a}_{k},V_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor a_{k}/h_{n}\rfloor}V_{h}^{0}(u_{k},jh_{n},\mathbf{a}_{k},V_{0})\lambda\rho e^{\rho jh_{n}}h_{n},

where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
is the floor function. We then propose the following algorithm:

1.

Take
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
T>0
sufficiently large such that
h_{n}=T/n
is small. For each
k\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,n\}
, define
u_{k}=kh_{n}
.

2.

Take a value
V_{0}\in[-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}(g^{2}),0)
.

3.

Define
b_{n}=0
and
\mathcal{V}(u_{n},b_{n})=0
. Let
b_{n-1}>0
be the solution to the equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=V_{0}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(b_{n-1})+F_{1}(u_{1},u_{n-1},b_{n-1},b_{n-1})h_{n},

subject to
V_{h}^{0}(u_{n-1},jh_{n},\mathbf{b}_{n-1},V_{0})\leq 0
for
j=1,2,\ldots,\lfloor b_{n-1}/h_{n}\rfloor
and that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{h}^{0}(u_{n-1},\mathbf{b}_{n-1},V_{0})+\lambda e^{-\rho b_{n-1}}\left[\frac{3\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2\rho}-\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+V_{0}\right]+G(u_{n-1},b_{n-1})\geq 0,

where
\mathbf{b}_{n-1}=(b_{n-1},b_{n})
.

4.

For
1\leq k\leq n-2
, let
b_{k}>0
be the solution to the equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=V_{0}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}W^{\prime}(b_{k})+\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}F_{1}(u_{i-k+1},u_{k},b_{k},b_{i},V_{0})h_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\mathcal{V}(u_{i+1},b(u_{i+1}))F_{2}(u_{i-k+1},b_{k},b_{i})h_{n},

subject to
V_{h}^{0}(u_{k},jh_{n},\mathbf{b}_{k},V_{0})\leq 0
for
j=1,2,\ldots,\lfloor b_{k}/h_{n}\rfloor
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{h}(u_{k},\mathbf{b}_{k},V_{0})+\lambda e^{-\rho b_{k}}\left[\frac{3\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2\rho}-\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+V_{0}\right]+G(u_{k},b_{k})\geq 0,

where
\mathbf{b}_{k}=(b_{i},i=k,\ldots,n)
. Note that in this step we calculate the values
b_{n-2}
,
\mathcal{V}_{h}(u_{n-2},\textbf{b}_{n-2},V_{0})
,
b_{n-3}
,
\mathcal{V}_{h}(u_{n-3}
,
\textbf{b}_{n-3},V_{0}),\ldots,b_{1}
,
\mathcal{V}_{h}(u_{1},\textbf{b}_{1},V_{0})
.

5.

Calculate the quantity

\displaystyle R^{V_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)+\frac{V_{0}}{h_{n}}-\frac{1}{h_{n}}V_{h}^{0}(u_{1},h_{n},\mathbf{b}_{1},V_{0}).

If the value of
R^{V_{0}}\approx 0
stop the algorithm, otherwise, go back to step 2 with a different choice of
V_{0}
.

The sequence
\{b_{k},k=1,\ldots,n-1\}
is a numerical approximation of the sequence
\{b(t_{k}),k=1,\ldots,n-1\}
, whereas
V_{0}
is an approximation of
V(0,0)
. Note that our algorithm is a simple method to approximate the curve b and to illustrate how Theorem
4.18
can be used for that purpose. Note that better methods are needed to achieve higher precision and shorter computation time.
We show in Figure
4
a numerical calculation of the optimal boundary and the value function using the parametrisation
\mu=3
,
\sigma=1
,
\lambda=1
and
\rho=1
. The functions
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
above were estimated using Monte Carlo simulations accordingly to the algorithm given in
Kuznetsov et al. 2011
.

Fig 4:
Numeric calculation of the optimal boundary and value function
V
for the Brownian motion with exponential jumps case.

A
Technical proofs

In this Appendix, we include the most technical proofs of the results presented in Sections
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
.

From equation (
2
) we know that

\displaystyle F(\theta,x):=\mathbb{E}_{x}(e^{-\theta\tau_{0}^{+}})=e^{\Phi(\theta)x},\qquad x\leq 0.

Then using the formula of Faà di Bruno (see for example
Spindler 2005
) we have that for any
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\theta^{n}}F(\theta,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{\Phi(\theta)x}x^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}=k,\\
k_{1}+\cdots+nk_{n}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{k_{1}!k_{2}!\cdots k_{n}!}\left(\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(\theta)}{1!}\right)^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\theta)}{2!}\right)^{k_{2}}\cdots\left(\frac{\Phi^{(n)}(\theta)}{n!}\right)^{k_{n}}.

(81)

Then evaluating at zero the above equation, using
\Phi(0)=0
and the fact that
\Phi^{(i)}(0)<\infty
for
i=1,\ldots,\lfloor p\rfloor+1
, we can find constants
A_{r},C_{r}\geq 0
such that
\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{r})\leq A_{r}+C_{r}|x|^{r}
for any
r\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor p\rfloor+1\}
. For any non integer
r<\lfloor p\rfloor+1
we can use Hölder’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{r})\leq[\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{\lfloor r\rfloor+1})]^{\frac{r}{\lfloor r\rfloor+1}}\leq(A_{\lfloor r\rfloor+1}+C_{\lfloor r\rfloor+1}|x|^{\lfloor r\rfloor+1})^{\frac{r}{\lfloor r\rfloor+1}}.

The result follows from the inequality in Lemma
2.4
. Now we show that the second inequality holds. From the strong Markov property, we get that for any
x<0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{r})\leq 2^{r}\mathbb{E}(g^{r})+2^{r}\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{r})\leq 2^{r}[\mathbb{E}(g^{r})+A_{r}]+2^{r}C_{r}|x|^{r}.

The proof is now complete.
∎

It follows from the definition of
g
that
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})=\mathbb{E}(g^{(-x)})
is non-negative and non-increasing. In order to check continuity notice that by integration by parts, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})

\displaystyle=p\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{p-1}\mathbb{P}_{x}(g>s)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=p\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{p-1}\mathbb{E}_{x}(1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(X_{s}))\textup{d}s,

where the last equality follows from (
25
). Take
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then using the equation above we have that

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})-\mathbb{E}_{x+\delta}(g^{p})|\leq p\psi^{\prime}(0+)\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{p-1}|W(X_{s}+x+\delta)-W(X_{s}+x)|\textup{d}s\right).

(82)

First, suppose that
X
is of infinite variation and thus
W
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
