. From the fact that
X
drifts to
\infty
we know that
W(\infty)=1/\psi^{\prime}(0+)
and therefore it follows that
s^{p-1}(1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(X_{s}))
is integrable with respect to the product measure
\mathbb{P}_{x}\times\lambda([0,\infty))
, where
\lambda
denotes Lebesgue measure.
We can now invoke the dominated convergence theorem to deduce that
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})
is continuous.

Next, in the case that
X
is of finite variation we have that
W
has a discontinuity at zero. However, the set
\{s\geq 0:X_{s}=x\}
is almost surely countable and thus has Lebesgue measure zero. We can again use the dominated convergence theorem to conclude that continuity also holds in this case.

We prove now the asymptotic behaviour of
\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})
. Note that when
x
tends to
-\infty
the random variable
g^{(-x)}\rightarrow\infty
. Then using Fatou’s lemma

\displaystyle\liminf_{x\rightarrow-\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})\geq\mathbb{E}(\liminf_{x\rightarrow-\infty}(g^{(-x)})^{p})=\infty.

Therefore,
\lim_{x\rightarrow-\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})=\infty
. On the other hand, note that for
x>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(g^{p}=0)=\mathbb{P}_{x}(g=0)=\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{0}^{-}=\infty)=\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(x)\xrightarrow{x\rightarrow\infty}1.

(83)

Hence we deduce that the sequence
\{(g^{(-n)})^{p}\}_{n\geq 1}
converges in probability to
0
(under the measure
\mathbb{P}
) when
n
tends to infinity. Moreover, since the sequence
\{\mathbb{E}((g^{-n})^{p})\}_{n\geq 1}
is a non-increasing sequence of positive numbers we get that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}((g^{-n})^{p})\leq\mathbb{E}(g^{p})<\infty,

where the last inequality holds due to Lemma
2.1
and by assumption. Then
\{(g^{(-n)})^{p}\}_{n\geq 1}
is an uniformly integrable family of random variables. Then, together with the convergence in probability, allows us to conclude that
\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})\rightarrow 0
when
x\rightarrow\infty
as claimed.
∎

First, notice that due to the spatial homogeneity of Lévy processes and that
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
is non-increasing, it suffices to prove the assertion for
x\leq 0
. Using Fubini’s theorem we have that for all
x\leq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,\infty)}\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{s}\in\textup{d}z).

Since
X
drifts to infinity we can use the density for the
0
-potential measure of
X
without killing (see equation (
12
)) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})\left[\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}-W(x-z)\right]\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})\left[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(x-z)\right]\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}\int_{x}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}z.

(84)

Then, we prove that the above two integrals are finite for all
x\leq 0
. From the fact that
z\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
and
W
is continuous on
(0,\infty)
we can assume without of loss of generality that
x=0
.

First, we show that the first integral on the right-hand side of (
84
) is finite. From Lemma
2.2
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})\left[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)\right]\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\mathbb{E}(-\underline{X}_{\infty})[\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})+A_{p-1}]+\frac{2^{p-1}}{p}C_{p-1}\mathbb{E}((-\underline{X}_{\infty})^{p}),

where
A_{p-1}
and
C_{p-1}
are non-negative constants. In the equality above we relied on the fact that
z\mapsto\psi(0+)W(z)
corresponds to the distribution function of the random variable
-\underline{X}_{\infty}
. We conclude from Lemma
2.1
that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(g^{p-1})\left[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)\right]\textup{d}z<\infty.

Now we proceed to check the finiteness of the second integral in (
84
) when
x=0
. Using the strong Markov property we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}((\tau_{0}^{-})^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})\textup{d}z+2^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}(\mathbb{E}_{X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}}(g^{p-1})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}((\tau_{0}^{-})^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})\textup{d}z+2^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}(\mathbb{E}_{\underline{X}_{\infty}}(g^{p-1})\mathbb{I}_{\{\underline{X}_{\infty}<0\}})\textup{d}z,

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
\underline{X}_{\infty}\leq X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}
and that
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
is a non-increasing function. Using Fubini’s theorem we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}(\mathbb{E}_{\underline{X}_{\infty}}(g^{p-1})\mathbb{I}_{\{\underline{X}_{\infty}<0\}})\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\mathbb{E}_{y}(g^{p-1})\mathbb{P}_{z}(\underline{X}_{\infty}\in\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,0)}\mathbb{E}_{y}(g^{p-1})\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{z}(\underline{X}_{\infty}\in\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-y}(g^{p-1})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(y)]\textup{d}y

\displaystyle<\infty.

It thus only remains to show that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}((\tau_{0}^{-})^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})\textup{d}z<\infty.

For this, define the function
F_{1}(q):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}(e^{-q\tau_{0}^{-}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})\textup{d}z
. Differentiating equation (
8
) with respect to
\beta
and evaluating at zero we obtain that

\displaystyle F_{1}(q)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(-\underline{X}_{\mathbf{e}_{q}}>z)\textup{d}z=\mathbb{E}(-\underline{X}_{\mathbf{e}_{q}})=\frac{1}{\Phi(q)}-\frac{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}{q},

where
\mathbf{e}_{q}
is an independent exponential random variable with parameter
q>0
. On the other hand, define the function
F_{2}(q)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}(e^{-q\tau_{0}^{+}})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z
. Using the expression for the Laplace transform of
\tau_{0}^{+}
in (
2
) and the definition of
W
, we have that

\displaystyle F_{2}(q)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\Phi(q)z}[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z=\frac{1}{\Phi(q)}-\frac{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}{q}=F_{1}(q).

The fact that
F_{2}=F_{1}
implies that, when
\alpha
is a natural number, we can take derivatives of order
\alpha
(with the help of the dominated convergence theorem) at
q=0
and conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{z}((\tau_{0}^{-})^{\alpha}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})\textup{d}z<\infty

if and only if

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{\alpha})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z<\infty.

Furthermore, if
\alpha=k+\lambda
, with
k
a positive integer and
0<\lambda<1
, we can draw the same conclusion using the Marchaud derivative (see e.g.
Laue 1980
). Using Lemma
2.2
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-z}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{p-1})[1-\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(z)]\textup{d}z<\infty.

and the proof is complete.
∎

Let
x\leq 0
and take
\delta>0
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0,x}((\tau_{D})^{p})=\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,0})^{p})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,0})^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{g+\delta<\tau_{b}^{g,0}\}})+\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{g+\delta>\tau_{b}^{g,0}\}}).

Note that on the event
\{g+\delta<\tau_{b}^{g,0}\}
we have

\displaystyle\tau_{b}^{g,0}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t>g+\delta:X_{t}\geq b(U_{t})\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t>0:X_{t+g+\delta}\geq b(t+\delta)\}+g+\delta

\displaystyle\leq\inf\{t>0:X_{t+g+\delta}\geq b(\delta)\}+g+\delta,

where the second equality follows from the fact that after
g
, the process
X
never goes back below zero, and the last inequality holds since
b
is non-increasing. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,0})^{p})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}((\inf\{t>0:X_{t+g+\delta}\geq b(\delta)\}+g+\delta)^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{g+\delta<\tau_{b}^{g,0}\}})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{g+\delta>\tau_{b}^{g,0}\}})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}_{x}((\inf\{t>0:X_{t+g+\delta}\geq b(\delta)\})^{p})+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p})

\displaystyle=2^{p}\mathbb{E}((\inf\{t>0:X_{t+g^{(-x)}+\delta}+x\geq b(\delta)\})^{p})+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p}),

where
g^{(-x)}=\sup\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq-x\}
. From
Bertoin 1998
(see Corollary VII.4.19) we know that the law of the process
\{X_{t+g^{(-x)}}-(-x),t\geq 0\}
is the same as that of
\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}
, where
\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}=\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{0}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{x}
, for
x\geq 0
, corresponds to the law of
X
starting at
x
conditioned to stay positive. Moreover, from Proposition VII.3.14 in
Bertoin 1998
, we know that the canonical process
X
is a Feller process for the family
\{\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{x},x\geq 0\}
. Hence, using the Markov property of
X
under
\{\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{x},x\geq 0\}
and equation VII.3.(6) in
Bertoin 1998
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,0})^{p})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}((\inf\{t>0:X_{t+g^{(-x)}+\delta}+x\geq b(\delta)\})^{p})+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p})

\displaystyle=2^{p}\mathbb{E}^{\uparrow}((\inf\{t>0:X_{t+\delta}\geq b(\delta)\})^{p})+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}^{\uparrow}(\mathbb{E}^{\uparrow}_{X_{\delta}}[(\tau_{b(\delta)}^{+})^{p}])+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p})

\displaystyle=2^{p}\mathbb{E}^{\uparrow}\left(\frac{W(b(\delta))}{W(X_{\delta})}\mathbb{E}_{X_{\delta}}[(\tau_{b(\delta)}^{+})^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}>\tau_{b(\delta)}^{+}\}}]\right)+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}[(\tau_{b(\delta)}^{+})^{p}]\mathbb{E}^{\uparrow}\left(\frac{W(b(\delta))}{W(X_{\delta})}\right)+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p})

\displaystyle=2^{p}\mathbb{E}[(\tau_{b(\delta)}^{+})^{p}]\int_{(0,\infty)}\frac{W(b(\delta))}{W(z)}\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}(X_{\delta}\in\textup{d}z)+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p}),

where the third inequality follows from the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{x}[(\tau_{a}^{+})^{p}]\leq\mathbb{E}[(\tau_{a}^{+})^{p}]
for all
0\leq x\leq a
and
X_{\delta}>0
under
\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}
. Thus, using that
\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}(X_{\delta}\in\textup{d}z)=[zW(z)/\delta]\mathbb{P}(X_{\delta}\in\textup{d}z)
(see e.g. Corollary VII.3.16 in
Bertoin 1998
) we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,0})^{p})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}[(\tau_{b(\delta)}^{+})^{p}]\int_{(0,\infty)}\frac{W(b(\delta))}{W(z)}\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}(X_{\delta}\in\textup{d}z)+(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g+\delta)^{p})

\displaystyle=2^{p}\mathbb{E}[(\tau_{b(\delta)}^{+})^{p}]\frac{W(b(\delta))}{\delta}\mathbb{E}(X_{\delta}^{+})+2^{p}(2^{p}+1)\delta^{p}+2^{p}(2^{p}+1)\mathbb{E}_{x}((g)^{p}),

(85)

where
X_{\delta}^{+}
is the positive part of
X_{\delta}
. Thus from Lemma
2.1
we have that
\mathbb{E}_{0,x}((\tau_{D})^{p})=\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,0})^{p})
is finite for
x\leq 0
.

Next, we show that
\mathbb{E}_{u,x}((\tau_{D})^{p})<\infty
when
u,x>0
. From the Markov property of Lévy processes, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}((\tau_{D})^{p})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{u,0})^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u,0}<\sigma_{0}^{-}\}})+\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,0})^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u,0}>\sigma_{0}^{-}\}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b(u)}^{+})^{p})+2^{p}\mathbb{E}_{x}((\sigma_{0}^{-})^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})+2^{p}\mathbb{E}_{x}(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\infty\}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}}[(\tau_{b}^{g,0})^{p}]).

Using (
85
), the inequality
|X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}|\leq|\underline{X}_{\infty}|
under the event
\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\infty\}
and Lemmas
2.1
and
2.2
we deduce that
\mathbb{E}_{u,x}((\tau_{D})^{p})<\infty
and the proof is complete.
∎

Using that
b
is a right-continuous and a non-decreasing function, and that
X
creeps upwards, it can be shown that for any
u\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\tau_{b}^{u,x+h}=\tau_{b}^{u,x}\text{ }\text{a.s.}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\lim_{(h_{1},h_{2})\rightarrow(0,0)+}\tau_{b}^{u+h_{1},x+h_{2}}=\tau_{b}^{u,x}\text{ }\text{a.s.}

These facts will be useful in the proof of the continuity of the function
V
.

First, we show that the function
u\mapsto V(u,x)
is continuous, for all
x>0
fixed. Take
u_{1},u_{2}>0
and
x>0
, then since the stopping time
\tau^{*}_{(u_{1},x)}:=\tau_{b}^{u_{1},x}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},x}<\sigma_{-x}^{-}\}}+\tau_{b}^{g,x}\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},x}\geq\sigma_{-x}^{-}\}}
is optimal for
V(u_{1},x)
(under
\mathbb{P}
) we have that

\displaystyle V(u_{1},x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\wedge\tau_{b}^{u_{1},x}}G(u_{1}+s,X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},x}\geq\sigma_{-x}^{-}\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x}^{-}}+x)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}}G(u_{1}+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s+\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}\geq\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})\right).

On the other hand, from (
31
) we get

\displaystyle V(u_{2},x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau^{*}_{(u_{1},x)}}\left\{G(u_{2}+s,X_{s}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}>s\}}+G(U_{s}^{(-x)},X_{s}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq s\}}\right\}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}<\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}}\left\{G(u_{2}+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}>s\}}+G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}\leq s\}}\right\}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}\geq\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{g,0}}\left\{G(u_{2}+s,X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}>s\}}+G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}\leq s\}}\right\}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(u_{2}+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}\geq\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}\int_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,0}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(u_{2}+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}\geq\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})\right),

where in the first equality we used the definition of
\tau^{*}_{(u_{1},x)}
given above, in the second equality that
\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}\leq\tau_{b}^{g,0}
and the last equality follows from the strong Markov property applied at time
\sigma_{0}^{-}
. Hence, we have that for any
x>0
fixed and
u_{1},u_{2}>0
,

\displaystyle|V(u_{2},x)-V(u_{1},x)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{b}^{u_{1},0}}|G(u_{2}+s,X_{s})-G(u_{1}+s,X_{s})|\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b(u_{1})}^{+}}|G(u_{2}+s,X_{s})-G(u_{1}+s,X_{s})|\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b(u_{1})}^{+}}|(u_{2}+s)^{p-1}-(u_{1}+s)^{p-1}|\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}|\mathbb{E}((\tau_{b(u_{1})}^{+}+u_{2})^{p})-\mathbb{E}((\tau_{b(u_{1})}^{+}+u_{1})^{p})-[u_{2}^{p}-u_{1}^{p}]|,

where
\tau_{b(u_{1})}^{+}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}>b(u_{1})\}
. Thus, letting
u_{2}\mapsto u_{1}
, together with the dominated convergence theorem and the fact that
\mathbb{E}((\tau_{a}^{+}+u)^{p})<\infty
, for all
u,a\geq 0
, we get that
u\mapsto V(u,x)
is continuous uniformly over all
x>0
.

Next, we show that
x\mapsto V(u,x)
is continuous. From equation (
35
) we easily deduce that
x\mapsto V(0,x)
is a continuous function on
(-\infty,0]
. Then, suppose that
u>0
and
x>0
. Recall from equation (
40
) that we can write

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\wedge\tau_{b}^{u,x}}G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x}^{-}}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq\tau_{b}^{u,x}\}}).

Note that for all
s<\tau_{b}^{u,x}\wedge\sigma_{-x}^{-}
, it holds that
0<X_{s}+x\leq b(u+s)\leq b(u)
, and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
(see equation (
36
)),
V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x}^{-}}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq\tau_{b}^{u,x}\}}\geq V(0,\underline{X}_{\infty}+x)\geq-A^{\prime}_{p-1}-C^{\prime}_{p-1}|\underline{X}_{\infty}+x|^{p}+V(0,0)
, where the last expression is integrable from Lemma
2.1
. Moreover, it can be shown that
\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\sigma_{x+h}^{-}=\sigma_{x}^{-}
a.s. and that
\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\tau_{b}^{u,x+h}=\tau_{b}^{u,x}
a.s., for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, by the dominated convergence theorem, the fact that
V
is continuous on
(-\infty,0]
and
x\mapsto G(u,x)
is continuous on
(0,\infty)
we conclude that, for each
u>0
, the mapping
x\mapsto V(u,x)
is continuous on
(0,\infty)
. Note that when
X
is of infinite variation,
\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\sigma_{-h}^{-}=\tau_{0}^{-}=0
a.s. and the previous argument also tells us that for all
u>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\downarrow 0}V(u,h)=V(0,0).

Note that the limit above implies that
\lim_{(u,x)\rightarrow(0,0)^{+}}V(u,x)=V(0,0)
in the infinite variation case. Then we proceed to prove that this also holds when
X
is of finite variation. In this case we know that
\tau_{0}^{-}>0
and then, due to the strong Markov property,

\displaystyle V(0,0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{0,0}\wedge\tau_{0}^{-}}G(s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{0,0}\}}V(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}})),

where
\tau_{b}^{0,0}=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}\geq b(s)\}
. Taking limits in (
40
), we have from the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\lim_{(u,x)\rightarrow(0,0)^{+}}V(u,x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{(u,x)\rightarrow(0,0)^{+}}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\wedge\tau_{b}^{u,x}}G(u+s,X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{(u,x)\rightarrow(0,0)^{+}}\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\sigma_{-x}^{-}}+x)\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{-x}^{-}\leq\tau_{b}^{u,x}\}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{b}^{0,0}}G(s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{0,0}\}}V(0,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}))

\displaystyle=V(0,0),

where we used that
\lim_{x\downarrow 0}\sigma_{-x}^{-}=\tau_{0}^{-}
and
\lim_{(u,x)\rightarrow(0,0)+}\tau_{b}^{u,x}=\tau_{b}^{0,0}
a.s. Therefore,
V
is continuous on the set
E
.
∎

Before proving Lemma
4.17
we first consider a technical lemma involving the derivative of the potential measure killed on exiting
[0,a]
. More specifically, for fixed
a>0
,
x\in(0,a)
and
r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
define the measure

\displaystyle U_{r}(a,x,\textup{d}y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{r}\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{t}\in\textup{d}y,t<\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{a}^{+})\textup{d}t.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
such that
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|x|^{q}\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
. Fix
a>0
and
0\leq x\leq a
. We have that for all
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,q\}
, the measure
U_{r}(a,x,\textup{d}y)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. It has a density
u_{r}(a,x,y)
given by

\displaystyle u_{r}(a,x,y)=\lim_{q\downarrow 0}(-1)^{r}\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial q^{r}}\left[\frac{W^{(q)}(x)W^{(q)}(a-y)}{W^{(a)}(a)}-W^{(q)}(x-y)\right],

for
y\in(0,a]
. Moreover, for a fixed
a>0
, the functions
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{a}^{+})^{r}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{a}^{+}\}})
and
x\mapsto u_{r}(a,x,y)
have finite left derivatives on
(0,a]
for all
y\in(0,a)
and
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,q\}
.

Let
a>0
and
x\in(0,a)
. First, we show that for all
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,q\}
, the measure
U_{r}(a,x,\textup{d}y)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Take any measurable set
A\subset(0,a)
, thus by Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\int_{A}U_{r}(a,x,\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{r}\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{t}\in A,t<\sigma_{0}^{-}\wedge\tau_{a}^{+})\textup{d}t

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{a}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}t^{r}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{t}\in A\}}\textup{d}t\right).

From Lemma
2.1
we know that
\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{a}^{+})^{r})<\infty
for all
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,q\}
. Then, by dominated convergence theorem we have that

\displaystyle\int_{A}U_{r}(a,x,\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{q\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{a}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}t^{r}e^{-qt}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{t}\in A\}}\textup{d}t\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\lim_{q\downarrow 0}(-1)^{r}\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial q^{r}}\left[\frac{W^{(q)}(x)W^{(q)}(a-y)}{W^{(a)}(a)}-W^{(q)}(x-y)\right]\textup{d}y,

where the last equality follows from (
10
). From the convolution representation of
W^{(q)}
(see equation (
3
)), the derivatives in the last equation above exist and indeed
u_{r}(a,x,y)
is a density of
U_{r}(a,x,\textup{d}y)
for all
y\in(0,a)
.
Now we proceed to show the differentiation statements. Note that from equations (
2
) and (
4
) we have that

\displaystyle f_{x}(q):=\mathbb{E}_{x}(e^{-q\tau_{a}^{+}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{a}^{+}\}})=e^{\Phi(q)(x-a)}-\frac{W^{(q)}(x)}{W^{(q)}(a)},

for any
x\in(0,a)
. Hence, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{a}^{+})^{r}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{a}^{+}\}})=\lim_{q\downarrow 0}(-1)^{r}\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial q^{r}}\left[e^{\Phi(q)(x-a)}-\frac{W^{(q)}(x)}{W^{(q)}(a)}\right]

for any
x\in(0,a)
. From (
81
) we know that the first term above is differentiable with respect to the variable
x
(note that is also possible to calculate directly the derivatives of
\Phi(q)
by using the identity
\psi(\Phi(q))=q
and the chain rule). Moreover, from (
3
) we can see that for any
q\geq 0
,
x\geq 0
and
r\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{q\downarrow 0}\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial q^{r}}W^{(q)}(x)=r!W^{*(r+1)}(x).

Since
W
is
C^{1}((0,\infty))
and has left and right derivatives at zero (see discussion above equation
3
), we conclude that
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{a}^{+})^{r}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{a}^{+}\}})
has finite left derivatives on
(0,a]
, for any
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,q\}
. A similar argument works for the function
x\mapsto u_{r}(a,x,y)
.
∎Before we prove Lemma
4.17
we show that
V(0,x)
is smooth on
(-\infty,0)
. This auxiliary result will also be useful later for the proof of Lemma
5.5
.

Lemma A.2
.

We have that the function
V(0,x)
is continuously differentiable on
(-\infty,0)
and has left derivative at
0
. Moreover, we have that
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x)
is non-increasing and non-negative on
(-\infty,0)
, and for any
x<0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x-u}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\leq\alpha_{p-1}+\gamma_{p-1}|x|^{p-1},

(86)

where
\alpha_{p-1}
and
\gamma_{p-1}
are non-negative constants.

We start showing that the inequality in (
86
) holds. Indeed, using Lemma
2.2
and the fact that
\mathbb{P}(-\underline{X}_{\infty}\in\textup{d}u)=\psi^{\prime}(0+)W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u
, we get that for all
x<0
and
u\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x-u}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}(2^{p-1}[\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})+A_{p-1}]+2^{p-1}C_{p-1}|x-u|^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{p-1}[\mathbb{E}(g^{p-1})+A_{p-1}]+4^{p-1}C_{p-1}\mathbb{E}((-\underline{X}_{\infty})^{p-1})}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}+\frac{4^{p-1}C_{p-1}}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)}|x|^{p-1},

where the second inequality follows by using Lemma
2.4
. Therefore, we conclude that the inequality in (
86
) holds by noticing that
\mathbb{E}((-\underline{X}_{\infty})^{p-1})<\infty
(see Lemma
2.1
). Next, we can easily deduce from the continuity of
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
(see Lemma
2.3
) and the dominated convergence theorem that the mapping
x\mapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x-u}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u
is continuous on
(-\infty,0]
. Thus, from (
35
) we deduce that for any
x<0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x-u}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u.

Therefore, we conclude that
x\mapsto V(0,x)
is continuously differentiable on
(-\infty,0)
and has left derivative at zero as claimed. Lastly, since
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
is non-increasing and non-negative (see Lemma
2.3
) and
W^{\prime}(u)>0
for all
u>0
, we deduce that
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x)
is a non-increasing and non-negative function. The proof is now complete.
∎

We are now ready to prove that the partial derivatives of
V
at
(u,b(u))
exist and are equal to zero.

We first show that for all
u>0
such that
b(u)>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u}V(u,b(u))=0.

From the proof of Lemma
4.10
we know that for any
h>0
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{V(u,b(u))-V(u-h,b(u))}{h}\leq\mathbb{E}_{b(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b(u-h)}^{+}}\frac{[(u+s)^{p-1}-(u-h+s)^{p-1}]}{h}\textup{d}s\right).

The result then follows by taking
h\downarrow 0
, from the fact that the function
u\mapsto u^{p}
is differentiable on
[0,\infty)
, the dominated convergence theorem and since
b
is continuous.

Next, we proceed to show that the smooth fit condition on the spatial argument holds for
u<u_{b}
, that is,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(u,b(u))=0.

Let
x>0
,
u>0
and
0<\varepsilon<1
such that
x-\varepsilon>0
and
b(u)>0
. From equation (
40
) we know that

\displaystyle V(u,x-\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{u,x-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon-x}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s}+x-\varepsilon)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon-x}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,x-\varepsilon}\}}\int_{\sigma_{\varepsilon-x}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,x-\varepsilon}}G(U_{s}^{(\varepsilon-x)},X_{s}+x-\varepsilon)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s}-\varepsilon)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\int_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}}G(U_{s}^{(\varepsilon)},X_{s}-\varepsilon)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s}-\varepsilon)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\int_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(U_{s}^{(\varepsilon)},X_{s}-\varepsilon)\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\int_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}}G(U_{s}^{(\varepsilon)},X_{s}-\varepsilon)\textup{d}s\right),

where in the last inequality we used that
\sigma_{\varepsilon}<\sigma_{0}^{-}
under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{x}
. On the other hand, define the stopping time
\tau_{*}:=\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}>\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}+\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}
. From equation (
31
) we have that

\displaystyle V(u,x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{*}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{*}\}}\int_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}^{\tau_{*}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\int_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}G(u+s,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\int_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right),

where we again used that
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}\leq\sigma_{0}^{-}
. Hence, for any
u>0
,
0<x\leq b(u)
and
0<\varepsilon<1
such that
x-\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{V(u,x)-V(u,x-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\leq R_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x)+R_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x)+R_{3}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x),

where

\displaystyle R_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}[G(u+s,X_{s})-G(u+s,X_{s}-\varepsilon)]\textup{d}s\right),

\displaystyle R_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\int_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}[G(u+s,X_{s})-G(U_{s}^{(\varepsilon)},X_{s}-\varepsilon)]\textup{d}s\right),

\displaystyle R_{3}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\int_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}}[G(U_{s},X_{s})-G(U_{s}^{(\varepsilon)},X_{s}-\varepsilon)]\textup{d}s\right).

We show that
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{i}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,b(u))=0
for
i=1,2,3
. From the fact that
b
is non-increasing we have that
\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\leq\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}
. Then, for all
u\in(0,u_{b})
we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq R_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,b(u))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{b(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}(u+s)^{p-1}\psi^{\prime}(0+)[W(X_{s})-W(X_{s}-\varepsilon)]\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{b(u)-\varepsilon}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b(u)}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}[\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}+\varepsilon}(g^{p-1})-\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})]\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{b(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}(u+s)^{p-1}\psi^{\prime}(0+)[W(X_{s})-W(X_{s}-\varepsilon)]\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{(0,b(u))}[\mathbb{E}_{z+\varepsilon}(g^{p-1})-\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})]\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{b(u)-\varepsilon}(X_{s}\in\textup{d}z,t<\tau_{b(u)}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})\textup{d}s.

Using the density of the
0
-potential measure of
X
exiting the interval
[0,b(u)]
given in equation (
10
), we obtain that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq R_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,b(u))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{b(u)}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}(u+s)^{p-1}\psi^{\prime}(0+)\frac{W(X_{s})-W(X_{s}-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{0}^{b(u)-\varepsilon}[\mathbb{E}_{z+\varepsilon}(g^{p-1})-\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\frac{W(b(u)-\varepsilon)W(b(u)-z)}{W(b(u))}-W(b(u)-\varepsilon-z)\right]\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{b(u)-\varepsilon}^{b(u)}[\mathbb{E}_{z+\varepsilon}(g^{p-1})-\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})]\left[\frac{W(b(u)-\varepsilon)W(b(u)-z)}{W(b(u))}\right]\textup{d}z.

Note that for all
s<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}
, we have
X_{s}\in(\varepsilon,b(u)+\varepsilon)
. Then, using the fact that
W\in C^{1}((0,\infty)
), the function
z\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{z}(g^{p-1})
is continuous,
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}=\tau_{b(u)}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}=0
a.s., under
\mathbb{P}_{b(u)}
, and the dominated convergence theorem, we conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,b(u))=0.

Now we show that
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,b(u))=0
. Take
0<x\leq b(u)
. Then, using the inequality
G(u,x)\leq u^{p-1}
, the fact that for
s<\sigma_{0}^{-}
,
X_{s}>0
(then
-\mathbb{E}_{-1}(g^{p-1})=G(0,-1)\leq G(U_{s}^{(\varepsilon)},X_{s}-\varepsilon)
) and the strong Markov property at time
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}
, we get that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq R_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}[\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}][(u+\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{-1}(g^{p-1})]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\}}f(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-},X_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}})\right),

where
f
is given for all
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle f(t,x):=[2^{p-1}(u+t)^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{-1}(g^{p-1})]\mathbb{E}_{x}(\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})+2^{p-1}\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})^{p})<\infty,

where the last inequality follows due to Lemma
4.7
. Note that
\mathbb{E}_{x}(\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})=\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})^{p})=0
for all
x\leq 0
. Thus, from (
85
) there exists
M>0
such that

\displaystyle\max\{\mathbb{E}_{x}(\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}),\mathbb{E}_{x}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})^{p})\}\leq M

for all
x\leq\varepsilon
. Hence, from the compensation formula for Poisson random measures, we get that

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle R_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}),\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})^{p})\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\}}[2^{p-1}(u+\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+})^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{-1}(g^{p-1})+2^{p-1}]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+M\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\}}[2^{p-1}(u+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-})^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{-1}(g^{p-1})+2^{p-1}]\mathbb{I}_{\{0<X_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}<\varepsilon\}}\right)

\displaystyle=\max\{\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}),\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})^{p})\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\}}[2^{p-1}(u+\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+})^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{-1}(g^{p-1})+2^{p-1}]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+M\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x-\varepsilon}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\tau_{b(u)}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}}\int_{(-\infty,0)}[2^{p-1}(u+t)^{p-1}-G(0,-1)+2^{p-1}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{I}_{\{-\varepsilon<X_{t}+y<0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}t\bigg)

\displaystyle=\max\{\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}),\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})^{p})\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\}}[2^{p-1}(u+\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+})^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{-1}(g^{p-1})+2^{p-1}]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{b(u)}\int_{(-\varepsilon-z,-z)}\frac{M}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\infty}[2^{p-1}(u+t)^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{-1}(g^{p-1})+2^{p-1}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{P}_{x-\varepsilon}(X_{t}\in\textup{d}z,t<\tau_{b(u)}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})\textup{d}t\Pi(\textup{d}y).

Letting
x=b(u)
and tending
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
we get from Lemma
A.1
that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,b(u))=0.

Lastly, using the Markov property at time
\sigma_{0}^{-}
and the fact that
\tau_{b}^{g,0}\leq\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}
, we get that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq R_{3}^{(\varepsilon)}(u,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}}[G(U_{s},X_{s})-G(U_{s}^{(\varepsilon)},X_{s}-\varepsilon)]\textup{d}s\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}[V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})-V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}-\varepsilon)]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}}\left[\int_{\tau_{b}^{g,0}}^{\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}}G(U_{s},X_{s})\textup{d}s\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}[V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})-V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}-\varepsilon)]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}}\left([\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}-\tau_{b}^{g,0}](\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p-1}\right)\right),

where we used the fact that
G(U_{s},X_{s})\leq s^{p-1}\leq(\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p-1}
for all
s\in[\tau_{b}^{g,0},\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}]
.
Thus, since
|X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}|\leq|\underline{X}_{\infty}|
, under the event
\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\infty\}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{x}((-\underline{X}_{\infty})^{p-1})<\infty
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we deduce from Lemma
A.2
that the mapping
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\infty\}})
is locally bounded. Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem and the right continuity of
b
, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}[V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})-V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}-\varepsilon)]\right)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,0}\}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}})\right).

In particular, when
x=b(u)
, we have that equation above is equal to zero. On the other hand, by conditioning on
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}
we obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}}\left([\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}-\tau_{b}^{g,0}](\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}f_{2}(\varepsilon,X_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}})\right),

where

\displaystyle 0\leq f_{2}(\varepsilon,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}\}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}}\left([\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}-\tau_{b}^{g,0}](\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p-1}\right)\right).

We show that
f_{2}
is a finite function. Conditioning with respect to
\tau_{0}^{+}
and the strong Markov property of Lévy processes we have for all
y\leq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{y}

\displaystyle\left([\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon}-\tau_{b}^{g,0}](\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p})+2^{p}\mathbb{E}_{y}((\tau_{0}^{+})^{p})\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p})+2^{p}A_{p}+2^{p}C_{p}|y|^{p}.

where the last inequality follows from Lemma
2.2
. Hence, since
|X_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}|\leq|\underline{X}_{\infty}|
on the event
\{\sigma_{0}^{-}<\infty\}
, we have that

\displaystyle f_{2}(\varepsilon,x)\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}2^{p}\mathbb{E}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p})+2^{p}A_{p}+2^{p}C_{p}\mathbb{E}_{x}(|\underline{X}_{\infty}|^{p}),&\text{ for }x>0,\\
2^{p}\mathbb{E}((\tau_{b}^{g,-\varepsilon})^{p})+2^{p}A_{p}+2^{p}C_{p}|x|^{p},&\text{ for }x\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

From Lemmas
2.1
and
4.7
we conclude that
f_{2}(\varepsilon,x)
is a finite function. Moreover, from the fact that
b
is continuous and
x\mapsto U_{t}^{(x)}
is right continuous, we can show that
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\tau_{b}^{g,\varepsilon}=\tau_{b}^{g,0}
a.s., and then, by the dominated convergence theorem,
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}f_{2}(\varepsilon,x)=0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, using the compensation formula for Poisson random measures, we get that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}f_{2}(\varepsilon,X_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+}\}}f_{2}(\varepsilon,X_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle=f_{2}(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+},X_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}}=\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}\int_{(-\infty,0)}f_{2}(\varepsilon,X_{t-}+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{\overline{X}_{t-}<b(u)+\varepsilon\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{\underline{X}_{t-}>\varepsilon\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{t-}+y\leq\varepsilon\}}N(\textup{d}t,\textup{d}y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq f_{2}(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b(u)+\varepsilon}^{+})}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x-\varepsilon}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}f_{2}(\varepsilon,X_{t}+\varepsilon+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{t<\tau_{b(u)}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{t}+y\leq 0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}t\right).

From the
0
-potential density of the process killed on exiting
[0,b(u)]
(see equation (
10
)) and from equation (
4
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{x}

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}f_{2}(\varepsilon,X_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle\leq f_{2}(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\frac{W(b(u))-W(x-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon W(b(u))}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{(0,b(u))}\int_{(-\infty,0)}f_{2}(\varepsilon,z+\varepsilon+y)\mathbb{I}_{\{z+y\leq 0\}}\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x-\varepsilon}(X_{t}\in\textup{d}z,t<\tau_{b(u)}^{+}\wedge\sigma_{0}^{-})\textup{d}t

\displaystyle=f_{2}(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\frac{W(b(u))-W(x-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon W(b(u))}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{(x-\varepsilon)\vee 0}\left[\frac{W(x-\varepsilon)W(b(u)-z)}{W(b(u))}-W(x-\varepsilon-z)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\int_{(-\infty,-z)}f_{2}(\varepsilon,z+\varepsilon+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{(x-\varepsilon)\vee 0}^{b(u)}\frac{W(x-\varepsilon)W(b(u)-z)}{W(b(u))}\int_{(-\infty,-z)}f_{2}(\varepsilon,z+\varepsilon+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z.

Note that since
\Pi
is finite on sets of the form
(-\infty,-\delta)
, for all
\delta>0
, Lemma
2.1
and equation (
A
), we have that the integrals above with respect to
\Pi
are finite and bounded. Hence, taking
x=b(u)
and from the dominated convergence theorem we conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{b(u)}

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}<\tau_{b}^{u,-\varepsilon}\}}g(\varepsilon,X_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-}})\right)\leq 0.

Therefore, we also have that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{3}{(\varepsilon)}(u,b(u))=0.

The conclusion of the lemma holds.
∎

Let
(u,x)\in E
, we first show that (
52
) is satisfied. Indeed, using that
|G(u,x)|<u^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
and that
U_{s}\leq u+s
(under
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|G(U_{s},X_{s})|\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(u+s)^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right).

(89)

From Lemma
4.1
we know that the second integral above is finite. Now we check that the first integral above is also finite. Consider
\delta>0
and let
g^{(b(\delta))}
be the last time
X
is below the level
b(\delta)
. Then, we have that
g^{(b(\delta))}\geq g
and
X_{s+g^{(b(\delta))}+\delta}\geq b(\delta)\geq b(U_{s})
for all
s\geq 0
. Hence, since
b
is non-increasing and
p>1
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(u+s)^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{g^{(b(\delta))}+\delta}(u+s)^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq[\mathbb{E}_{x}((g^{(b(\delta))}+\delta+u)^{p})-u^{p}]

(90)

\displaystyle<\infty,

where the last expectation is finite by Lemma
2.1
. Therefore, we conclude that (
52
) holds. Moreover, assume that
X
is of finite variation with
\Pi(-\infty,0)<\infty
and take
u>u_{b}
. Then, we have that
b(u+s)=0
for all
s\geq 0
, and for any
\delta>0
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{\sigma_{0}^{-}}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|G(U_{s},X_{s})|\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}((g^{(b(\delta))}+\delta)^{p})+\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right),

where the last inequality follows from equations (
89
) and (
90
). From the fact that
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})
is non increasing, that
\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p})=0
(see Lemma
2.3
) and the dominated convergence theorem we then deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}((g^{(b(\delta))}+\delta)^{p})+\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\delta

for any
\delta>0
. Hence, by letting
\delta\downarrow 0
, we conclude that when
X
is of finite variation with finite activity,

\displaystyle\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}G(U_{s},X_{s})\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}<b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)=0.

Next, we prove that (
53
) also holds. Take
(u,x)\in E
and let
N>0
be any positive number. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0)}

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(u)\}}

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u\leq N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(u)\}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(N)\}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{b(N)\geq x>b(u)\}}.

(91)

Using that
\int_{(-\infty,0)}V(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)+G(u,x)\geq 0
for all
u>0
and
x>b(u)
(see Lemma
4.14
), that
G(u,x)\leq u^{p-1}
for all
(u,x)\in E
and that
b
is non increasing, we obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u\leq N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(u)\}}

\displaystyle\geq-G(u,x)\mathbb{I}_{\{u\leq N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(u)\}}

\displaystyle\geq-u^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{u\leq N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(u)\}}

\displaystyle\geq-N^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{u\leq N\}}.

(92)

Then, we have that for any
(u,x)\in E
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{U_{s}\leq N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\geq-N^{p-1}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}_{\{U_{s}\leq N\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\geq-N^{p-1}[\mathbb{E}_{x}(g)+N]

\displaystyle>-\infty,

(93)

where in the second last inequality we used the fact that
U_{s}>N
for all
s\geq g+N
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(N)\}}

\displaystyle\geq-u^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{b(N)\geq x>b(u)\}}

\displaystyle\geq-u^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{x\leq b(N)\}}.

(94)

Thus, we can see that for any
(u,x)\in E
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{U_{s}>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{b(N)\geq X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{g^{(b(N))}}(u+s)^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}\leq b(N)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{E}_{x}((u+g^{(b(N))})^{p})

\displaystyle>-\infty,

(95)

where we used that
U_{s}\leq u+s
and that
g^{(b(N))}=\sup\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq b(N)\}
has moments of order
p
(see Lemma
2.1
). Moreover, since
V
is non-decreasing in each argument we have that

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(N)\}}\geq\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(N,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(N)\}}.

(96)

Hence, since
b
is non-decreasing and by Fubini’s theorem deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{U_{s}>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(N)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(N,X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(N)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{(b(N),\infty)}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(N,z+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{s}\in\textup{d}z)\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\geq\Phi^{\prime}(0)\int_{b(N)}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(N,z+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z,

where in the last inequality we used a density of the
0
-potential measure of
X
without killing (see (
12
)), that
V\leq 0
and that
W
is a non-negative function that vanishes on
(-\infty,0)
. From Fubini’s theorem and since
V
is a non-decreasing function in each argument that vanishes on
D
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{U_{s}>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(N)\}}\textup{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\geq\Phi^{\prime}(0)\int_{b(N)}^{b(N)+1}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(N,z+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad+\Phi^{\prime}(0)\int_{b(N)+1}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(N,z+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\geq\Phi^{\prime}(0)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(N,b(N)+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Phi^{\prime}(0)\int_{(-\infty,-1)}\int_{b(N)+1}^{b(N)-y}\widetilde{V}(N,z+y)\textup{d}z\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\geq\Phi^{\prime}(0)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(N,b(N)+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)-\Phi^{\prime}(0)\int_{(-\infty,-1)}(y+1)\widetilde{V}(0,y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle>-\infty,

(97)

where the finiteness of the last integrals follow from Lemmas
2.1
and
4.14
and equation (
36
). Therefore, we have that (
53
) follows from equations (
91
)-(
97
). Moreover, from the dominated convergence theorem we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u+s,X_{s}+x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+x>b(u+s)\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{s<\sigma_{-x}^{-}\}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s}^{(-x)},X_{s}+x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+x>b(U_{s}^{(-x)})\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{s\geq\sigma_{-x}^{-}\}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{V}(u+s,X_{s}+x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+x>b(u+s)\}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{V}(U_{s}^{(-x)},X_{s}+x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}+x>b(U_{s}^{(-x)})\}}\right).

Note that
b
is a decreasing function and then
\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}V(u,x)=0
and
\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}V(u,x)=0
for any
u>0
. Moreover, for any
s\geq 0
,
x\mapsto U_{s}^{(-x)}
is increasing and bounded so then
\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}U_{s}^{(-x)}
exists. Then we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{u,x\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(U_{s},X_{s}+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{s}>b(U_{s})\}}\right)=0

as claimed.
∎

The next two Lemmas are auxiliary results for the proof of Lemma
5.5
. For ease of notation, we define for any
(u,x)\in E
,

\displaystyle K_{1}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=G(u,x)\mathbb{I}_{\{x\leq b(u)\}},

\displaystyle K_{2}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(u)\}}.

The following result is an application of formula (
21
) to the functions
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
.

Lemma A.3
.

Assume that
\sigma>0
. Then for any
\delta>0
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}[K_{1}

\displaystyle(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})+K_{2}(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})]\textup{d}s\bigg)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}}K^{-}(\delta,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}-\varepsilon)\right)+K^{+}(\delta,\varepsilon)}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(\varepsilon)},

where for all
\delta>0
and
x\leq 0
,

\displaystyle K^{-}(\delta,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{+}}[K_{1}(\delta,X_{r})+K_{2}(\delta,X_{r})]\textup{d}r\right),

and for all
\delta,x>0
,

\displaystyle K^{+}(\delta,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{-}}[K_{1}(\delta+s,X_{r})+K_{2}(\delta+s,X_{r})]\textup{d}r\right).

For
N>0
fixed and
(u,x)\in E
, we define the functions

\displaystyle K_{3}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u\leq N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(u)\}},

\displaystyle K_{4}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{x>b(N)\}},

\displaystyle K_{5}(u,x)

\displaystyle:=-\int_{(-\infty,0)}\widetilde{V}(u,x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\mathbb{I}_{\{u>N\}}\mathbb{I}_{\{b(N)\geq x>b(u)\}}.

Then, it follows that for any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}[K_{1}

\displaystyle(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})+K_{2}(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})]\textup{d}s\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{1}(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right)+\sum_{i=3}^{5}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{i}(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})\textup{d}s\right).

Since
G
,
V
and
b
are continuous functions and
b
is non-increasing, it is easy to see that
K_{i}
is left-continuous in each argument, for each
i\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
. Moreover, since
|G(u,x)|\leq u^{p-1}+\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
u\geq 0
, and
b
is non-increasing, we have that
|K_{1}(u+\delta,x)|\leq C_{1}(u,x)
, where
C_{1}(u,x):=(u+\delta)^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{x<b(\delta)\}}+\mathbb{E}_{x}(g^{p-1})
. Note that for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
u\mapsto C_{1}(u,x)
is monotone and, for each
(u,x)\in E
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u,x}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}C_{1}(U_{r},X_{r}+y)\textup{d}r\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x+y-b(\delta)}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(u+r+\delta)^{p-1}\mathbb{I}_{\{X_{r}<0\}}\textup{d}r\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x+y}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{r}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}r\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x+y-b(\delta)}\left(\int_{0}^{g}(u+r+\delta)^{p-1}\textup{d}r\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x+y}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{r}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}r\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x+y-b(\delta)}\left((u+g+\delta)^{p}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{x+y}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X_{r}}(g^{p-1})\textup{d}r\right)

\displaystyle<\infty,

where in the first inequality we used that
U_{r}\leq u+r
for all
r\geq 0
(under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{u,x}
) and the last inequality follows from Lemmas
2.1
and
4.1
. Similarly, from equations (
92
)-(
97
) we see that for each
i\in\{3,4,5\}
, there exists a non-negative function
C_{i}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that
u\mapsto C_{i}(u,x)
is a monotone function for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
|K_{i}(u+\delta,x)|\leq C_{i}(u,x)
and
\mathbb{E}_{u,x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}C_{i}(U_{r},X_{r}+y)\textup{d}r\right)<\infty
for all
(u,x)\in E
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Hence, using formula (
21
), applied to the functions
K_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,3,4,5\}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}[K_{1}

\displaystyle(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})+K_{2}(U_{s}+\delta,X_{s})]\textup{d}s\bigg)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{0}^{-}<\infty\}}K^{-}(\delta,X_{\tau_{0}^{-}}-\varepsilon)\right)+K^{+}(\delta,\varepsilon)}{\psi^{\prime}(0+)W(\varepsilon)},

as claimed.
∎The following result is also used in the proof of Lemma
5.5
.

Lemma A.4
.

Suppose that
\sigma>0
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})-\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})}{\varepsilon}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{-}(0,0).

Recall from Lemma
A.2
that
V(0,x)
is continuously differentiable on
(-\infty,0)
with

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x-u}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u

for
x<0
. Moreover, from the continuity of
x\mapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x-u}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u
, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{-}(0,0)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{u}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u=\lim_{x\uparrow 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x).

(98)

Hence, from (
35
) we have that for any
\varepsilon>0
and
x\leq-\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle V(0,x+\varepsilon)-V(0,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{-x-\varepsilon}^{-x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{-u-z+x}(g^{p-1})W^{\prime}(u)\textup{d}u\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{0}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x-z)\textup{d}z.

Thus, since
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x)
is non-increasing (see Lemma
A.2
) we see that

\displaystyle\varepsilon\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x+\varepsilon)\leq V(0,x+\varepsilon)-V(0,x)\leq\varepsilon\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,x)

Hence, for any
\varepsilon>0
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})-\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}}\right).

(99)

Note that, under the event
\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}
, we have
|X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}|\leq|\underline{X}_{\infty}|
. Then, from (
86
) we deduce that

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}}\leq\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}}\leq\alpha_{p-1}+\gamma_{p-1}|\underline{X}_{\infty}|^{p-1}.

Moreover, since
\mathbb{E}(|\underline{X}_{\infty}|^{p-1})<\infty
(see Lemma
2.1
) we see that the random variables above are bounded by an integrable random variable. Furthermore, it is easy to show that
\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}\downarrow\tau_{0}^{-}=0
a.s., when
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
(where the equality follows since
X
is of infinite variation), and, since
X
is right-continuous, we have that
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}=X_{0}=0
almost surely. Therefore, by letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
in (
99
), the dominated convergence theorem and (
98
), we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}}+\varepsilon)\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})-\mathbb{E}(V(0,X_{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}})\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_{-\varepsilon}^{-}<\infty\}})}{\varepsilon}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}V_{-}(0,0)

as claimed.

∎

B
Variational inequalities for spectrally negative Lévy processes

In
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
(see Section 2), variational inequalities in the sense of distributions are studied for general Lévy processes, and such results are applied to characterise the price of an American option. It turns out that, although there are many similarities to our setting, their assumptions do not entirely fit our optimal stopping problem (cf. Proposition 2.5 in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
), and their results are not directly applicable. This section is dedicated to relaxing the assumptions on Proposition 2.5 in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
imposed to the value function. This extension is natural and most of the proofs remain the same, but for completeness, some of them are included in this section.

Let
X
be a spectrally negative Lévy process with the following representation:

\displaystyle X_{t}=-\mu t+\sigma B_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(-\infty,-1)}xN(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(-1,0)}x(N(\textup{d}s,\textup{d}x)-\textup{d}s\Pi(\textup{d}x)),

where
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma\geq 0
,
\{B_{t},t\geq 0\}
is a standard Brownian motion and
N
is a Poisson random measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\{0\}
with intensity
\textup{d}t\times\Pi(\textup{d}y)
, where
\Pi
is a Lévy measure, i.e.,
\Pi
satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1\wedge|x|^{2})\Pi(\textup{d}x)<\infty
.

Fix
f\in C^{1,2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
, the set of all bounded
C^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
functions with bounded derivatives. By applying Itô formula we obtain the following decomposition

\displaystyle f(t,X_{t})=f(0,X_{0})+M_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(s,X_{s})\textup{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0,

where
M
is a martingale starting at zero and
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)
is the infinitesimal generator of
(t,X)
, applied to
f
, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(t,x)-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f(t,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}f(t,x)+B_{X}(f)(t,x),

with

\displaystyle B_{X}(f)(t,x)=\int_{(-\infty,0)}\left(f(t,x+y)-f(t,x)-y\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f(t,x)\right)\Pi(\textup{d}y).

Note that for the derivatives in the operator
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}
to be defined it is only needed that
f\in C^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
). In the next Lemma, we show that
B_{X}
can be defined in a subset
B\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
provided some conditions are met in the set
B
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
an open set. Assume that
f
is a
C^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
function and that

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|f(t,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)<\infty,

for all
(x,y)\in B
. We have that
|B_{X}(f)(t,x)|<\infty
for all
(t,x)\in B
. Moreover if
f
, its derivatives and
(t,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|f(t,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
are bounded functions in
B
, we have that, for any
T>0
,
B_{X}(f)
is bounded in
B\cap([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})
and continuous in
B
.

Take
(t,x)\in B
. By Taylor’s theorem we know that for each
y\in(-1,0)
, there exists
c_{y}\in[x+y,x]\subset[x-1,x]
such that

\displaystyle f(t,x+y)-f(t,x)-y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f(t,x)=y^{2}\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}f(t,c_{y}).

Hence, we have that for any
(t,x)\in B
,

\displaystyle|B_{X}(f)(t,x)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{(-1,0)}\left(f(t,x+y)-f(t,x)-y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f(t,x)\right)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+\left.\int_{(-\infty,-1]}\left(f(t,x+y)-f(t,x)\right)\Pi(\textup{d}y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(-1,0)}y^{2}\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}f(t,c_{y})\right|\Pi(\textup{d}y)+\int_{(-\infty,-1]}\left|f(t,x+y)-f(t,x)\right|\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{z\in[x-1,x]}\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}f(t,z)\right|\int_{(-1,0)}y^{2}\Pi(\textup{d}y)+\int_{(-\infty,-1]}\left|f(t,x+y)\right|\Pi(\textup{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad+|f(t,x)|\Pi(-\infty,-1]

\displaystyle<\infty,

where we used that
\Pi
is finite on any set away from zero and that the derivatives of
f
are continuous on
B
(then bounded on compact sets). The second assertion follows by the fact that the second derivative is continuous and bounded on the compact set containing the set
\tilde{B}=\{(t,x-y)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}:(t,x)\in B,\text{ }y\in(0,1)\text{ and }(t,x-y)\notin B\}
, and since
f
and
(t,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|f(t,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
are bounded in
B
. The continuity of
B_{X}(f)
in
B
follows from the fact that
f
is
C^{1,2}
and the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Consider the stopping time
\tau_{B}
as the first time the process
(t,X)
exits the open set
B
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}=\inf\{t\geq 0:(s+t,X_{t}+x)\notin B\}.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
an open set. Assume that
f
is a
C^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
function such that
f
, its derivatives and
(t,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|f(t,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
are bounded in
B
. Then, for any
t\geq 0
, we have the following decomposition

\displaystyle f(s+t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)},X_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}+x)=f(s,x)+M_{t}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(u+r,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}r,

(100)

where
\{M_{t},t\geq 0\}
is a zero mean
\mathbb{P}
-martingale.

Let
(s,x)\in B
and
t\geq 0
. Since
f
is a
C^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
function we have by Itô formula,

\displaystyle f(s+t,X_{t}+x)-f(s,x)

\displaystyle=M_{t}^{(1)}+M_{t}^{(2)}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(s+r,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}r,

where

\displaystyle M_{t}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(s+r,X_{r-}+x)\textup{d}B_{r}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(-1,0)}y\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(s+r,X_{r-}+x)\widetilde{N}(\textup{d}r,\textup{d}x),

\displaystyle M_{t}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(-\infty,0)}\bigg[f(s+r,X_{r-}+x+y)-f(s+r,X_{r-}+x)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-y\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(s+r,X_{r-}+x)\bigg]\widetilde{N}(\textup{d}r,\textup{d}y).

Note that for any
r<\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}
we have
(s+r,X_{r}+x)\in B
. Hence, since
\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}
is bounded in the set
B
, we have that the stopped process
\{M^{(1)}_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}},t\geq 0\}
is a martingale. Moreover, from Lemma
B.1
we have that
B_{X}(f)
is a bounded function on
B\cap([0,t]\times\mathbb{R})
. Hence, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}B_{X}(f)(s+r,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}r\right)<\infty

for all
t\geq 0
. Thus, the process
\{M^{(2)}_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}},t\geq 0\}
is also a martingale.
∎

Let
G
be a continuous function. Define the process
Z^{(s,x)}=\{Z_{t}^{(s,x)},t\geq 0\}
, where

\displaystyle Z_{t}^{(s,x)}=f(s+t,X_{t}+x)+\int_{0}^{t}G(r+s,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}r,\qquad t\geq 0.

We have the following proposition.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
an open set. Assume that
f
is a
C^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
function such that
f
, its derivatives and
(t,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|f(t,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
are bounded in
B
, and
G:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function bounded in
B
. Then, for all
(s,x)\in B
, the process
\{Z_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{(s,x)},t\geq 0\}
is a submartingale if and only if
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)+G\geq 0
in
B
.

Fix
(s,x)\in B
. Suppose that
\{Z_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{(s,x)},t\geq 0\}
is a submartingale. We prove that
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(s,x)+G(s,x)\geq 0
. From the submartingale property, we have that for every
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{t}(Z_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{(s,x)}-Z_{0}^{(s,x)})\right]\geq 0

which implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{t}[f(s+t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)},X_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}+x)-f(s,x)]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}G(s+r,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}r\right]\geq 0.

By the decomposition (
100
) we then get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(s+r,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}s\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}G(s+r,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}r\right]\geq 0.

Due to the right continuity of
(t,X)
and since
B
is open, we have
\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}>0
almost surely. Therefore, tending
t
to zero in the above inequality, by Fubini’s theorem and the fundamental theorem of calculus (since
r\mapsto X_{r}
is right continuous) we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(s,x)+G(s,x)\geq 0.

Next, suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(s,x)+G(s,x)\geq 0
for all
(s,x)\in B
. We show that, for any
(s,x)\in B
, the process
\{Z_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{(s,x)},t\geq 0\}
is a submartingale. For any
(s,x)\in B
and
0\leq r\leq t
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Z_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{(s,x)}|\mathcal{F}_{r})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[f(s,x)+M_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(v+s,X_{v}+x)\textup{d}v\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{r}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}G(v+s,X_{v}+x)\textup{d}v\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=Z_{r\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{(s,x)}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{r\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(v+s,X_{v}+x)\textup{d}v\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{r}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{r\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}G(v+s,X_{v}+x)\textup{d}v\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{r}\right]

\displaystyle\geq Z_{s\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{(s,x)},

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
(v+s,X_{v}+x)\in B
for all
v\in(r\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)},t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)})
and that
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)(s,x)+G(s,x)\geq 0
in
B
. Therefore the process
Z_{t\wedge\tau_{B}}^{(s,x)}
is a submartingale.
∎

It turns out that the above proposition can be extended to a more general class of functions, provided that the inequality
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)+G\geq 0
is taken in the sense of distributions. For this, we recall some facts and notation from the theory of distributions (see e.g.
Friedlander
et al. 1998
or
Rudin 1991
for further details). We introduce the multi-index notation. A multi-index is a
n
-tuplet
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
of non-negative integers with order
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
. We set the notation

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}f=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}}.

If
\mathcal{O}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
\mathcal{D(O)}
the set of test functions in
\mathcal{O}
, i.e., the set of all
C^{\infty}
functions with compact support in
\mathcal{O}
, and by
\mathcal{D^{\prime}(O)}
the space of distributions on
\mathcal{O}
. That is,
\mathcal{D^{\prime}(O)}
is the space of linear forms
u
in
\mathcal{D(O)}
such that, for every compact set
K\subset O
, there is a real number
C\geq 0
and a non-negative integer
N
that satisfy

\displaystyle|\langle u,\psi\rangle|\leq C\sum_{|\alpha|\leq N}\sup|\partial^{\alpha}\psi|

for all
\psi\in\mathcal{D(O)}
, where
\langle u,\varphi\rangle
denotes the evaluation of the distribution
u
on the test function
\varphi
. Inspired by the integration by parts formula, the derivative of the distribution
u
is defined by

\displaystyle\langle\partial^{\alpha}u,\varphi\rangle=(-1)^{|\alpha|}\langle u,\partial^{\alpha}\varphi\rangle,\qquad\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}).

If
u
is a locally integrable function on
\mathcal{O}
(
u
is a measurable function and
\int_{K}|u(x)|\textup{d}x<\infty
for any compact set
K\subset\mathcal{O}
), we can define the distribution

\displaystyle\langle u,\varphi\rangle=\int u(x)\varphi(x)\textup{d}x,\qquad\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}),

which is usually identified only with the function
u
. Hence, if
g
is a locally integrable function on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, the differential operator,
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}^{0}(g)
, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}^{0}(g)(u,x):=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}g(t,x)-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}g(t,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}g(t,x)

can be defined in the sense of distributions. Indeed, for any test function
\varphi
with compact support in
O\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, we define

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}^{0}(g),\varphi\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t,x)\left[-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\varphi(t,x)+\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(t,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\varphi(t,x)\right]\textup{d}x\textup{d}t.

(101)

Moreover,
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
showed (see Proposition 2.1) that the operator defined by

\displaystyle B_{X}(g)(t,x):=\int_{(-\infty,0)}(g(t,x+y)-g(t,x)-y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}g(t,x)\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}})\Pi(\textup{d}y)

can be also defined in the sense of distributions, when
g
is a bounded Borel measurable function. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})
, consider the operator
B^{*}_{X}
given by

\displaystyle B_{X}^{*}(\varphi)(t,x)=\int_{(-\infty,0)}[\varphi(t,x-y)-\varphi(t,x)+y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(t,x)\mathbb{I}_{\{y>-1\}}]\Pi(\textup{d}y),

(102)

for any
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. From Proposition 2.1 in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
, we know that
B^{*}_{X}(\varphi)
is continuous and integrable on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
and that the operator

\displaystyle\langle B_{X}(g),\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(u,x)B^{*}_{X}(\varphi)(u,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

(103)

defines a distribution. The following lemma shows that the boundedness condition imposed on
g
can be relaxed.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
g
be a locally integrable function in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|g(u,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)

(104)

is locally integrable. The linear operator
B_{X}(g)
defined in (
103
) defines a distribution on any open set
O\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, if in addition we suppose that
g
is a
C^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
function we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}B_{X}(g)(t,x)\varphi(t,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t,x)B_{X}^{*}(\varphi)(t,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}t

for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}})
.

It is clear that the operator defined in (
103
) is linear. Take a test function
\varphi
with support in a compact set
H\times K\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. We have

\displaystyle|\langle B_{X}(g),\varphi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(u,x)||B^{*}_{X}(\varphi)(u,x)|\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\leq\int_{H}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(u,x)|\int_{(-1,0)}|\varphi(u,x-y)-\varphi(u,x)+y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{H}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(u,x)|\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|\varphi(u,x-y)-\varphi(u,x)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u.

Note that if
x\notin K+(-1,0]
we have that
x\notin K
(if we assume that
x\in K
then
x=x+0\in K+(-1,0]
, which is a contradiction) and
x-y\notin K
for all
y\in(-1,0)
(if
z=x-y\in K
, then
x=z+y\in K+(-1,0)\subset K+(-1,0]
and we have got a contradiction), which implies
\varphi(u,x-y)-\varphi(u,x)+y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)=0
. Denote
k_{*}=\inf K
, since
x\mapsto\varphi(u,x)
has support in
K
and using Taylor’s formula we obtain

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\langle B_{X}(g),\varphi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{H}\int_{K+(-1,0]}|g(u,x)|\int_{(-1,0)}|\varphi(u,x-y)-\varphi(u,x)+y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\varphi(u,x)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{H}\int_{K}|g(u,x)|\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|\varphi(u,x-y)-\varphi(u,x)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{H}\int_{-\infty}^{k_{*}}|g(u,x)|\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|\varphi(u,x-y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup|\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\varphi|\int_{(-1,0)}y^{2}\Pi(\textup{d}y)\int_{H}\int_{K+(-1,0]}|g(u,x)|\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+2\sup|\varphi|\Pi((-\infty,-1))\int_{H}\int_{K}|g(u,x)|\textup{d}x\textup{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup|\varphi|\int_{H}\int_{K}\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|g(u,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)\textup{d}x\textup{d}u,

which proves the assertion, since
\Pi
is a Lévy measure and
(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|g(u,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
is locally integrable by assumption. The last assertion follows the same argument in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
(see Proposition 2.1), so the proof is omitted.
∎Therefore, if
g
is a locally integrable function in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
such that the function defined in (
104
) is locally integrable, we can define the distribution
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(g)=\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}^{0}(g)+B_{X}(g)
in any open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Let
u
be a distribution and
\theta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
. Then the function

\displaystyle(\theta*u)(t,x)=\langle u(s,y),\theta(t-s,x-y)\rangle

is a member of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
and defines a distribution given by

\displaystyle\langle\theta*u,\phi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle u(s,y),\theta(t-s,x-y)\rangle\phi(t,x)\textup{d}x\textup{d}t,

for any
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
. It turns out that Proposition 2.3 in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
can also be extended to this case. The proof remains the same so it is omitted.

Proposition B.5
.

Let
g
be a Borel and locally integrable function in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
such that the function
\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|g(u,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
is locally integrable. We have that for every
\theta
and
\varphi
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(g*\theta),\varphi\rangle=\langle\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(g),\varphi*\check{\theta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(g)*\theta,\varphi\rangle,

where
\check{\theta}(u,x)=\theta(-u,-x)
for any
(u,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Let
u
be a distribution in
\mathcal{O}
, we say that
u
is non-negative if for any non-negative test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O})
,

\displaystyle\langle u,\varphi\rangle\geq 0.

The next result is an extension of Proposition 2.5 in
Lamberton and
Mikou 2008
. The proof is essentially the same but we include it for completeness.

Proposition B.6
.

Let
B
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,
G:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and
(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|f(u,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
are locally integrable functions in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and bounded in
B
. Assume that the process
\{Z_{t\wedge\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}}^{(s,x)},t\geq 0\}
is a submartingale for every
(s,x)\in B
, where
Z_{t}^{(s,x)}=f(s+t,X_{t}+x)+\int_{0}^{t}G(s+r,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}r
and
\tau_{B}^{(s,x)}=\inf\{t\geq 0:(t+s,X_{t}+x)\notin B\}
. Then,
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)+G
is a non-negative distribution on
B
.

Take
z_{0}=(u_{0},x_{0})\in B
and choose
a>0
such that
\textbf{B}(z_{0},2a)\subset B
, where
\textbf{B}(z_{0},2a)
is the open ball with center
z_{0}
and radius
2a
. We define the stopping time

\displaystyle\tau_{B}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\text{ there exists }z\in\textbf{B}(z_{0},a)\text{ such that }z+(t,X_{t})\notin B\}.

Note that for every
(u,x)\in\textbf{B}(z_{0},a/2)
and
(v,y)\in\textbf{B}(0,a/2)
we have that
(u-v,x-y)\in\textbf{B}(z_{0},a)\subset B
and then
\tau_{B}\leq\tau_{B}^{(u-v,x-y)}
. Hence, the process
\{Z_{t\wedge\tau_{B}}^{(u-v,x-y)},t\geq 0\}
is a submartingale, and then, for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle f(u-v

\displaystyle,x-y)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(f(u-v+t\wedge\tau_{B},X_{t\wedge\tau_{B}}+x-y)+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}}G(u-v+r,X_{r}+x-y)\textup{d}r\right).

Next, we consider a sequence of even non-negative functions
\{\rho_{n},n\geq 1\}
in
C^{\infty}
such that, for each
n\geq 1
, the support of
\rho_{n}
is in
\textbf{B}(0,a/(2n))
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{n}(v,y)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y=1
. Then, by integrating the equation above with respect to
\rho_{n}(v,y)
and Fubini’s theorem, we get that

\displaystyle(f*\rho_{n})

\displaystyle(u,x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left((f*\rho_{n})(u+t\wedge\tau_{B},X_{t\wedge\tau_{B}}+x)\right)+\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}}(G*\rho_{n})(u+r,X_{r}+x)\textup{d}r\right).

(105)

Fix
(u,x)\in\textbf{B}(z_{0},a/2)
. Note that, since
f
is bounded, we have that for all
n\geq 1
, the function
(s,w)\mapsto f*\rho_{n}(u+s,w+x)
is
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
and has bounded derivatives in the open set
\widetilde{B}=\{(s,w)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}:z+(s,w)\in B\text{ for all }z\in\textbf{B}(z_{0},a)\}
.
Moreover, since
(u,x)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}|f(u,x+y)|\Pi(\textup{d}y)
is bounded in
B
, the function
(s,w)\mapsto\int_{(-\infty,-1)}(f*\rho_{n})(u+s,w+x+y)\Pi(\textup{d}y)
is bounded in
\widetilde{B}
. Thus, since
\tau_{B}
is the first exit time of
(s,X_{s})
from the set
\widetilde{B}
, we get from Lemma
B.2
that

\displaystyle(f*\rho_{n})

\displaystyle(u+t\wedge\tau_{B},X_{t\wedge\tau_{B}}+x)

\displaystyle\qquad=(f*\rho_{n})(u,x)+M_{t}^{(u,x)}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}}\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f*\rho_{n})(u+s,X_{s}+x)\textup{d}s,

where
\{M_{t}^{(u,x)},t\geq 0\}
is a martingale. Therefore equation (
105
) reads

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{B}}\left[\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f*\rho_{n})(u+r,X_{r}+x)+(G*\rho_{n})(u+r,X_{r}+x)\right]\textup{d}r\right)\geq 0.

Note that
\tau_{B}>0
a.s. (since
\textbf{B}(0,a)\subset\widetilde{B}
), dividing by
t>0
the equation above and taking
t\downarrow 0
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f*\rho_{n})(u,x)+(G*\rho_{n})(u,x)\geq 0

(106)

for all
n\geq 1
and
(u,x)\in\textbf{B}(z_{0},a/2)
. That implies that for any non-negative test function
\psi
in
\textbf{B}(z_{0},a/2)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}_{(t,x)}(f*\rho_{n})+G*\rho_{n},\psi\rangle\geq 0.

Then, from Proposition
B.5
we conclude that
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)*\rho_{n}+G*\rho_{n}\geq 0
in the sense of distributions on
\textbf{B}(z_{0},a/2)
. By letting
n
go to infinity, we conclude that
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)+G\geq 0
on
\textbf{B}(z_{0},a/2)
in the sense of distributions. Since
z_{0}
is any arbitrary point in
B
, using a partition of unity argument, we conclude that
\mathcal{A}_{(t,X)}(f)+G\geq 0
in the sense of distributions on
B
.

∎
